Essay · 15Explain the determination of break-point chlorination using graph? What are its advantages? BL-2+

Breakpoint chlorination is the addition of enough chlorine to satisfy the water’s chlorine demand and pass through the destruction of combined chlorine, after which increasing free residual chlorine can be obtained. Read the graph with chlorine dose on the horizontal axis and chlorine residual on the vertical axis.

  • Up to A: reducing substances consume chlorine; little or no residual is measured.
  • A–B: reaction with ammonia/nitrogen-containing substances forms chloramines; combined residual rises.
  • B–C: further chlorine destroys chloramines and other combined-chlorine compounds, so residual falls.
  • At C: the breakpoint is reached. Much of the chlorine demand/combined chlorine has been dealt with.
  • Beyond C: additional chlorine gives an increasing free-chlorine residual, providing disinfection after an appropriate contact period.
The breakpoint does not mean all chlorine addition must cease in every treatment process; the purpose beyond it is an appropriate disinfectant residual.

Unit 1.pdf

Short answer · 1Name the salts that cause temporary hardness. How are they removed? [BL-2]+

Temporary hardness is mainly caused by calcium bicarbonate, Ca(HCO₃)₂, and magnesium bicarbonate, Mg(HCO₃)₂. Boiling removes it by decomposing the bicarbonates and separating insoluble CaCO₃ or Mg(OH)₂.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 2Mention the different units of hardness? Give the relation between the units. [BL-2]+

The principal units are mg/L as CaCO₃, ppm as CaCO₃, degrees French (°Fr) and degrees Clarke (°Cl). For dilute water, 1 mg/L ≈ 1 ppm.

\(1\text{ ppm}=0.1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}\approx0.07\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\)
\(1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}=10\text{ ppm};\quad1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\approx14.3\text{ ppm}\)
The source gives rounded conversions. Numerical answers here consistently use 0.07 °Cl per ppm rather than mixing that factor with the separately rounded reciprocal 14.3.
Short answer · 3List out the disadvantages of usage of hard water in industries. [BL-2]+
  • Boilers: scale/sludge formation, poor heat transfer, fuel wastage, overheating and corrosion-related troubles.
  • Textile/dyeing and laundry: soap wastage, scum, stains and uneven colour.
  • Paper: poor finish and staining from iron salts.
  • Sugar: interference with refining/crystallisation.
  • Concrete and pharmaceutical manufacture: unwanted salts can interfere with product quality.
Short answer · 4Hardness is expressed in terms of CaCO3 equivalents? Give reason. [BL-3]+

Different hardness-producing substances have different molecular masses. Expressing each contribution as an equivalent amount of CaCO₃ puts them on one common chemical-equivalence basis so that contributions can be added and compared. CaCO₃ has a convenient molecular mass of 100 and equivalent mass of 50.

\(\text{CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}=\text{concentration}\times\dfrac{50}{\text{equivalent mass of substance}}\)
Short answer · 5Mention the dissolved salts that cause temporary and permanent hardness in water. [BL-2]+
  • Temporary: Ca(HCO₃)₂ and Mg(HCO₃)₂.
  • Permanent: CaCl₂, MgCl₂, CaSO₄, MgSO₄, Ca(NO₃)₂ and Mg(NO₃)₂. Permanent hardness is not removed by simple boiling.
Short answer · 6Give the principle involved in the estimation of hardness by EDTA method. [BL-2]+

At about pH 10, calcium and magnesium ions form stable complexes with EDTA. Eriochrome Black T (EBT) first gives a wine-red metal–indicator complex. EDTA binds the metal ions more strongly, releasing the blue indicator at the endpoint. The standard EDTA volume measures the hardness.

Short answer · 7Why is ammonical (NH4Cl+NH4OH) buffer added during the determination of hardness in water samples? [BL-3]+

NH₄Cl/NH₄OH buffer maintains pH close to 10, the conditions needed for the EDTA complexation and a clear EBT endpoint. If the pH drifts, metal–EDTA/indicator equilibria change and the endpoint becomes unreliable.

Short answer · 8A water sample containing Ca(HCO₃)₂ and MgSO₄ has temporary hardness of 200 ppm and permanent hardness of 300 ppm. Calculate the total hardness of water in ppm, °French, and °Clarke.+

Total hardness = temporary + permanent = 200 + 300 = 500 ppm.

Degrees French = 500 × 0.1 = 50 °Fr. Degrees Clarke ≈ 500 × 0.07 = 35 °Cl.

Final answer: Total hardness = 500 ppm = 50 °Fr ≈ 35 °Cl.
Short answer · 9A water sample contains 20 mg/L of Ca²⁺ and 24 mg/L of Mg²⁺ ions. Calculate hardness of water in ppm as CaCO₃ equivalent. (Atomic mass: Ca = 40, Mg = 24, Molar mass of CaCO₃ = 100).+

Both ions are divalent, as is the calcium represented by the CaCO₃ reference. Convert each ionic concentration using its atomic mass.

\(\mathrm{Ca^{2+}}:20\times\dfrac{100}{40}=50\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}:24\times\dfrac{100}{24}=100\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(H_{total}=50+100=150\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\approx150\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Hardness = 150 ppm as CaCO₃.
Short answer · 10What is internal water treatment in boilers and why is it necessary? [BL-2]+

Internal treatment means adding suitable chemicals directly to boiler water. They either convert scale-forming impurities into loose sludge removable by blow-down or keep hardness ions in soluble complexes. It reduces adherent deposits, heat-transfer loss and overheating. Examples are Calgon, phosphate and colloidal conditioning.

Short answer · 11Specify the requirements for potable water? [BL-2]+
  • Clear, colourless, odourless and acceptable in taste.
  • Low turbidity; free from objectionable suspended/colloidal impurities and disease-causing organisms.
  • Free from harmful amounts of toxic minerals and objectionable dissolved gases.
  • The supplied handout lists pH 6.5–8.5, turbidity not exceeding 10 ppm and colour 5 ppm.
These numerical entries are retained as course-handout values. Its embedded table is labelled Indian Standard IS 10500; they have not been certified or relabelled as current WHO specifications.
Short answer · 12What is disinfection? What are the different methods of disinfection? [BL-2]+

Disinfection is the destruction of disease-producing microorganisms in water. Methods include boiling and chemical disinfection by chlorine, bleaching powder and chloramine. Chlorination produces hypochlorous acid (HOCl), responsible for much of the germicidal action.

Short answer · 13Why is chloramine preferred to bleaching powder during disinfection? [BL-3]+

Chloramine provides a longer-lasting disinfectant residual because it is more persistent than free chlorine. In this comparison it also avoids the objectionable taste/odour associated with excess bleaching powder and does not introduce the calcium component of bleaching powder. Its advantage here is persistent residual protection.

Short answer · 14How does break point chlorination improve water quality in municipal water supplies? [BL-3]+

Chlorine is first consumed by reducing substances and ammonia/organic matter. Sufficient further addition destroys much of the combined chlorine. Beyond the breakpoint, a controllable free-chlorine residual can remain for disinfection rather than all added chlorine being consumed by the water’s demand.

Short answer · 15What happens if natural water is fed into boilers? [BL-3]+

Evaporation concentrates dissolved salts. Untreated feed water can cause scale and sludge, priming/foaming (carryover), boiler corrosion and caustic embrittlement. Deposits lower heat transfer, waste fuel, choke passages and may cause overheating or failure.

Short answer · 16Differentiate between scales and sludges. [BL-2]+
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Short answer · 17Name the hardest scale formed? How is it removed? [BL-2]+

Calcium sulphate (CaSO₄) forms a hard, tenacious boiler scale. It is adherent and difficult to remove. EDTA treatment forms soluble calcium complexes. Cleaning conditions must suit the boiler and deposit.

Final answer: CaSO₄ scale; remove by suitable EDTA chemical cleaning.

Wording qualification: “hardest” is not a universal ranking established by these pages. The same source also describes very firmly adherent calcium/magnesium silicate deposits; their removal requires deposit-specific assessment. Dilute HCl is the source treatment for carbonate scale, not a universal sulphate/silicate treatment. This answer gives the textbook-supported hard-scale/removal pair without claiming a lecturer-approved superlative.
Short answer · 18State two reasons for the formation of scales in boilers. [BL-2]+
  • Heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate, depositing CaCO₃.
  • At high boiler temperatures the solubility of CaSO₄ decreases, causing a hard CaSO₄ deposit.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 19Mention the type of scales formed in low pressure boilers with necessary equations. [BL-2]+

Calcium carbonate is the principal low-pressure scale formed in boilers. Heating bicarbonate-containing water produces CaCO₃ precipitation. Magnesium bicarbonate can also give Mg(OH)₂ deposits.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 20In demineralization process, water is usually passed through cation exchanger and then through anion exchanger. Give reason. [BL-3]+

The cation bed removes Ca²⁺/Mg²⁺ and releases H⁺, producing acidic water. The following anion bed replaces anions with OH⁻, which combines with H⁺ to form water. Removing hardness cations first also avoids exposing them to the alkaline conditions an anion-first sequence could create, with precipitates fouling the bed.

\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
Short answer · 21Illustrate the reactions involved in calgon conditioning of hard water. [BL-2]+

Calgon is sodium hexametaphosphate, (NaPO₃)₆, also written Na₂[Na₄(PO₃)₆]. It complexes calcium into a soluble species, preventing calcium-salt scale. Reaction:

\(\mathrm{Na_2[Na_4(PO_3)_6]\rightleftharpoons2Na^+ +[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}}\)
\(\mathrm{2CaSO_4+[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}\rightarrow[Ca_2P_6O_{18}]^{2-}+2Na_2SO_4}\)
Short answer · 22Why is calgon conditioning much preferred over phosphate conditioning? [BL-3]+

Calgon keeps calcium in a soluble complex. Phosphate conditioning instead precipitates calcium/magnesium phosphates as soft sludge, which must be removed by blow-down. Thus Calgon’s advantage in this comparison is suppression of precipitated deposits/sludge rather than merely changing a hard deposit into a loose one. Treatment choice still depends on boiler conditions.

Short answer · 23State and explain the principle involved in reverse osmosis process? [BL-1]+

A pressure greater than the osmotic pressure is applied to the concentrated/brackish-water side of a semipermeable membrane. Water then moves from the concentrated side to the dilute/pure-water side, opposite to spontaneous osmosis, while most dissolved salts are retained.

Short answer · 24List any two advantages of nanofiltration. [BL-2]+
  • It removes many divalent ions and is useful for water softening.
  • It rejects many small organic pollutants while generally requiring lower pressure than reverse osmosis.
Essay · 1Explain in detail how hardness of a water sample is estimated by EDTA method. [BL-2]+

Principle: disodium EDTA complexes Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺. At pH about 10, EBT forms a wine-red metal–indicator complex; EDTA displaces the indicator, giving a blue endpoint.

Prepare standard hard water containing 1 g CaCO₃ equivalent per litre (1 mg/mL): dissolve pure CaCO₃ in minimum dilute HCl, evaporate to dryness, redissolve and make to 1 L.

Standardise EDTA: pipette 50 mL standard hard water, add 10–15 mL buffer and 4–5 drops EBT. Titrate to clear blue. Let the titre be V₁ mL. The aliquot contains 50 mg CaCO₃ equivalent.

Total hardness: titrate 50 mL of the water sample in the same way; let the titre be V₂ mL.

Permanent hardness: boil 250 mL of sample down to about 50 mL, filter off the precipitate and wash it. Collect filtrate and washings and make back to 250 mL. Titrate a 50 mL aliquot; let the titre be V₃ mL. Restoring the original volume avoids an artificial concentration change.

Temporary hardness is total minus permanent hardness. The equations below follow the 50 mL aliquots and standard-water concentration specified above.

\(\mathrm{[M-EBT]+EDTA\rightarrow[M-EDTA]+EBT}\quad\text{(wine red → blue)}\)
\(1\text{ mL EDTA}=\dfrac{50}{V_1}\text{ mg CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}\)
\(H_{total}=\dfrac{V_2(50/V_1)\times1000}{50}=\dfrac{1000V_2}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=\dfrac{1000V_3}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{temporary}=\dfrac{1000(V_2-V_3)}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
Essay · 2Calculate the permanent hardness and temporary hardness of a water sample containing the following salts, Mg(HCO3)2 = 146 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2 = 40.5mg/L, CaSO4 = 34mg/L, MgSO4 = 60mg/L. [BL-3]+

Use CaCO₃ equivalent = concentration × 100/molecular mass for the divalent salts.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:146\times\dfrac{100}{146}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:40.5\times\dfrac{100}{162}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:34\times\dfrac{100}{136}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:60\times\dfrac{100}{120}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=100+25=125\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=25+50=75\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=125+75=200\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 125 ppm; permanent hardness = 75 ppm; total = 200 ppm, all as CaCO₃.
Essay · 3Calculate temporary and total hardness present in 10,000 liters of a water sample containing the following impurities, Mg(HCO3)2 = 73 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2 = 162 mg/L, CaSO4 = 136mg/L, MgCl2 = 95mg/L. [BL-3]+

First find concentration as CaCO₃ equivalent. The 10,000 L volume does not change the ppm value; it is used only when calculating the total equivalent mass in the batch.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:73\times\dfrac{100}{146}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:162\times\dfrac{100}{162}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:136\times\dfrac{100}{136}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgCl_2}:95\times\dfrac{100}{95}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=50+100=150\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=100+100=200\text{ ppm};\quad H_{total}=150+200=350\text{ ppm}\)
\(m_{temporary}=150\times10000=1500000\text{ mg}=1.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
\(m_{total}=350\times10000=3500000\text{ mg}=3.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 150 ppm and total hardness = 350 ppm; the 10,000 L batch contains 1.5 kg temporary and 3.5 kg total CaCO₃-equivalent hardness.
Essay · 4Explain the stages involved in the process of break-point chlorination with necessary illustration. [BL-2]+

Breakpoint chlorination is the addition of enough chlorine to satisfy the water’s chlorine demand and pass through the destruction of combined chlorine, after which increasing free residual chlorine can be obtained. Read the graph with chlorine dose on the horizontal axis and chlorine residual on the vertical axis.

  • Up to A: reducing substances consume chlorine; little or no residual is measured.
  • A–B: reaction with ammonia/nitrogen-containing substances forms chloramines; combined residual rises.
  • B–C: further chlorine destroys chloramines and other combined-chlorine compounds, so residual falls.
  • At C: the breakpoint is reached. Much of the chlorine demand/combined chlorine has been dealt with.
  • Beyond C: additional chlorine gives an increasing free-chlorine residual, providing disinfection after an appropriate contact period.
The breakpoint does not mean all chlorine addition must cease in every treatment process; the purpose beyond it is an appropriate disinfectant residual.
Essay · 5Define scales and sludges? How are they formed? Give their disadvantages and removal methods. [BL-2]+

As boiler water evaporates, dissolved salts concentrate and precipitate. Sludge is a soft, loose, slimy deposit, often accumulating in cooler/slow-flow parts. Scale is a hard adherent coating on the boiler wall.

  • CaCO₃ scale: heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate.
  • CaSO₄ scale: solubility decreases at high boiler temperatures.
  • Mg(OH)₂ deposits: hydrolysis/decomposition of magnesium salts.
  • Silica/silicate deposits: calcium/magnesium silicates stick firmly to the wall.
  • Consequences: poor heat transfer and fuel wastage, choked pipes/valves, lower efficiency, overheating, weakened tubes and possible failure or explosion.
  • Sludge removal: blow-down and brushing; reduce sludge formation by suitable feed-water softening.
  • Scale removal: scraper/wire brushing for loose deposits, suitable chemical treatment for the deposit and controlled thermal treatment where appropriate. A typical catalyst contains dilute HCl for carbonate scale and EDTA treatment for calcium/magnesium scales.
  • Prevention: external feed-water treatment plus internal conditioning and removal of accumulated sludge.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Essay · 6Discuss the reasons and control methods for the boiler trouble caused by scale formation. [BL-2]+

Scale forms as salts concentrate and become insoluble under boiler conditions. Important causes are bicarbonate decomposition, reduced CaSO₄ solubility at high temperature, magnesium-salt hydrolysis and silica/silicate deposition.

  • External control: remove Ca/Mg salts before the feed water enters the boiler, for example by ion exchange.
  • Calgon conditioning: keep calcium in soluble complexes.
  • Phosphate conditioning: convert hardness salts into soft, non-adherent phosphate sludge and remove it by blow-down.
  • Colloidal conditioning: organic substances coat precipitates and help form loose, non-sticky deposits, particularly in low-pressure treatment described by the notes.
  • Remove existing deposits using a method suited to the deposit and equipment; avoid treating every scale as an acid-soluble carbonate.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{MgCl_2+2H_2O\rightarrow Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2HCl}\)
Essay · 7
a) Differentiate between scales and sludges? [BL-2]
b) A sample of hard water contains the following dissolved salts per litre. Ca (HCO3)2 = 16.2 mgs, Mg (HCO3)2 = 14.6 mg/L, CaCl2 = 111mgs, CaSO4 = 1.36mgs, turbidity = 10mgs. Calculate the temporary, permanent and total hardness of water in ppm. [BL-3]+
Part (a)

Scale is hard and adherent; sludge is soft, loose and slimy. The main differences are:

FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Part (b)

All masses are given per litre, so treat them as mg/L. Turbidity is suspended matter and is not included as Ca/Mg hardness.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:16.2\times\dfrac{100}{162}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:14.6\times\dfrac{100}{146}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{CaCl_2}:111\times\dfrac{100}{111}=100\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:1.36\times\dfrac{100}{136}=1\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(H_{temporary}=10+10=20\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=100+1=101\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=20+101=121\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 20 ppm; permanent hardness = 101 ppm; total hardness = 121 ppm.
Essay · 8Discuss various steps involved in the treatment of potable water. [BL-2]+
  • Screening: retain floating debris such as leaves, wood and large particles on screens.
  • Sedimentation: allow suspended matter to settle under gravity.
  • Coagulation/flocculation: add a suitable coagulant, mix, and form gelatinous flocs which capture fine suspended/colloidal impurities; allow these flocs to settle. Alum can produce Al(OH)₃ floc in water with suitable alkalinity.
  • Filtration: pass clarified water through a supported sand bed, retaining remaining fine particles; clean/maintain the bed as required.
  • Disinfection: use a suitable disinfectant, such as chlorine, to destroy remaining disease-causing microorganisms. Chlorine forms HOCl; adequate mixing/contact time and an appropriate residual are needed.
\(\mathrm{Al_2(SO_4)_3+3Ca(HCO_3)_2\rightarrow2Al(OH)_3\downarrow+3CaSO_4+6CO_2}\)
\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
\(\mathrm{HOCl\rightleftharpoons H^+ +OCl^-}\)
Essay · 9Explain the purification of water by Ion exchange process. Give the reactions involved. [BL-3]+

Ion-exchange resins are insoluble cross-linked polymers with exchangeable ions. A cation exchanger in the H⁺ form removes calcium, magnesium and other cations. An anion exchanger in the OH⁻ form removes anions. The released H⁺ and OH⁻ combine to form water.

Cation bed: hard water first passes through acidic resin, represented RH. Two H⁺ sites are needed to replace one divalent ion.

Anion bed: the acidic effluent passes through basic resin, represented R′OH. Anions are exchanged for hydroxyl ions.

Regenerate exhausted cation resin with dilute mineral acid and anion resin with dilute NaOH; rinse the beds before reuse. Turbid feed must be clarified to avoid resin-bed fouling.

\(\mathrm{2RH+CaCl_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+MgSO_4\rightarrow R_2Mg+H_2SO_4}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+Ca(HCO_3)_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2H_2CO_3}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}OH+HCl\rightarrow R^{\prime}Cl+H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{2R^{\prime}OH+H_2SO_4\rightarrow(R^{\prime})_2SO_4+2H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{R_2Ca+2HCl\rightarrow2RH+CaCl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}Cl+NaOH\rightarrow R^{\prime}OH+NaCl}\)
Essay · 10How is natural water disinfected by - [BL-1]
a) chlorine
b) bleaching powder
c) chloramine+
Part (a) Chlorine

Chlorine gas or its solution is mixed with water. Hydrolysis produces hypochlorous acid, which provides germicidal action. Allow sufficient contact and maintain an appropriate residual without excess chlorine causing objectionable taste/odour.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
\(\mathrm{HOCl\rightleftharpoons H^+ +OCl^-}\)
Part (b) Bleaching powder

The course represents bleaching powder as CaOCl₂. On treatment with water it makes chlorine available; chlorine then produces HOCl. It is convenient for small supplies but deteriorates during storage, can introduce calcium salts and must be used in a controlled amount.

\(\mathrm{CaOCl_2+H_2O\rightarrow Ca(OH)_2+Cl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
Part (c) Chloramine

Chlorine reacts with ammonia to form chloramine. It gives a more persistent residual; its action involves slow availability of HOCl.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+NH_3\rightarrow NH_2Cl+HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{NH_2Cl+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HOCl+NH_3}\)
Essay · 11Compare MF, UF, and NF membranes based on pore size, advantages, limitations, and applications.+
MembraneTypical pore size in supplied notesAdvantagesLimitationsApplications
MF0.1–1 μmRemoves suspended solids and bacteria; low pressure; useful pretreatmentDoes not reliably remove small viruses or dissolved saltsWastewater pretreatment, drinking-water clarification, food/beverage processing
UF0.01–0.1 μmRemoves colloids, bacteria and some viruses; moderate pressureDoes not remove dissolved ions or small molecules; virus removal is incompleteDairy, pharmaceuticals and water treatment
NF0.001–0.01 μmRemoves many divalent ions and small organics; lower pressure than ROLimited removal of monovalent ions and some small moleculesWater softening, dye/pesticide separation and food processing
Updated source comparison: the newer 07-02-2026 handout, PDF pp.61–62, visibly specifies MF 0.1–1 μm, UF 0.01–0.1 μm and NF 0.001–0.01 μm. This answer follows those course ranges. The earlier answer used MF 0.1–10 μm; ranges are illustrative rather than universal membrane cutoffs. The same newer handout gives conflicting RO ranges on these two pages, so no single RO pore range is inferred here.
Essay · 12What is desalination? Explain the process of desalination of water by reverse osmosis. [BL-2]+

Desalination is removal of dissolved salts from saline or brackish water to obtain lower-salinity water. In normal osmosis, water moves through a semipermeable membrane from the dilute side towards the more concentrated side.

In reverse osmosis, pressure greater than the osmotic pressure is applied to the concentrated side. Water is forced across the membrane towards the dilute/pure-water side; most dissolved salts are retained in the concentrated reject stream.

A simple cell has a pressurised saline-water compartment, a semipermeable membrane and a permeate outlet. The diagram shows the pressure applied through a piston, with pure water leaving below the membrane.

Advantages include compact equipment, continuous operation and no evaporation/condensation phase change. Limitations include pressure/energy requirements, membrane fouling and the need to manage the concentrate.

Problems on hardness .docx.pdf

Problem · 1A sample of water on analysis has been found to maintain the following in gm/L. Ca (HCO3)2 = 4.86, Mg (HCO3)2 = 5.84, CaSO4 = 6.80, MgSO4 = 8.40. Calculate the temporary and permanent hardness of water in degree French & degree Clarke.+

Given values

Hardness causing saltsAmount of hardness in mg/lMol.wtCaCO3 equivalents
Ca (HCO3)24.86=48601623000
Mg (HCO3)25.84=58401464000
CaSO46.80=68001365000
MgSO48.40=84001207000

Working

Convert gm/L to mg/L by multiplying by 1000. The concentrations are 4860, 5840, 6800 and 8400 mg/L respectively.

For these divalent salts: \(\text{CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}=\dfrac{\text{salt concentration}\times100}{\text{molecular weight of salt}}\).

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:4860\times\dfrac{100}{162}=3000\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:5840\times\dfrac{100}{146}=4000\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:6800\times\dfrac{100}{136}=5000\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:8400\times\dfrac{100}{120}=7000\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

Temporary hardness comes from the two bicarbonates: 3000 + 4000 = 7000 mg/L as CaCO₃.

Permanent hardness comes from the two sulphates: 5000 + 7000 = 12000 mg/L as CaCO₃.

Using \(1\text{ ppm}=0.1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}=0.07\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\), with the usual dilute-water approximation 1 mg/L ≈ 1 ppm.

Temporary hardness: 7000 × 0.1 = 700 °Fr; 7000 × 0.07 = 490 °Cl.

Permanent hardness: 12000 × 0.1 = 1200 °Fr; 12000 × 0.07 = 840 °Cl.

Final answer: Temporary hardness = 700 °Fr ≈ 490 °Cl; permanent hardness = 1200 °Fr ≈ 840 °Cl.
Problem · 2Calculate the temporary and permanent hardness of water sample having the following salts. Ca (HCO3)2 = 162 mg/L, Mg (HCO3)2 = 73 mg/L, CaSO4 = 136 mg/L, MgCl2 = 95 mg/L, CaCl2 = 111 mg/L, NaCl= 100 mg/L, Suspended matter = 50 mg/L.+

Given values

Hardness causing saltsAmount of hardness in mg/lMol.wtCaCO3 equivalents
Ca (HCO3)2162162100
Mg (HCO3)27314650
CaSO4136136100
MgCl29595100
CaCl2111111100

Working

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:162\times\dfrac{100}{162}=100\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:73\times\dfrac{100}{146}=50\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:136\times\dfrac{100}{136}=100\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{MgCl_2}:95\times\dfrac{100}{95}=100\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{CaCl_2}:111\times\dfrac{100}{111}=100\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

NaCl and suspended matter are not calcium/magnesium hardness contributions.

Temporary hardness = 100 + 50 = 150 ppm. Permanent hardness = 100 + 100 + 100 = 300 ppm. Total hardness = 150 + 300 = 450 ppm.

Final answer: Temporary hardness = 150 ppm; permanent hardness = 300 ppm; total hardness = 450 ppm.
Problem · 3Determine the total hardness of a sample of water in degree French & degree Clarke, which showed the following analysis: MgCl2 = 19 ppm, MgSO4 = 12 ppm, Ca(NO3)2= 16.4 ppm, NaOH = 40ppm, KOH = 56 ppm, suspended matter= 100 ppm, Ca (HCO3)2 = 162 ppm and Mg(NO3)2 =14.8 ppm.+

Given values

Hardness causing saltsAmount of hardness in mg/lMol.wtCaCO3 equivalents
Ca (HCO3)2162162100
Mg(NO3)214.814810
MgSO41212010
MgCl2199520
Ca(NO3)216.416410

Working

Working. Use the calcium/magnesium salt inventory in the table. NaOH, KOH and suspended matter are not added to the total Ca/Mg hardness; this calculation does not model subsequent precipitation reactions.

\(\mathrm{MgCl_2}:19\times\dfrac{100}{95}=20\) ppm as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:12\times\dfrac{100}{120}=10\) ppm as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Ca(NO_3)_2}:16.4\times\dfrac{100}{164}=10\) ppm as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:162\times\dfrac{100}{162}=100\) ppm as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Mg(NO_3)_2}:14.8\times\dfrac{100}{148}=10\) ppm as CaCO₃.

Total hardness = 20 + 10 + 10 + 100 + 10 = 150 ppm.

Degrees French = 150 × 0.1 = 15 °Fr. Degrees Clarke = 150 × 0.07 = 10.5 °Cl, using the rounded conversion.

Final answer: Total hardness = 150 ppm = 15 °Fr ≈ 10.5 °Cl.
Problem · 4Calculate the temporary and permanent hardness of water in ppm, degree French & degree Clarke of a sample of water containing the following dissolved salts. CaCO3 = 100 mg/L, Mg (HCO3)2 = 14.6 mg/L, HCl= 36 mg/L, MgSO4 = 12 mg/L, NaCl= 84 mg/L, CaCl2 = 11.1mg/L+
Source discrepancy
The question prints Mg(HCO₃)₂ = 14.6 mg/L; its table uses 146 mg/L and a CaCO₃ equivalent of 100 mg/L. The question’s equivalent is 10 mg/L. The supplied table also omits HCl from its classification. The solution uses the question literally and accounts for acid neutralization under the stated dissolved-salt assumption. If 146 mg/L were intended instead, the same method would give temporary hardness ≈ 150.68 ppm, permanent hardness ≈ 69.32 ppm and total hardness = 220 ppm; this is not the primary answer.

Given values

Hardness causing saltsAmount of hardness in mg/lMol.wtCaCO3 equivalents
CaCO3100100100
Mg (HCO3)2146146100
MgSO41212010
CaCl211.111110

Working

Using the given data, including Mg(HCO₃)₂ = 14.6 mg/L and HCl = 36 mg/L.

Assumption: the phrase “dissolved salts” is taken literally, so the listed CaCO₃ contributes 100 mg/L of dissolved carbonate hardness. Assume no removal of Ca/Mg by precipitation. If CaCO₃ instead means undissolved solid, the question needs a different dissolution/equilibrium model; the answer below is for the stated dissolved-salt interpretation.

\(\mathrm{CaCO_3}:100\times\dfrac{100}{100}=100\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:14.6\times\dfrac{100}{146}=10\) mg/L as CaCO₃ — not the table’s 100.

\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:12\times\dfrac{100}{120}=10\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

\(\mathrm{CaCl_2}:11.1\times\dfrac{100}{111}=10\) mg/L as CaCO₃.

NaCl has no Ca/Mg hardness contribution. HCl does not add calcium or magnesium, so it must not be added to total hardness.

Total hardness = 100 + 10 + 10 + 10 = 130 ppm as CaCO₃. Before accounting for acid, carbonate hardness is 100 + 10 = 110 ppm and non-carbonate hardness is 10 + 10 = 20 ppm.

HCl consumes carbonate/bicarbonate alkalinity, converting an equivalent amount of carbonate (temporary) hardness into non-carbonate (permanent) hardness. It changes the classification, while the total Ca/Mg hardness remains 130 ppm under the assumption above.

A representative conversion is \(\mathrm{CaCO_3+2HCl\rightarrow CaCl_2+H_2O+CO_2}\). Thus 2 mol HCl neutralize alkalinity equivalent to 1 mol CaCO₃.

Use M(HCl) = 36.5 g/mol (H = 1, Cl = 35.5) and M(CaCO₃) = 100 g/mol. The printed concentration remains 36 mg/L; it is not changed to 36.5 mg/L.

\[A=36\times\dfrac{100}{2\times36.5}=36\times\dfrac{50}{36.5}=49.315068\ldots\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\]

Acid is insufficient to consume all 110 ppm of carbonate hardness. Temporary hardness = 110 − 49.315068… = 60.684931… ppm. Permanent hardness = 20 + 49.315068… = 69.315068… ppm.

Check: 60.684931… + 69.315068… = 130 ppm. The acidity is subtracted from temporary hardness and added to permanent hardness exactly once.

Using 1 ppm = 0.1 °Fr = 0.07 °Cl: temporary hardness = 6.068493… °Fr ≈ 4.247945… °Cl; permanent hardness = 6.931506… °Fr ≈ 4.852054… °Cl. Total hardness = 13 °Fr ≈ 9.10 °Cl.

Final answer: Using the printed question and stated assumption: temporary hardness ≈ 60.68 ppm = 6.068 °Fr ≈ 4.248 °Cl; permanent hardness ≈ 69.32 ppm = 6.932 °Fr ≈ 4.852 °Cl; total hardness = 130 ppm = 13 °Fr ≈ 9.10 °Cl.

Problem · 1A sample of water contains Mg(HCO₃)₂ = 73, CaCl₂ = 111, Ca(HCO₃)₂ = 81, MgSO₄ = 40 and MgCl₂ = 95 mg/L. Calculate temporary, permanent and total hardness.+

Convert each salt to CaCO₃ equivalent

\[\text{CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}=\dfrac{\text{salt concentration}\times100}{\text{molar mass of salt}}\]
SaltConcentration (mg/L)Molar massCaCO₃ equivalent (mg/L)
Mg(HCO₃)₂7314673 × 100 ÷ 146 = 50.00
CaCl₂111111111 × 100 ÷ 111 = 100.00
Ca(HCO₃)₂8116281 × 100 ÷ 162 = 50.00
MgSO₄4012040 × 100 ÷ 120 = 33.33
MgCl₂959595 × 100 ÷ 95 = 100.00

Bicarbonates contribute temporary hardness; calcium/magnesium chlorides and sulphates contribute permanent hardness. NaCl does not contribute hardness.

Temporary hardness = sum of bicarbonate equivalents; permanent hardness = sum of the other hardness-causing equivalents.

Answer

\[\boxed{\text{Temporary}=100.00\text{ ppm};\quad\text{Permanent}=233.33\text{ ppm}}\]

Total hardness

\[\boxed{100.00+233.33=333.33\text{ ppm}}\]

Water and its Treatment_notes.pdf

Short answer · 1Name the salts that cause temporary hardness. How are they removed? BL-1+

Temporary hardness is mainly caused by calcium bicarbonate, Ca(HCO₃)₂, and magnesium bicarbonate, Mg(HCO₃)₂. Boiling removes it by decomposing the bicarbonates and separating insoluble CaCO₃ or Mg(OH)₂.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 2Mention the different units of hardness? Give the relation between the units. BL-2+

The principal units are mg/L as CaCO₃, ppm as CaCO₃, degrees French (°Fr) and degrees Clarke (°Cl). For dilute water, 1 mg/L ≈ 1 ppm.

\(1\text{ ppm}=0.1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}\approx0.07\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\)
\(1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}=10\text{ ppm};\quad1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\approx14.3\text{ ppm}\)
The source gives rounded conversions. Numerical answers here consistently use 0.07 °Cl per ppm rather than mixing that factor with the separately rounded reciprocal 14.3.
Short answer · 3List out the disadvantages of usage of hard water in industries. BL-2+
  • Boilers: scale/sludge formation, poor heat transfer, fuel wastage, overheating and corrosion-related troubles.
  • Textile/dyeing and laundry: soap wastage, scum, stains and uneven colour.
  • Paper: poor finish and staining from iron salts.
  • Sugar: interference with refining/crystallisation.
  • Concrete and pharmaceutical manufacture: unwanted salts can interfere with product quality.
Short answer · 4Hardness is expressed in terms of CaCO3 equivalents? Give reason. BL-3+

Different hardness-producing substances have different molecular masses. Expressing each contribution as an equivalent amount of CaCO₃ puts them on one common chemical-equivalence basis so that contributions can be added and compared. CaCO₃ has a convenient molecular mass of 100 and equivalent mass of 50.

\(\text{CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}=\text{concentration}\times\dfrac{50}{\text{equivalent mass of substance}}\)
Short answer · 5Mention the dissolved salts that cause temporary and permanent hardness in water. BL-2+
  • Temporary: Ca(HCO₃)₂ and Mg(HCO₃)₂.
  • Permanent: CaCl₂, MgCl₂, CaSO₄, MgSO₄, Ca(NO₃)₂ and Mg(NO₃)₂. Permanent hardness is not removed by simple boiling.
Short answer · 6Give the principle involved in the estimation of hardness by EDTA method. BL-2+

At about pH 10, calcium and magnesium ions form stable complexes with EDTA. Eriochrome Black T (EBT) first gives a wine-red metal–indicator complex. EDTA binds the metal ions more strongly, releasing the blue indicator at the endpoint. The standard EDTA volume measures the hardness.

Short answer · 7Why is ammonical (NH4Cl+NH4OH) buffer added during the determination of hardness in water samples? BL-3+

NH₄Cl/NH₄OH buffer maintains pH close to 10, the conditions needed for the EDTA complexation and a clear EBT endpoint. If the pH drifts, metal–EDTA/indicator equilibria change and the endpoint becomes unreliable.

Short answer · 8Specify the requirements for portable water? BL-2+
  • Clear, colourless, odourless and acceptable in taste.
  • Low turbidity; free from objectionable suspended/colloidal impurities and disease-causing organisms.
  • Free from harmful amounts of toxic minerals and objectionable dissolved gases.
  • The supplied handout lists pH 6.5–8.5, turbidity not exceeding 10 ppm and colour 5 ppm.
These numerical entries are retained as course-handout values. Its embedded table is labelled Indian Standard IS 10500; they have not been certified or relabelled as current WHO specifications.
Short answer · 9What is disinfection? What are the different methods of disinfection? BL-2+

Disinfection is the destruction of disease-producing microorganisms in water. Methods include boiling and chemical disinfection by chlorine, bleaching powder and chloramine. Chlorination produces hypochlorous acid (HOCl), responsible for much of the germicidal action.

Short answer · 10Why is chloramine preferred to bleaching powder during disinfection? BL-3+

Chloramine provides a longer-lasting disinfectant residual because it is more persistent than free chlorine. In this comparison it also avoids the objectionable taste/odour associated with excess bleaching powder and does not introduce the calcium component of bleaching powder. Its advantage here is persistent residual protection.

Short answer · 11Define break point chlorination? BL-1+

Chlorine is first consumed by reducing substances and ammonia/organic matter. Sufficient further addition destroys much of the combined chlorine. Beyond the breakpoint, a controllable free-chlorine residual can remain for disinfection rather than all added chlorine being consumed by the water’s demand.

Short answer · 12What happens if natural water is fed into boilers? BL-3+

Evaporation concentrates dissolved salts. Untreated feed water can cause scale and sludge, priming/foaming (carryover), boiler corrosion and caustic embrittlement. Deposits lower heat transfer, waste fuel, choke passages and may cause overheating or failure.

Short answer · 13How does presence of Na2CO3 in boiler feed water cause caustic embrittlement and suggest its prevention. BL-2+

Sodium carbonate can produce alkaline conditions, and evaporation concentrates NaOH in crevices and stressed joints. The concentrated alkali promotes intergranular cracking called caustic embrittlement. Prevent it by controlling alkalinity, using phosphate rather than carbonate conditioning, and suitable inhibitors/treatment.

Short answer · 14The presence of CO2 in the boiler feed water is not advisable. Justify. BL-3+

Dissolved CO₂ forms carbonic acid: CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃. This lowers pH and attacks boiler/condensate-line metal. Deaeration and suitable controlled water treatment reduce this corrosion.

Short answer · 15Why oxygen is to be removed from boiler feed water and how is it removed? BL-3+

Dissolved oxygen causes corrosion and pitting of boiler metal. Remove it by mechanical deaeration and suitable oxygen scavengers. For sodium sulphite: 2Na₂SO₃ + O₂ → 2Na₂SO₄.

Short answer · 16Differentiate between scales and sludges. BL-2+
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Short answer · 17Name the hardest scale formed? How is it removed? BL-2+

Calcium sulphate (CaSO₄) forms a hard, tenacious boiler scale. It is adherent and difficult to remove. EDTA treatment forms soluble calcium complexes. Cleaning conditions must suit the boiler and deposit.

Final answer: CaSO₄ scale; remove by suitable EDTA chemical cleaning.

Wording qualification: “hardest” is not a universal ranking established by these pages. The same source also describes very firmly adherent calcium/magnesium silicate deposits; their removal requires deposit-specific assessment. Dilute HCl is the source treatment for carbonate scale, not a universal sulphate/silicate treatment. This answer gives the textbook-supported hard-scale/removal pair without claiming a lecturer-approved superlative.
Short answer · 18State two reasons for the formation of scales in boilers? BL-2+
  • Heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate, depositing CaCO₃.
  • At high boiler temperatures the solubility of CaSO₄ decreases, causing a hard CaSO₄ deposit.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 19Mention the type of scales formed in low pressure boilers with necessary equations. BL-2+

Calcium carbonate is the principal low-pressure scale formed in boilers. Heating bicarbonate-containing water produces CaCO₃ precipitation. Magnesium bicarbonate can also give Mg(OH)₂ deposits.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 20In demineralization process, water is usually passed through cation exchanger and then through anion exchanger. Give reason. BL-3+

The cation bed removes Ca²⁺/Mg²⁺ and releases H⁺, producing acidic water. The following anion bed replaces anions with OH⁻, which combines with H⁺ to form water. Removing hardness cations first also avoids exposing them to the alkaline conditions an anion-first sequence could create, with precipitates fouling the bed.

\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
Short answer · 21Illustrate the reactions involved in calgon conditioning of hard water.BL-2+

Calgon is sodium hexametaphosphate, (NaPO₃)₆, also written Na₂[Na₄(PO₃)₆]. It complexes calcium into a soluble species, preventing calcium-salt scale. Reaction:

\(\mathrm{Na_2[Na_4(PO_3)_6]\rightleftharpoons2Na^+ +[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}}\)
\(\mathrm{2CaSO_4+[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}\rightarrow[Ca_2P_6O_{18}]^{2-}+2Na_2SO_4}\)
Short answer · 22State and explain the principle involved in reverse osmosis process? BL-1+

A pressure greater than the osmotic pressure is applied to the concentrated/brackish-water side of a semipermeable membrane. Water then moves from the concentrated side to the dilute/pure-water side, opposite to spontaneous osmosis, while most dissolved salts are retained.

Short answer · 23Why Calgon conditioning better than phosphate conditioning? BL- 3+

Calgon keeps calcium in a soluble complex. Phosphate conditioning instead precipitates calcium/magnesium phosphates as soft sludge, which must be removed by blow-down. Thus Calgon’s advantage in this comparison is suppression of precipitated deposits/sludge rather than merely changing a hard deposit into a loose one. Treatment choice still depends on boiler conditions.

Essay · 1Explain in detail how hardness of a water sample is estimated by EDTA method. BL-2+

Principle: disodium EDTA complexes Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺. At pH about 10, EBT forms a wine-red metal–indicator complex; EDTA displaces the indicator, giving a blue endpoint.

Prepare standard hard water containing 1 g CaCO₃ equivalent per litre (1 mg/mL): dissolve pure CaCO₃ in minimum dilute HCl, evaporate to dryness, redissolve and make to 1 L.

Standardise EDTA: pipette 50 mL standard hard water, add 10–15 mL buffer and 4–5 drops EBT. Titrate to clear blue. Let the titre be V₁ mL. The aliquot contains 50 mg CaCO₃ equivalent.

Total hardness: titrate 50 mL of the water sample in the same way; let the titre be V₂ mL.

Permanent hardness: boil 250 mL of sample down to about 50 mL, filter off the precipitate and wash it. Collect filtrate and washings and make back to 250 mL. Titrate a 50 mL aliquot; let the titre be V₃ mL. Restoring the original volume avoids an artificial concentration change.

Temporary hardness is total minus permanent hardness. The equations below follow the 50 mL aliquots and standard-water concentration specified above.

\(\mathrm{[M-EBT]+EDTA\rightarrow[M-EDTA]+EBT}\quad\text{(wine red → blue)}\)
\(1\text{ mL EDTA}=\dfrac{50}{V_1}\text{ mg CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}\)
\(H_{total}=\dfrac{V_2(50/V_1)\times1000}{50}=\dfrac{1000V_2}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=\dfrac{1000V_3}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{temporary}=\dfrac{1000(V_2-V_3)}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
Essay · 2Calculate the permanent hardness and temporary hardness of a water sample containing the following salts, Mg(HCO3)2 = 146 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2= 40.5mg/L, CaSO4=34mg/L, MgSO4=60mg/L. BL-3+

Use CaCO₃ equivalent = concentration × 100/molecular mass for the divalent salts.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:146\times\dfrac{100}{146}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:40.5\times\dfrac{100}{162}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:34\times\dfrac{100}{136}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:60\times\dfrac{100}{120}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=100+25=125\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=25+50=75\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=125+75=200\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 125 ppm; permanent hardness = 75 ppm; total = 200 ppm, all as CaCO₃.
Essay · 3Calculate temporary and total hardness present in 10,000 liters of a water sample containing the following impurities, Mg(HCO3)2 = 73 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2= 162 mg/L, CaSO4=136mg/L, MgCl2=95mg/L. BL-3+

First find concentration as CaCO₃ equivalent. The 10,000 L volume does not change the ppm value; it is used only when calculating the total equivalent mass in the batch.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:73\times\dfrac{100}{146}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:162\times\dfrac{100}{162}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:136\times\dfrac{100}{136}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgCl_2}:95\times\dfrac{100}{95}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=50+100=150\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=100+100=200\text{ ppm};\quad H_{total}=150+200=350\text{ ppm}\)
\(m_{temporary}=150\times10000=1500000\text{ mg}=1.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
\(m_{total}=350\times10000=3500000\text{ mg}=3.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 150 ppm and total hardness = 350 ppm; the 10,000 L batch contains 1.5 kg temporary and 3.5 kg total CaCO₃-equivalent hardness.
Essay · 4Explain how caustic embrittlement occurs in a boiler and outline the prevention methods. BL- 2+

Caustic embrittlement and boiler corrosion

Caustic embrittlement is intergranular cracking of stressed boiler steel associated with concentrated caustic alkali. Sodium carbonate can produce alkaline conditions: Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Concentration by evaporation in crevices/riveted joints can create aggressive conditions at stressed regions; hydrogen/stress effects depend on the service conditions.

The traditional course net scheme is Na₂CO₃ + H₂O → 2NaOH + CO₂ under the described boiler conditions. The aqueous carbonate hydrolysis step can instead be written Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Both descriptions explain the development of caustic alkalinity; the net scheme is a simplified course model, not an unrestricted ambient-water conversion.

Prevention: use sodium phosphate rather than sodium carbonate; tannin/lignin or sodium sulphate are described as blocking hair-cracks and reducing caustic penetration. This explains why Na₂SO₄ controls caustic embrittlement; it is not neutralisation of NaOH. Typical historical Na₂SO₄:NaOH concentration ratios of 1:1, 2:1 and 3:1 for its stated pressure categories. Actual boiler dosing must follow the applicable engineered water-chemistry programme, rather than using those textbook ratios as a universal operating instruction.

Dissolved O₂ accelerates corrosion/pitting; remove it by deaeration and suitable scavengers: 2Na₂SO₃ + O₂ → 2Na₂SO₄, or N₂H₄ + O₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O (a historical toxic chemical requiring controlled handling). CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid: CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃, increasing condensate-line corrosion; remove/neutralise it through properly designed treatment. Hydrolysis of MgCl₂ can create acidic corrosive conditions. Prevent corrosion through feedwater treatment, deaeration and monitored chemistry, not improvised chemical dosing.

Essay · 5Define scales and sludges? How are they formed? Give their disadvantages and removal methods. BL-2+

As boiler water evaporates, dissolved salts concentrate and precipitate. Sludge is a soft, loose, slimy deposit, often accumulating in cooler/slow-flow parts. Scale is a hard adherent coating on the boiler wall.

  • CaCO₃ scale: heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate.
  • CaSO₄ scale: solubility decreases at high boiler temperatures.
  • Mg(OH)₂ deposits: hydrolysis/decomposition of magnesium salts.
  • Silica/silicate deposits: calcium/magnesium silicates stick firmly to the wall.
  • Consequences: poor heat transfer and fuel wastage, choked pipes/valves, lower efficiency, overheating, weakened tubes and possible failure or explosion.
  • Sludge removal: blow-down and brushing; reduce sludge formation by suitable feed-water softening.
  • Scale removal: scraper/wire brushing for loose deposits, suitable chemical treatment for the deposit and controlled thermal treatment where appropriate. A typical catalyst contains dilute HCl for carbonate scale and EDTA treatment for calcium/magnesium scales.
  • Prevention: external feed-water treatment plus internal conditioning and removal of accumulated sludge.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Essay · 6Write short note on boiler corrosion. BL-2+

Caustic embrittlement and boiler corrosion

Caustic embrittlement is intergranular cracking of stressed boiler steel associated with concentrated caustic alkali. Sodium carbonate can produce alkaline conditions: Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Concentration by evaporation in crevices/riveted joints can create aggressive conditions at stressed regions; hydrogen/stress effects depend on the service conditions.

The traditional course net scheme is Na₂CO₃ + H₂O → 2NaOH + CO₂ under the described boiler conditions. The aqueous carbonate hydrolysis step can instead be written Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Both descriptions explain the development of caustic alkalinity; the net scheme is a simplified course model, not an unrestricted ambient-water conversion.

Prevention: use sodium phosphate rather than sodium carbonate; tannin/lignin or sodium sulphate are described as blocking hair-cracks and reducing caustic penetration. This explains why Na₂SO₄ controls caustic embrittlement; it is not neutralisation of NaOH. Typical historical Na₂SO₄:NaOH concentration ratios of 1:1, 2:1 and 3:1 for its stated pressure categories. Actual boiler dosing must follow the applicable engineered water-chemistry programme, rather than using those textbook ratios as a universal operating instruction.

Dissolved O₂ accelerates corrosion/pitting; remove it by deaeration and suitable scavengers: 2Na₂SO₃ + O₂ → 2Na₂SO₄, or N₂H₄ + O₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O (a historical toxic chemical requiring controlled handling). CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid: CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃, increasing condensate-line corrosion; remove/neutralise it through properly designed treatment. Hydrolysis of MgCl₂ can create acidic corrosive conditions. Prevent corrosion through feedwater treatment, deaeration and monitored chemistry, not improvised chemical dosing.

Essay · 7
a) Differentiate between scales and sludges? BL-2
b) A sample of hard water contains the following dissolved salts per litre. Ca (HCO3)2 = 16.2 mgs, Mg (HCO3)2 = 14.6 mg/L, CaCl2= 111mgs, CaSO4=1.36mgs, turbidity =10mgs.Calculate the temporary permanent and total hardness of water in ppm. BL-3+
Part (a)

Scale is hard and adherent; sludge is soft, loose and slimy. The main differences are:

FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Part (b)

All masses are given per litre, so treat them as mg/L. Turbidity is suspended matter and is not included as Ca/Mg hardness.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:16.2\times\dfrac{100}{162}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:14.6\times\dfrac{100}{146}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{CaCl_2}:111\times\dfrac{100}{111}=100\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:1.36\times\dfrac{100}{136}=1\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(H_{temporary}=10+10=20\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=100+1=101\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=20+101=121\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 20 ppm; permanent hardness = 101 ppm; total hardness = 121 ppm.
Essay · 9Discuss the reasons and control methods for the following boiler troubles. BL-2
i) Scales
ii) caustic embrittlement+

Scale forms as salts concentrate and become insoluble under boiler conditions. Important causes are bicarbonate decomposition, reduced CaSO₄ solubility at high temperature, magnesium-salt hydrolysis and silica/silicate deposition.

  • External control: remove Ca/Mg salts before the feed water enters the boiler, for example by ion exchange.
  • Calgon conditioning: keep calcium in soluble complexes.
  • Phosphate conditioning: convert hardness salts into soft, non-adherent phosphate sludge and remove it by blow-down.
  • Colloidal conditioning: organic substances coat precipitates and help form loose, non-sticky deposits, particularly in low-pressure treatment described by the notes.
  • Remove existing deposits using a method suited to the deposit and equipment; avoid treating every scale as an acid-soluble carbonate.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{MgCl_2+2H_2O\rightarrow Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2HCl}\)

Caustic embrittlement and boiler corrosion

Caustic embrittlement is intergranular cracking of stressed boiler steel associated with concentrated caustic alkali. Sodium carbonate can produce alkaline conditions: Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Concentration by evaporation in crevices/riveted joints can create aggressive conditions at stressed regions; hydrogen/stress effects depend on the service conditions.

The traditional course net scheme is Na₂CO₃ + H₂O → 2NaOH + CO₂ under the described boiler conditions. The aqueous carbonate hydrolysis step can instead be written Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Both descriptions explain the development of caustic alkalinity; the net scheme is a simplified course model, not an unrestricted ambient-water conversion.

Prevention: use sodium phosphate rather than sodium carbonate; tannin/lignin or sodium sulphate are described as blocking hair-cracks and reducing caustic penetration. This explains why Na₂SO₄ controls caustic embrittlement; it is not neutralisation of NaOH. Typical historical Na₂SO₄:NaOH concentration ratios of 1:1, 2:1 and 3:1 for its stated pressure categories. Actual boiler dosing must follow the applicable engineered water-chemistry programme, rather than using those textbook ratios as a universal operating instruction.

Dissolved O₂ accelerates corrosion/pitting; remove it by deaeration and suitable scavengers: 2Na₂SO₃ + O₂ → 2Na₂SO₄, or N₂H₄ + O₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O (a historical toxic chemical requiring controlled handling). CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid: CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃, increasing condensate-line corrosion; remove/neutralise it through properly designed treatment. Hydrolysis of MgCl₂ can create acidic corrosive conditions. Prevent corrosion through feedwater treatment, deaeration and monitored chemistry, not improvised chemical dosing.

Essay · 10Explain the purification of water by Ion exchange process. Give the reactions involved. BL-3+

Ion-exchange resins are insoluble cross-linked polymers with exchangeable ions. A cation exchanger in the H⁺ form removes calcium, magnesium and other cations. An anion exchanger in the OH⁻ form removes anions. The released H⁺ and OH⁻ combine to form water.

Cation bed: hard water first passes through acidic resin, represented RH. Two H⁺ sites are needed to replace one divalent ion.

Anion bed: the acidic effluent passes through basic resin, represented R′OH. Anions are exchanged for hydroxyl ions.

Regenerate exhausted cation resin with dilute mineral acid and anion resin with dilute NaOH; rinse the beds before reuse. Turbid feed must be clarified to avoid resin-bed fouling.

\(\mathrm{2RH+CaCl_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+MgSO_4\rightarrow R_2Mg+H_2SO_4}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+Ca(HCO_3)_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2H_2CO_3}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}OH+HCl\rightarrow R^{\prime}Cl+H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{2R^{\prime}OH+H_2SO_4\rightarrow(R^{\prime})_2SO_4+2H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{R_2Ca+2HCl\rightarrow2RH+CaCl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}Cl+NaOH\rightarrow R^{\prime}OH+NaCl}\)
Essay · 11How is natural water disinfected by BL-1
a) chlorine
b) bleaching powder
c) chloramine+
Part (a) Chlorine

Chlorine gas or its solution is mixed with water. Hydrolysis produces hypochlorous acid, which provides germicidal action. Allow sufficient contact and maintain an appropriate residual without excess chlorine causing objectionable taste/odour.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
\(\mathrm{HOCl\rightleftharpoons H^+ +OCl^-}\)
Part (b) Bleaching powder

The course represents bleaching powder as CaOCl₂. On treatment with water it makes chlorine available; chlorine then produces HOCl. It is convenient for small supplies but deteriorates during storage, can introduce calcium salts and must be used in a controlled amount.

\(\mathrm{CaOCl_2+H_2O\rightarrow Ca(OH)_2+Cl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
Part (c) Chloramine

Chlorine reacts with ammonia to form chloramine. It gives a more persistent residual; its action involves slow availability of HOCl.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+NH_3\rightarrow NH_2Cl+HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{NH_2Cl+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HOCl+NH_3}\)
Essay · 12What is desalination? Explain desalination of water by reverse osmosis process. BL-2+

Desalination is removal of dissolved salts from saline or brackish water to obtain lower-salinity water. In normal osmosis, water moves through a semipermeable membrane from the dilute side towards the more concentrated side.

In reverse osmosis, pressure greater than the osmotic pressure is applied to the concentrated side. Water is forced across the membrane towards the dilute/pure-water side; most dissolved salts are retained in the concentrated reject stream.

A simple cell has a pressurised saline-water compartment, a semipermeable membrane and a permeate outlet. The diagram shows the pressure applied through a piston, with pure water leaving below the membrane.

Advantages include compact equipment, continuous operation and no evaporation/condensation phase change. Limitations include pressure/energy requirements, membrane fouling and the need to manage the concentrate.

Water and Its Treatment (class on 07-02-2026) (1).pdf

Essay · 1Explain in detail how hardness of a water sample is estimated by EDTA method [BL-2]+

Principle: disodium EDTA complexes Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺. At pH about 10, EBT forms a wine-red metal–indicator complex; EDTA displaces the indicator, giving a blue endpoint.

Prepare standard hard water containing 1 g CaCO₃ equivalent per litre (1 mg/mL): dissolve pure CaCO₃ in minimum dilute HCl, evaporate to dryness, redissolve and make to 1 L.

Standardise EDTA: pipette 50 mL standard hard water, add 10–15 mL buffer and 4–5 drops EBT. Titrate to clear blue. Let the titre be V₁ mL. The aliquot contains 50 mg CaCO₃ equivalent.

Total hardness: titrate 50 mL of the water sample in the same way; let the titre be V₂ mL.

Permanent hardness: boil 250 mL of sample down to about 50 mL, filter off the precipitate and wash it. Collect filtrate and washings and make back to 250 mL. Titrate a 50 mL aliquot; let the titre be V₃ mL. Restoring the original volume avoids an artificial concentration change.

Temporary hardness is total minus permanent hardness. The equations below follow the 50 mL aliquots and standard-water concentration specified above.

\(\mathrm{[M-EBT]+EDTA\rightarrow[M-EDTA]+EBT}\quad\text{(wine red → blue)}\)
\(1\text{ mL EDTA}=\dfrac{50}{V_1}\text{ mg CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}\)
\(H_{total}=\dfrac{V_2(50/V_1)\times1000}{50}=\dfrac{1000V_2}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=\dfrac{1000V_3}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{temporary}=\dfrac{1000(V_2-V_3)}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
Essay · 2Calculate the permanent hardness and temporary hardness of a water sample containing the following salts, Mg(HCO3)2 = 146 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2 = 40.5mg/L, CaSO4 = 34mg/L, MgSO4 = 60mg/L. [BL-3]+

Use CaCO₃ equivalent = concentration × 100/molecular mass for the divalent salts.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:146\times\dfrac{100}{146}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:40.5\times\dfrac{100}{162}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:34\times\dfrac{100}{136}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:60\times\dfrac{100}{120}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=100+25=125\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=25+50=75\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=125+75=200\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 125 ppm; permanent hardness = 75 ppm; total = 200 ppm, all as CaCO₃.
Essay · 3Explain the stages involved in the process of break-point chlorination with necessary illustration. [BL-2]+

Breakpoint chlorination is the addition of enough chlorine to satisfy the water’s chlorine demand and pass through the destruction of combined chlorine, after which increasing free residual chlorine can be obtained. Read the graph with chlorine dose on the horizontal axis and chlorine residual on the vertical axis.

  • Up to A: reducing substances consume chlorine; little or no residual is measured.
  • A–B: reaction with ammonia/nitrogen-containing substances forms chloramines; combined residual rises.
  • B–C: further chlorine destroys chloramines and other combined-chlorine compounds, so residual falls.
  • At C: the breakpoint is reached. Much of the chlorine demand/combined chlorine has been dealt with.
  • Beyond C: additional chlorine gives an increasing free-chlorine residual, providing disinfection after an appropriate contact period.
The breakpoint does not mean all chlorine addition must cease in every treatment process; the purpose beyond it is an appropriate disinfectant residual.
Short answer · 1Name the salts that cause temporary hardness. How are they removed? [BL-2]+

Temporary hardness is mainly caused by calcium bicarbonate, Ca(HCO₃)₂, and magnesium bicarbonate, Mg(HCO₃)₂. Boiling removes it by decomposing the bicarbonates and separating insoluble CaCO₃ or Mg(OH)₂.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 2Mention the diffe rent units of hardness? Give the relation between the units. [BL-2]+

The principal units are mg/L as CaCO₃, ppm as CaCO₃, degrees French (°Fr) and degrees Clarke (°Cl). For dilute water, 1 mg/L ≈ 1 ppm.

\(1\text{ ppm}=0.1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}\approx0.07\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\)
\(1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Fr}=10\text{ ppm};\quad1\,^{\circ}\mathrm{Cl}\approx14.3\text{ ppm}\)
The source gives rounded conversions. Numerical answers here consistently use 0.07 °Cl per ppm rather than mixing that factor with the separately rounded reciprocal 14.3.
Short answer · 3List out the disadvantages of usage of hard water in industries. [BL-2]+
  • Boilers: scale/sludge formation, poor heat transfer, fuel wastage, overheating and corrosion-related troubles.
  • Textile/dyeing and laundry: soap wastage, scum, stains and uneven colour.
  • Paper: poor finish and staining from iron salts.
  • Sugar: interference with refining/crystallisation.
  • Concrete and pharmaceutical manufacture: unwanted salts can interfere with product quality.
Short answer · 4Hardness is expressed in terms of CaCO3 equivalents? Give reason. [BL-3]+

Different hardness-producing substances have different molecular masses. Expressing each contribution as an equivalent amount of CaCO₃ puts them on one common chemical-equivalence basis so that contributions can be added and compared. CaCO₃ has a convenient molecular mass of 100 and equivalent mass of 50.

\(\text{CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}=\text{concentration}\times\dfrac{50}{\text{equivalent mass of substance}}\)
Short answer · 5Mention the dissolved salts that cause temporary and permanent hardness in water. [BL-2]+
  • Temporary: Ca(HCO₃)₂ and Mg(HCO₃)₂.
  • Permanent: CaCl₂, MgCl₂, CaSO₄, MgSO₄, Ca(NO₃)₂ and Mg(NO₃)₂. Permanent hardness is not removed by simple boiling.
Short answer · 6Give the principle involved in the estimation of hardness by EDTA method. [BL-2]+

At about pH 10, calcium and magnesium ions form stable complexes with EDTA. Eriochrome Black T (EBT) first gives a wine-red metal–indicator complex. EDTA binds the metal ions more strongly, releasing the blue indicator at the endpoint. The standard EDTA volume measures the hardness.

Short answer · 7Why is (NH4Cl+NH4OH) buffe r added during the determination of hardness in water samples? [BL-3]+

NH₄Cl/NH₄OH buffer maintains pH close to 10, the conditions needed for the EDTA complexation and a clear EBT endpoint. If the pH drifts, metal–EDTA/indicator equilibria change and the endpoint becomes unreliable.

Short answer · 8Specify the requirements for potable water? [BL-2]+
  • Clear, colourless, odourless and acceptable in taste.
  • Low turbidity; free from objectionable suspended/colloidal impurities and disease-causing organisms.
  • Free from harmful amounts of toxic minerals and objectionable dissolved gases.
  • The supplied handout lists pH 6.5–8.5, turbidity not exceeding 10 ppm and colour 5 ppm.
These numerical entries are retained as course-handout values. Its embedded table is labelled Indian Standard IS 10500; they have not been certified or relabelled as current WHO specifications.
Short answer · 9What is disinfection? What are the diffe rent methods of disinfection? [BL-2]+

Disinfection is the destruction of disease-producing microorganisms in water. Methods include boiling and chemical disinfection by chlorine, bleaching powder and chloramine. Chlorination produces hypochlorous acid (HOCl), responsible for much of the germicidal action.

Short answer · 10Why is chloramine preferred to bleaching powder during disinfection? [BL-3]+

Chloramine provides a longer-lasting disinfectant residual because it is more persistent than free chlorine. In this comparison it also avoids the objectionable taste/odour associated with excess bleaching powder and does not introduce the calcium component of bleaching powder. Its advantage here is persistent residual protection.

Short answer · 11How does break point chlorination improve water quality in municipal water supplies? [BL-3]+

Chlorine is first consumed by reducing substances and ammonia/organic matter. Sufficient further addition destroys much of the combined chlorine. Beyond the breakpoint, a controllable free-chlorine residual can remain for disinfection rather than all added chlorine being consumed by the water’s demand.

Short answer · 2What happens if natural water is fed into boilers? BL-3+

Evaporation concentrates dissolved salts. Untreated feed water can cause scale and sludge, priming/foaming (carryover), boiler corrosion and caustic embrittlement. Deposits lower heat transfer, waste fuel, choke passages and may cause overheating or failure.

Short answer · 1Differentiate between scales and sludges. BL-2+
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities

Short answer · 2Name the hardest scale formed? How is it removed? BL-2+

Calcium sulphate (CaSO₄) forms a hard, tenacious boiler scale. It is adherent and difficult to remove. EDTA treatment forms soluble calcium complexes. Cleaning conditions must suit the boiler and deposit.

Final answer: CaSO₄ scale; remove by suitable EDTA chemical cleaning.

Wording qualification: “hardest” is not a universal ranking established by these pages. The same source also describes very firmly adherent calcium/magnesium silicate deposits; their removal requires deposit-specific assessment. Dilute HCl is the source treatment for carbonate scale, not a universal sulphate/silicate treatment. This answer gives the textbook-supported hard-scale/removal pair without claiming a lecturer-approved superlative.
Short answer · 3State two reasons for the formation of scales in boilers? BL-2+
  • Heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate, depositing CaCO₃.
  • At high boiler temperatures the solubility of CaSO₄ decreases, causing a hard CaSO₄ deposit.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 4Mention the type of scales formed in low pressure boilers with necessary equations. BL-2+

Calcium carbonate is the principal low-pressure scale formed in boilers. Heating bicarbonate-containing water produces CaCO₃ precipitation. Magnesium bicarbonate can also give Mg(OH)₂ deposits.

\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
Short answer · 5In demineralization process, water is usually passed through cation exchanger and then through anion exchanger. Give reason. BL-3+

The cation bed removes Ca²⁺/Mg²⁺ and releases H⁺, producing acidic water. The following anion bed replaces anions with OH⁻, which combines with H⁺ to form water. Removing hardness cations first also avoids exposing them to the alkaline conditions an anion-first sequence could create, with precipitates fouling the bed.

\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
Short answer · 6Illustrate the reactions involved in calgon conditioning of hard water.BL-2+

Calgon is sodium hexametaphosphate, (NaPO₃)₆, also written Na₂[Na₄(PO₃)₆]. It complexes calcium into a soluble species, preventing calcium-salt scale. Reaction:

\(\mathrm{Na_2[Na_4(PO_3)_6]\rightleftharpoons2Na^+ +[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}}\)
\(\mathrm{2CaSO_4+[Na_4P_6O_{18}]^{2-}\rightarrow[Ca_2P_6O_{18}]^{2-}+2Na_2SO_4}\)
Short answer · 7State and explain the principle involved in reverse osmosis process? BL-1+

A pressure greater than the osmotic pressure is applied to the concentrated/brackish-water side of a semipermeable membrane. Water then moves from the concentrated side to the dilute/pure-water side, opposite to spontaneous osmosis, while most dissolved salts are retained.

Essay · 1Explain in detail how hardness of a water sample is estimated by EDTA method. BL-2+

Principle: disodium EDTA complexes Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺. At pH about 10, EBT forms a wine-red metal–indicator complex; EDTA displaces the indicator, giving a blue endpoint.

Total hardness: titrate 50 mL of the water sample in the same way; let the titre be V₂ mL.

\(\mathrm{[M-EBT]+EDTA\rightarrow[M-EDTA]+EBT}\quad\text{(wine red → blue)}\)
\(1\text{ mL EDTA}=\dfrac{50}{V_1}\text{ mg CaCO}_3\text{ equivalent}\)
\(H_{total}=\dfrac{V_2(50/V_1)\times1000}{50}=\dfrac{1000V_2}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)

\(H_{temporary}=\dfrac{1000(V_2-V_3)}{V_1}\text{ ppm}\)
Essay · 2Calculate the permanent hardness and temporary hardness of a water sample containing the following salts, Mg(HCO3)2 = 146 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2= 40.5mg/L, CaSO4=34mg/L, MgSO4=60mg/L. BL-3+

Use CaCO₃ equivalent = concentration × 100/molecular mass for the divalent salts.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:146\times\dfrac{100}{146}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:40.5\times\dfrac{100}{162}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:34\times\dfrac{100}{136}=25\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgSO_4}:60\times\dfrac{100}{120}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=100+25=125\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=25+50=75\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{total}=125+75=200\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 125 ppm; permanent hardness = 75 ppm; total = 200 ppm, all as CaCO₃.
Essay · 3Calculate temporary and total hardness present in 10,000 liters of a water sample containing the following impurities, Mg(HCO3)2 = 73 mg/L, Ca(HCO3)2= 162 mg/L, CaSO4=136mg/L, MgCl2=95mg/L. BL-3+

First find concentration as CaCO₃ equivalent. The 10,000 L volume does not change the ppm value; it is used only when calculating the total equivalent mass in the batch.

\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:73\times\dfrac{100}{146}=50\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:162\times\dfrac{100}{162}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{CaSO_4}:136\times\dfrac{100}{136}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(\mathrm{MgCl_2}:95\times\dfrac{100}{95}=100\text{ mg/L}\)
\(H_{temporary}=50+100=150\text{ ppm}\)
\(H_{permanent}=100+100=200\text{ ppm};\quad H_{total}=150+200=350\text{ ppm}\)
\(m_{temporary}=150\times10000=1500000\text{ mg}=1.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
\(m_{total}=350\times10000=3500000\text{ mg}=3.5\text{ kg as CaCO}_3\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 150 ppm and total hardness = 350 ppm; the 10,000 L batch contains 1.5 kg temporary and 3.5 kg total CaCO₃-equivalent hardness.
Essay · 5Define scales and sludges? How are they formed? Give their disadvantages and removal methods. BL-2+

As boiler water evaporates, dissolved salts concentrate and precipitate. Sludge is a soft, loose, slimy deposit, often accumulating in cooler/slow-flow parts. Scale is a hard adherent coating on the boiler wall.

  • CaCO₃ scale: heating decomposes calcium bicarbonate.
  • CaSO₄ scale: solubility decreases at high boiler temperatures.
  • Mg(OH)₂ deposits: hydrolysis/decomposition of magnesium salts.
  • Silica/silicate deposits: calcium/magnesium silicates stick firmly to the wall.
  • Consequences: poor heat transfer and fuel wastage, choked pipes/valves, lower efficiency, overheating, weakened tubes and possible failure or explosion.
  • Sludge removal: blow-down and brushing; reduce sludge formation by suitable feed-water softening.
  • Scale removal: scraper/wire brushing for loose deposits, suitable chemical treatment for the deposit and controlled thermal treatment where appropriate. A typical catalyst contains dilute HCl for carbonate scale and EDTA treatment for calcium/magnesium scales.
  • Prevention: external feed-water treatment plus internal conditioning and removal of accumulated sludge.
\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{heat}Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+2CO_2\uparrow}\)
FeatureScaleSludge
NatureHard, adherent depositSoft, loose, slimy precipitate
LocationFirm coating on heated boiler surfacesOften collects in cooler or low-flow regions and bends
FormationPrecipitation/deposition as dissolved salts concentrate or their solubility changesLoosely deposited precipitate from boiler-water impurities
RemovalMechanical cleaning, suitable chemical descaling or controlled thermal treatmentBlow-down and brushing
EffectHeat-transfer loss, overheating, reduced efficiency and possible tube failureHeat-transfer loss, choking and disturbed circulation
Essay · 4Write short note on boiler corrosion. BL-2+

Caustic embrittlement and boiler corrosion

Caustic embrittlement is intergranular cracking of stressed boiler steel associated with concentrated caustic alkali. Sodium carbonate can produce alkaline conditions: Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Concentration by evaporation in crevices/riveted joints can create aggressive conditions at stressed regions; hydrogen/stress effects depend on the service conditions.

The traditional course net scheme is Na₂CO₃ + H₂O → 2NaOH + CO₂ under the described boiler conditions. The aqueous carbonate hydrolysis step can instead be written Na₂CO₃ + H₂O ⇌ NaHCO₃ + NaOH. Both descriptions explain the development of caustic alkalinity; the net scheme is a simplified course model, not an unrestricted ambient-water conversion.

Prevention: use sodium phosphate rather than sodium carbonate; tannin/lignin or sodium sulphate are described as blocking hair-cracks and reducing caustic penetration. This explains why Na₂SO₄ controls caustic embrittlement; it is not neutralisation of NaOH. Typical historical Na₂SO₄:NaOH concentration ratios of 1:1, 2:1 and 3:1 for its stated pressure categories. Actual boiler dosing must follow the applicable engineered water-chemistry programme, rather than using those textbook ratios as a universal operating instruction.

Dissolved O₂ accelerates corrosion/pitting; remove it by deaeration and suitable scavengers: 2Na₂SO₃ + O₂ → 2Na₂SO₄, or N₂H₄ + O₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O (a historical toxic chemical requiring controlled handling). CO₂ dissolves to form carbonic acid: CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃, increasing condensate-line corrosion; remove/neutralise it through properly designed treatment. Hydrolysis of MgCl₂ can create acidic corrosive conditions. Prevent corrosion through feedwater treatment, deaeration and monitored chemistry, not improvised chemical dosing.

Part (a)

Scale is hard and adherent; sludge is soft, loose and slimy. The main differences are:
