\(\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2}:16.2\times\dfrac{100}{162}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)
\(\mathrm{Mg(HCO_3)_2}:14.6\times\dfrac{100}{146}=10\text{ mg/L as CaCO}_3\)

\(H_{total}=20+101=121\text{ ppm}\)
Final answer: Temporary hardness = 20 ppm; permanent hardness = 101 ppm; total hardness = 121 ppm.
Essay · 162 mgs, Mg (HCO3)2 = 14.6 mg/L, CaCl2= 111mgs, CaSO4=1.36mgs, turbidity =10mgs.Calculate the temporary permanent and total hardness of water in ppm. BL-3+

Essay · 10Explain the purification of water by Ion exchange process. Give the reactions involved. BL-3+

Ion-exchange resins are insoluble cross-linked polymers with exchangeable ions. A cation exchanger in the H⁺ form removes calcium, magnesium and other cations. An anion exchanger in the OH⁻ form removes anions. The released H⁺ and OH⁻ combine to form water.

Cation bed: hard water first passes through acidic resin, represented RH. Two H⁺ sites are needed to replace one divalent ion.

Anion bed: the acidic effluent passes through basic resin, represented R′OH. Anions are exchanged for hydroxyl ions.

Regenerate exhausted cation resin with dilute mineral acid and anion resin with dilute NaOH; rinse the beds before reuse. Turbid feed must be clarified to avoid resin-bed fouling.

\(\mathrm{2RH+CaCl_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+MgSO_4\rightarrow R_2Mg+H_2SO_4}\)
\(\mathrm{2RH+Ca(HCO_3)_2\rightarrow R_2Ca+2H_2CO_3}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}OH+HCl\rightarrow R^{\prime}Cl+H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{2R^{\prime}OH+H_2SO_4\rightarrow(R^{\prime})_2SO_4+2H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{H^+ +OH^-\rightarrow H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{R_2Ca+2HCl\rightarrow2RH+CaCl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{R^{\prime}Cl+NaOH\rightarrow R^{\prime}OH+NaCl}\)
Essay · 10How is natural water disinfected by BL-1
a) chlorine
b) bleaching powder
c) chloramine+
Part (a) Chlorine

Chlorine gas or its solution is mixed with water. Hydrolysis produces hypochlorous acid, which provides germicidal action. Allow sufficient contact and maintain an appropriate residual without excess chlorine causing objectionable taste/odour.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
\(\mathrm{HOCl\rightleftharpoons H^+ +OCl^-}\)
Part (b) Bleaching powder

The course represents bleaching powder as CaOCl₂. On treatment with water it makes chlorine available; chlorine then produces HOCl. It is convenient for small supplies but deteriorates during storage, can introduce calcium salts and must be used in a controlled amount.

\(\mathrm{CaOCl_2+H_2O\rightarrow Ca(OH)_2+Cl_2}\)
\(\mathrm{Cl_2+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HCl+HOCl}\)
Part (c) Chloramine

Chlorine reacts with ammonia to form chloramine. It gives a more persistent residual; its action involves slow availability of HOCl.

\(\mathrm{Cl_2+NH_3\rightarrow NH_2Cl+HCl}\)
\(\mathrm{NH_2Cl+H_2O\rightleftharpoons HOCl+NH_3}\)

Unit 2 · Electrochemistry and Corrosion

Unit 2 QB.docx.pdf

Short answer · 1Differentiate between an electrolytic cell and a galvanic cell. [BL-2]+
FeatureGalvanic cellElectrolytic cell
Driving processSpontaneous redox reactionExternal electrical supply drives a non-spontaneous reaction
Energy conversionChemical → electricalElectrical → chemical
Anode / cathode signsAnode negative; cathode positiveAnode positive; cathode negative
ExampleDaniell cellElectrolysis of molten NaCl
Short answer · 2What is electrode potential? Define standard electrode potential. [BL-1]+

Electrode potential is the potential developed when an electrode is in contact with a solution of its ions; it expresses its tendency to undergo oxidation or reduction. Standard electrode potential, E°, is measured relative to SHE under specified standard conditions. Use 25°C, 1 bar gas pressure and unit ion activity (approximately 1 M). SHE is assigned 0 V.

Short answer · 3Write down Nernst equation and outline its significance? [BL-2]+

For the stated reaction, \(E=E^\circ-\dfrac{RT}{nF}\ln Q\). At 298 K, \(E=E^\circ-\dfrac{0.0591}{n}\log_{10}Q\) V. Q is the activity-based reaction quotient; R is the gas constant, T temperature, n the electron number and F the Faraday constant.

The equation determines how potential changes with composition and temperature and supports cell-voltage and concentration-cell calculations. At equilibrium Q = K and the full-cell EMF is zero. R25 requires no derivation.

Short answer · 4Mention the conditions under which standard electrode potential is measured. [BL-2]+

Use the stated standard conditions: temperature 298 K (25°C), gaseous species at 1 bar, and dissolved ions at unit activity (represented approximately by 1 M). Measure against the Standard Hydrogen Electrode, assigned zero potential. Do not silently equate 1 bar with 1 atm.

Short answer · 5Why do we need a stable reference electrode in electrochemical cells? [BL-3]+

An isolated half-cell potential cannot be measured directly without a second electrode. A reference electrode supplies a known, stable potential. Measuring the cell EMF relative to it allows the unknown electrode potential to be calculated, with reproducible results.

Short answer · 6What characteristics make an ideal reference electrode? [BL-3]+

An ideal reference electrode has a stable, well-defined and reproducible potential; is non-polarizable under small measurement currents; is chemically compatible with the test solution; and is easy to prepare, maintain and handle. Its electrolyte composition and temperature should remain controlled.

Short answer · 7State the working principle of the Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE). [BL-2]+

Platinum black catalyses the reversible equilibrium \(2\mathrm H^++2\mathrm e^-\rightleftharpoons\mathrm H_2\). Hydrogen is passed over the platinum surface in contact with hydrogen ions at the specified standard conditions. SHE can act as an anode (hydrogen oxidation) or a cathode (hydrogen-ion reduction); its standard reference potential is defined as zero.

Short answer · 8Why is SHE called a primary reference electrode? [BL-2]+

SHE is the primary reference because the standard hydrogen electrode is assigned E° = 0 V by convention. Other standard electrode potentials are determined relative to that reference, establishing the hydrogen scale. Secondary references such as calomel are calibrated against it.

Short answer · 9Mention one advantage and one limitation of the Calomel electrode. [BL-2]+

Advantage: it provides a stable, reproducible potential and is easier to set up and maintain than a hydrogen-gas electrode. Limitation: it contains toxic mercury; its potential also depends on temperature and KCl concentration.

Short answer · 10What is the function of an ion-selective electrode? [BL-2]+

An ISE develops a membrane potential that responds preferentially to the activity of a selected ion in a sample. Measured against a reference electrode and calibrated with standards, that potential is used to estimate the target-ion activity/concentration. The glass electrode is selective for H⁺ and is used for pH measurement.

Short answer · 11Write the electrode reaction of the Calomel electrode. [BL-1]+

The reduction half-reaction is \(\mathrm{Hg}_2\mathrm{Cl}_2(s)+2\mathrm e^-\rightleftharpoons2\mathrm{Hg}(l)+2\mathrm{Cl}^-(aq)\). The reverse reaction applies when the electrode acts as an anode.

The balanced product is neutral Hg(l), not Hg⁺.

Short answer · 12What is a glass electrode? In which scenarios is it preferred? [BL-2]+

A glass electrode is an H⁺-selective electrode with a thin pH-sensitive glass membrane and an internal reference system. A combined assembly includes both measuring and reference systems. It is preferred for routine pH measurement in laboratories, industrial processes, environmental samples, biological fluids and food analysis, including coloured or turbid samples.

Short answer · 13In a given electrochemical experiment, how would you decide which reference electrode to use? [BL-3]+

Choose according to the measurement, required accuracy, sample compatibility and convenience. SHE provides the primary reference for standard-potential work but requires carefully controlled hydrogen and a clean platinum surface. Calomel is a convenient secondary reference for routine compatible electrochemical measurements. For pH, use a glass sensing electrode with a suitable reference, commonly combined in one probe.

The glass membrane is the pH-sensitive measuring element; the combined assembly also contains a reference system.

Short answer · 14Enumerate the effects of corrosion. [BL-1]+

Corrosion causes plant shutdowns; leakage and loss of products from tanks, pipelines and containers; reduced efficiency; contamination of chemicals, medicines, dyes and foods; environmental damage; and hazards in installations such as nuclear plants. It also weakens engineering components.

Short answer · 15Differentiate between dry corrosion and wet corrosion. [BL-3]+
FeatureDry corrosionWet corrosion
MechanismDirect chemical attack, e.g. by gasesCoupled anodic oxidation and cathodic reduction
EnvironmentDry conditions; may involve hot gases or liquid metalsMoisture or a conducting aqueous electrolyte
Controlling featuresNature and protectiveness of the surface filmAnodic/cathodic areas, electrolyte and cathodic reaction
ExampleOxidation of a metal in oxygenRusting of iron in moist air
Short answer · 16What is Pilling-Bedworth rule? Give its significance. [BL-2]+

Pilling–Bedworth ratio is the volume of oxide formed divided by the volume of metal consumed. An oxide with insufficient volume cannot continuously cover the metal; an adherent non-porous film can reduce oxygen access.

The simple volume-ratio rule is a guideline, but real protection also depends on film integrity, stress, transport and environment. Large ratios can produce buckling, so “larger always means less corrosion” is not a universal law.

Short answer · 17The specific volume ratios of Ni, Cr and W are 1.6, 2.0 and 3.6 respectively. Name the metal which undergoes least oxidation corrosion. Give the reason.+

Using the simplified volume-ratio rule, choose W: its ratio 3.6 exceeds Cr (2.0) and Ni (1.6). Actual protection also depends on film integrity and environment.

Qualification: the numerical ratio alone does not determine a real oxidation-rate ranking. Film integrity and stress matter; high expansion can cause oxide buckling. Thus the course answer must not be generalized to all environments.

Verification of the physical qualification: Yankova et al., Nature Communications (2023), introduction.

Final answer: W, under the supplied handout’s simplified model.
Short answer · 18Corrosion of water filled steel tanks occurs below the water level. Give reasons. [BL-3]+

Oxygen concentration is lower just below the waterline than at the well-aerated surface. A differential-aeration cell forms: the less-aerated region is anodic and dissolves, while the more-aerated region is cathodic. Attack is therefore concentrated just beneath the water meniscus.

Short answer · 19Compare differential aeration corrosion and differential metal corrosion. [BL-2]+
FeatureDifferential aerationDifferential metal
CauseUnequal oxygen access over a metal surfaceElectrical contact between dissimilar metals in a corrosive medium
AnodeLess oxygenated regionMore active metal of the couple
CathodeMore oxygenated regionLess active metal of the couple
ExampleWaterline or under-deposit corrosionIron in contact with copper
Short answer · 20The metal under the water drop undergoes accelerated corrosion. Why? [BL-3]+

The edge of a water drop receives oxygen more readily than its centre. The oxygen-poor central region becomes anodic and dissolves; the oxygen-rich edge supports the cathodic oxygen-reduction reaction. This localized differential-aeration cell accelerates attack beneath the drop.

Short answer · 21Nuts and bolts should be made of the same metal. Why? [BL-3]+

Dissimilar metals in electrical contact and exposed to moisture can form a galvanic couple. The more active member becomes the anode and corrodes. Using the same metal avoids the dissimilar-metal potential difference and reduces this cause of corrosion.

Short answer · 22Small anodic areas result in intense corrosion. Give the reason. [BL-2]+

A large cathode can demand a substantial total anodic current. If dissolution is concentrated on a small anode, the anodic current density (current per unit area) becomes high. Metal loss per unit anodic area therefore increases; a small steel pipe connected to a large copper tank is the supplied example.

Short answer · 23Passivity results in more corrosion resistance. Why? [BL-2]+

Passivity is unusually high corrosion resistance caused by a thin, adherent protective surface film. The film separates the metal from the environment and slows further reaction. The notes describe passive films as insoluble, non-porous and capable of reforming under suitable oxidizing conditions.

Short answer · 24Why does corrosion occur in steel pipe connected to copper plumbing? [BL-3]+

Steel and copper in contact through plumbing water form a galvanic couple. Iron is the more active metal and becomes the anode, while copper acts as cathode. Iron dissolves as Fe²⁺. Attack is especially severe if a small steel area is connected to a much larger copper area.

Short answer · 25Iron corrodes faster than Al, even though iron is placed below Al in the galvanic series. Why? [BL-3]+

Position in a galvanic series alone does not determine the observed rate. Aluminium develops an adherent protective oxide film and becomes passive. Ordinary rust on iron does not provide comparable durable protection, so iron can corrode faster even though aluminium is more active.

Short answer · 26Why does the rate of metallic corrosion increase with the increase in temperature. [BL-2]+

The supplied notes explain that increasing temperature increases reaction rates and diffusion rates, generally accelerating corrosion under the stated conditions. The actual rate still depends on the metal, electrolyte and protective film.

Short answer · 27Impure metal corrodes faster than pure metal under identical conditions. Give the reason. [BL-3]+

Impurities create heterogeneity and local electrochemical cells. Anodic portions of the metal dissolve while cathodic impurity regions support reduction. The resulting local galvanic action can make an impure metal corrode faster than a pure specimen under otherwise identical conditions.

Short answer · 28Iron corrodes under drops of salt solution. Give the reason. [BL-3]+

A salt-solution drop supplies a conducting electrolyte and unequal oxygen access. The less-aerated region under the drop is anodic; the better-aerated region is cathodic. Iron dissolves at the anode and oxygen reduction occurs at the cathode, producing localized corrosion.

Short answer · 29A piece of impure zinc and pure zinc are placed in a salt solution. Which will metal corrode faster? Give the reason. [BL-3]+

Impure zinc corrodes faster under the stated identical conditions. Impurities such as Fe or Pb form local cathodic sites with zinc as the anodic metal; zinc dissolution is accelerated by these small electrochemical cells. Pure zinc has fewer such heterogeneities.

Final answer: Impure zinc corrodes faster under the stated conditions.
Short answer · 30What is cathodic protection? Give an example of a sacrificial anode. [BL-1]+

Cathodic protection makes the structure to be protected the cathode of an electrochemical system, suppressing its anodic dissolution. It may use a more active sacrificial metal or an external DC source. Zinc, magnesium or aluminium can serve as sacrificial anodes; zinc blocks attached to a steel ship hull are a supplied example.

Short answer · 31Compare sacrificial anode method with impressed current cathodic protection. [BL-3]+
FeatureSacrificial anodeImpressed current
Current sourceGalvanic action of a more active metalExternal DC power supply / rectifier
AnodeMg, Zn, Al or suitable alloy; consumed and replacedAuxiliary anode, e.g. graphite or suitable high-silicon iron
Protected structureCathodeConnected as cathode to the supply negative terminal
Control / scaleLimited driving potential; monitor anode consumptionAdjustable output; suited to larger structures
MaintenanceReplace consumed anodesMaintain power supply, wiring and protection potential
Short answer · 32How is cathodic protection of iron different from its galvanization. [BL-3]+

Cathodic protection describes the electrochemical method of making iron the cathode, either by an attached sacrificial anode or by impressed current. Galvanization is the application of a zinc coating to iron: the intact coating acts as a barrier, and zinc can additionally provide sacrificial protection where iron is exposed. Thus galvanization is a coating process with a sacrificial action, while cathodic protection is the broader electrochemical protection principle.

Short answer · 33Zinc-coated iron article is protected from rusting, even when the coating is cracked. Explain. [BL-3]+

When a crack exposes iron to moisture, zinc and iron remain electrically connected. Zinc is more active and dissolves as the sacrificial anode; iron is the cathode and is protected while sufficient connected zinc remains. This extends protection beyond the coating’s physical barrier.

Short answer · 34Why is coating of zinc on iron called sacrificial anode? [BL-3]+

Zinc is more active than iron in the supplied galvanic example. In contact with iron and an electrolyte, zinc oxidizes preferentially and supplies electrons, making iron cathodic. Zinc is consumed to protect the iron, hence the term sacrificial anode.

Essay · 1Explain the construction and working of an electrochemical (galvanic) cell with a neat, labelled diagram.+

A galvanic cell converts the chemical energy of a spontaneous redox reaction into electrical energy. In the Daniell cell, a zinc electrode is immersed in ZnSO₄ solution and a copper electrode in CuSO₄ solution. A salt bridge containing an inert electrolyte joins the half-cells, and an external wire with a voltmeter/load joins the metal electrodes.

The cell notation is \(\mathrm{Zn}|\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}(1\,\mathrm M)||\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}(1\,\mathrm M)|\mathrm{Cu}\). Zinc is the negative anode and copper the positive cathode.

At the anode: \(\mathrm{Zn}\rightarrow\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}+2\mathrm e^-\). At the cathode: \(\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}+2\mathrm e^-\rightarrow\mathrm{Cu}\). Adding the half-reactions gives \(\mathrm{Zn}+\mathrm{Cu}^{2+}\rightarrow\mathrm{Zn}^{2+}+\mathrm{Cu}\).

Electrons pass through the external wire from zinc to copper. The salt bridge carries ionic current and maintains charge balance: anions move towards the zinc half-cell as Zn²⁺ forms, and cations towards the copper half-cell as Cu²⁺ is consumed. Oxidation and reduction remain spatially separated but electrically coupled.

The labelled Daniell-cell diagram below shows the separated half-cells, salt bridge and electron path.

Final answer: Chemical → electrical energy; zinc oxidizes, copper ions reduce, and electrons flow externally from Zn to Cu.
Essay · 2Compare and contrast the construction and working of the Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE) and Calomel electrode. Mention their uses and limitations.+
AspectSHECalomel electrode
ConstructionPlatinized platinum in a hydrogen-ion solution; pure H₂ bubbled at the stated reference pressureMercury in contact with Hg₂Cl₂ paste and KCl solution of specified concentration; porous junction connects to the test solution
Reduction reaction2H⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ H₂Hg₂Cl₂ + 2e⁻ ⇌ 2Hg + 2Cl⁻
PotentialAssigned E° = 0 V under reference conditionsKnown secondary-reference potential; depends on KCl concentration and temperature
WorkingReversible hydrogen-ion/hydrogen equilibrium on catalytic PtReversible Hg/Hg₂Cl₂/Cl⁻ equilibrium
AdvantagesPrimary standard; reproducible with carefully controlled conditionsConvenient, portable, stable and comparatively easy to maintain
LimitationsNeeds pure hydrogen, clean platinum and controlled conditions; costly and inconvenient for routine useContains mercury; temperature/KCl concentration matter; incompatible with some samples
UsesEstablishing standard potentials, calibrating secondary electrodesRoutine potential measurements, potentiometry and reference in pH measurements

For each electrode, identify the metal/contact, electrolyte, gas inlet where applicable, and junction to the other half-cell in its original labelled diagram. Both source diagrams are included below; the SHE source-label correction is stated in its caption.

For saturated KCl at 25°C, use +0.2422 V versus SHE as in the table and existing book record. The balanced product is neutral Hg(l).

Essay · 3Explain the construction, working, and applications of a Combined Glass Electrode as an ion-selective electrode./ Describe how a Combined Glass Electrode functions as an Ion-Selective Electrode (ISE).+

A combined glass electrode is an H⁺-selective pH sensor that contains the glass measuring system and a reference system in one convenient assembly.

Construction: the thin glass bulb is made from pH-sensitive glass. The supplied notes specify 72% SiO₂, 22% Na₂O and 6% CaO. An internal Ag/AgCl electrode contacts a fixed internal solution (0.1 N HCl in the notes). A second reference compartment contains KCl and contacts the sample through a porous junction. The original diagram labels both reference systems and the thin membrane.

Working: hydration of the glass surface produces an ion-exchange layer. H⁺ interaction at the inner and outer surfaces gives a membrane potential related to the difference in hydrogen-ion activity. The internal solution is held fixed, so changes in sample pH change the measured voltage relative to the reference.

Calibrate with buffer solutions, immerse the membrane and reference junction in the sample, and measure with a high-impedance pH meter. Calibration establishes the practical voltage offset and response. Do not arbitrarily set the combined probe’s offset to zero.

Advantages: compact single assembly, convenient routine use and H⁺ selectivity; it can be used with coloured/turbid samples. Limitations: fragile membrane, calibration and temperature dependence, ageing and interfering-ion response; the source specifically warns about alkaline error above pH 12.

Applications include laboratory pH, industrial process control, environmental monitoring, biological fluids and food/beverage analysis. The original combined-probe diagram and glass-membrane illustration are included below.

With EG = EG° + 0.0592 pH in the existing book convention, E(SCE) − EG has slope −0.0592 V per pH at 25°C. The offset must be calibrated.

Essay · 4Analyse how different reference electrodes help in determining the electrode potential of unknown electrodes.+

A single half-cell potential is measured relative to a second, known electrode. Connect the unknown half-cell and reference half-cell through a suitable ionic junction and connect the electrical leads to measure the cell EMF with negligible current.

Keep the cell orientation and reduction-potential convention explicit: \(E_{\mathrm{cell}}=E_{\mathrm{right}}-E_{\mathrm{left}}\). If the reference is on the left, \(E_{\mathrm{unknown}}=E_{\mathrm{cell}}+E_{\mathrm{reference}}\); if it is on the right, \(E_{\mathrm{unknown}}=E_{\mathrm{reference}}-E_{\mathrm{cell}}\).

With SHE, use the specified reference conditions and E° = 0 V for standard-potential determination. Its reproducibility defines the potential scale, but gas purity, platinum cleanliness and control of pressure/activity make routine use demanding.

With calomel, use its known potential for the actual KCl concentration and temperature. It is convenient for routine measurements, but use the correct concentration-dependent reference value and compatible sample chemistry.

An unknown metal electrode is a working electrode; the glass membrane is instead an ion-selective measuring element for pH. In a combined probe it is measured against its incorporated reference. A stable reference allows changes in the measured voltage to be attributed to the working/sensing electrode rather than reference drift.

Final answer: Calculate the unknown potential from the measured cell EMF, the known reference potential and the explicitly recorded cell orientation.
Essay · 5Explain the mechanism involved in the rusting of iron by the wet theory of corrosion./ Discuss in detail the mechanism of electrochemical corrosion theory with the evolution of hydrogen and absorption of oxygen. [BL-2]+

Wet corrosion is electrochemical attack in a conducting aqueous or moist environment. Different metals, impurities or unequal oxygen access create anodic and cathodic regions. Metal dissolution occurs at the anode; reduction occurs at the cathode.

For iron the anodic reaction is \(\mathrm{Fe}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}+2\mathrm e^-\). Electrons pass through the metal to cathodic regions; ionic conduction through the electrolyte completes the cell.

In an acidic environment, the cathodic reaction is hydrogen evolution: \(2\mathrm H^++2\mathrm e^-\rightarrow\mathrm H_2\uparrow\). Adding the half-reactions gives \(\mathrm{Fe}+2\mathrm H^+\rightarrow\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}+\mathrm H_2\uparrow\).

In neutral/slightly alkaline aerated water, oxygen reduction occurs: \(\dfrac12\mathrm O_2+\mathrm H_2\mathrm O+2\mathrm e^-\rightarrow2\mathrm{OH}^-\). Ferrous ions then precipitate: \(\mathrm{Fe}^{2+}+2\mathrm{OH}^-\rightarrow\mathrm{Fe(OH)}_2\).

Further oxidation gives \(4\mathrm{Fe(OH)}_2+\mathrm O_2+2\mathrm H_2\mathrm O\rightarrow4\mathrm{Fe(OH)}_3\). Rust is hydrated ferric oxide/oxyhydroxide material; the supplied notes also write hydrated Fe₂O₃. It does not form a durable protective barrier.

Hydrogen evolution and oxygen absorption are alternative cathodic mechanisms depending on environment. The anodic region loses iron in both cases; the cathodic region supports reduction.

Essay · 6a) Differentiate between dry corrosion and wet corrosion. [BL-2]b) Explain differential metal (Galvanic) corrosion with an example. [BL-2]+
(a) Dry corrosion versus wet corrosion
FeatureDry corrosionWet corrosion
MechanismDirect chemical attack, e.g. by gasesCoupled anodic oxidation and cathodic reduction
EnvironmentDry conditions; may involve hot gases or liquid metalsMoisture or a conducting aqueous electrolyte
Controlling featuresNature and protectiveness of the surface filmAnodic/cathodic areas, electrolyte and cathodic reaction
ExampleOxidation of a metal in oxygenRusting of iron in moist air
(b) Differential metal (galvanic) corrosion

When dissimilar metals are electrically connected in a conducting corrosive environment, they form a galvanic cell. The more active metal becomes the anode and dissolves; the less active metal supports cathodic reduction.

For iron in contact with zinc, zinc becomes the anode and corrodes preferentially, protecting iron. For steel in contact with copper, iron is anodic and can suffer accelerated attack. Greater potential separation and a large cathode/small anode make the anodic attack more severe.

Reduce galvanic attack by avoiding unnecessary dissimilar-metal contact or electrically insulating the metals. A sacrificial anode deliberately uses the same mechanism to protect the desired structure.

Essay · 7Explain how the following factors affect the rate of corrosion. [BL-2]a) passivityb) position of the metalc) relative areas of anode and cathoded) pH+
(a) Passivity

A thin adherent, non-porous surface film can make an active metal highly corrosion-resistant. The notes use aluminium and chromium-containing alloys as examples. The film must remain stable in the environment; passivity can be lost when the film is damaged or conditions change.

(b) Position of the metal

For dissimilar metals in electrical contact in an electrolyte, the more active metal is anodic. A greater separation in the relevant galvanic series generally increases the driving force for attack of that member. Actual behaviour also depends on film formation and environment.

(c) Relative anode and cathode areas

A small anode connected to a large cathode has a high anodic current density and rapid local metal loss. The example is a small steel pipe connected to a large copper tank. Avoid this area combination when designing galvanic couples.

(d) pH

The supplied notes state that acidic media are generally more corrosive to metals readily attacked by acid. Increasing pH can reduce their attack, but behaviour depends on the metal. The given example is zinc: rapid corrosion below pH 5 and minimum corrosion around pH 11 in the stated source.

Essay · Additional (a)a) Discuss about chemical (Dry) Corrosion involving: Oxidation corrosion, Corrosion by other gases, Liquid metal corrosion+
1 Oxidation corrosion

Oxygen directly attacks the metal to form an oxide. The resulting film controls further attack: a stable adherent non-porous film can protect, whereas a porous or volatile film permits continued attack. The notes list stable, unstable, volatile and porous oxide layers, and introduce the Pilling–Bedworth volume ratio.

Retain the source’s oxide examples and W/Cr/Ni illustration as attributed source material. The general formula and high-volume-ratio conclusion have qualifications recorded in the notes.

2 Corrosion by other gases

Gases such as SO₂, Cl₂, H₂S, F₂ and CO₂ may attack metals directly. The chemical affinity of the gas for the metal and the film’s nature determine the extent of attack. A protective non-porous product can limit reaction; a porous or volatile product permits deeper attack. The examples are AgCl formed by chlorine on silver and SnCl₄ formed by chlorine on tin.

3 Liquid-metal corrosion

At high temperatures, flowing liquid metal can attack a solid metal/alloy. The supplied notes identify dissolution of the solid in the liquid metal and penetration of liquid metal into the solid as mechanisms. Both weaken the solid; nuclear-power equipment is the supplied application context.

Essay · 8What is differential aeration corrosion? Explain the following with an example. [BL-2]a) water line corrosionb) pitting corrosion.+

Differential-aeration corrosion arises from unequal oxygen concentration at different parts of the same metal surface. Less-aerated regions become anodic and dissolve; more-aerated regions become cathodic and support oxygen reduction.

(a) Waterline corrosion

In a partially filled steel tank, oxygen reaches the surface/meniscus region more easily than the submerged region just beneath it. The less-aerated submerged area becomes anodic. Attack is concentrated just below the waterline; the more-aerated region supports cathodic reduction. The examples include storage tanks and ships.

(b) Pitting corrosion

Under a dust particle, deposit or damaged protective film, oxygen access is restricted. The covered small region becomes an anode while the larger exposed surface is cathodic. Metal dissolves locally and a pit develops.

The notes give machinery parts, oil-tank bottoms and chloride-exposed stainless steel, titanium or aluminium alloys as examples. Remove deposits, keep surfaces clean/smooth, and design structures to reduce crevices and deposit accumulation. The original waterline and pitting figures are included below.

Essay · 9Explain cathodic protection method for controlling corrosion./ Define cathodic protection. Explain sacrificial anodic protection and impressed current cathodic protection.+

Cathodic protection suppresses corrosion of the structure by making it the cathode of an electrochemical system. The two supplied methods are sacrificial-anode protection and impressed-current protection.

1 Sacrificial-anode method

Connect the steel structure to a more active metal such as Mg, Zn or Al in the same electrolyte. The added metal oxidizes and supplies electrons; the steel becomes cathodic and is protected. Replace the anode when consumed.

Examples: zinc blocks on steel ship hulls and anodes for buried pipelines, cables, tanks and marine structures. Advantages include simple operation without an external DC source and relatively low installation/operating cost. Limitations include finite driving potential, anode consumption and replacement/monitoring.

The source composite below preserves the three sacrificial-protection arrangements.

2 Impressed-current method

Use an external DC supply or rectifier. Connect its negative terminal to the structure and positive terminal to the auxiliary anode in the electrolyte. The applied current makes the structure cathodic, opposing its anodic dissolution.

The notes list graphite, high-silicon iron and other suitable auxiliary anodes. Backfill may improve electrical contact with soil. Applications include larger water/oil pipelines, tanks and other extensive steel structures.

The output can be controlled and larger structures protected, but an external power supply, wiring, monitoring and maintenance are necessary. Initial cost and vulnerability of anode leads are limitations. The original pipeline/rectifier diagram is included below; its faint lines are a source limitation.

Bare structures can receive cathodic protection. Limited driving voltage, current demand, electrolyte resistance and anode consumption constrain sacrificial systems; the source limitations have been corrected with originals retained in the ledger.

Short answer · 1Enumerate the effects of corrosion. BL-1+

Corrosion causes plant shutdowns; leakage and loss of products from tanks, pipelines and containers; reduced efficiency; contamination of chemicals, medicines, dyes and foods; environmental damage; and hazards in installations such as nuclear plants. It also weakens engineering components.

Short answer · 3What is Pilling-Bedworth rule? Give its significance. BL-1+

Pilling–Bedworth ratio is the volume of oxide formed divided by the volume of metal consumed. An oxide with insufficient volume cannot continuously cover the metal; an adherent non-porous film can reduce oxygen access.

The simple volume-ratio rule is a guideline, but real protection also depends on film integrity, stress, transport and environment. Large ratios can produce buckling, so “larger always means less corrosion” is not a universal law.

Short answer · 4Corrosion of water filled steel tanks occurs below the water level. Give reasons. BL-3+

Oxygen concentration is lower just below the waterline than at the well-aerated surface. A differential-aeration cell forms: the less-aerated region is anodic and dissolves, while the more-aerated region is cathodic. Attack is therefore concentrated just beneath the water meniscus.

Short answer · 6The metal under water drop undergoes accelerated corrosion. Why? BL-3+

Galvanic and differential-aeration corrosion

Differential-aeration corrosion can occur on one metal where oxygen access differs. The oxygen-poor region becomes anodic relative to the oxygen-rich region. Under a water drop, the centre is less aerated and iron dissolves there; the rim supports oxygen reduction. In a partly filled tank, attack is concentrated just below the waterline because that region is less oxygenated than the near-surface cathodic area.

Short answer · 7Nuts and bolts are to be made of same metal. Justify. BL-3+

Galvanic and differential-aeration corrosion

Galvanic (differential-metal) corrosion occurs when dissimilar metals are electrically connected in an electrolyte. The more active member becomes the anode and dissolves; the more noble member supports cathodic reduction. For a steel–copper connection in typical aerated water, steel is anodic: Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻, while oxygen reduction occurs preferentially on copper. Use compatible metals, electrical isolation and suitable coatings to reduce attack. Nuts and bolts of the same metal avoid creating such a couple.

Short answer · 8Small anodic area results in intense corrosion. Give reason. BL-2+

Factors controlling corrosion

Impurities may form microgalvanic cathodes, so impure zinc commonly corrodes faster than pure zinc in the same salt solution. A small anode connected to a large cathode suffers high anodic current density (current/anode area), causing intense local attack. A large anode/small cathode is usually less severe per unit anodic area.

Short answer · 9Passivity results in more corrosion resistance. Why? BL-2+

Passivity is unusually high corrosion resistance caused by a thin, adherent protective surface film. The film separates the metal from the environment and slows further reaction. The notes describe passive films as insoluble, non-porous and capable of reforming under suitable oxidizing conditions.

Short answer · 10Iron corrodes faster than Al, even though iron is placed below Al in the galvanic series. Why? BL-3+

Position in a galvanic series alone does not determine the observed rate. Aluminium develops an adherent protective oxide film and becomes passive. Ordinary rust on iron does not provide comparable durable protection, so iron can corrode faster even though aluminium is more active.

Short answer · 11Why the rate of metallic corrosion increases with increase in temperature. BL-2+

Factors controlling corrosion

Passivity is formation of a thin, adherent protective film. Aluminium can resist corrosion despite its active position because Al₂O₃ limits further attack, while ordinary iron rust is porous. Higher temperature commonly accelerates reactions/diffusion, but dissolved-oxygen changes and film formation can alter the trend. Acidic pH often accelerates dissolution/hydrogen evolution; amphoteric metals may also be attacked in strong alkali.

Short answer · 12Galvanization of iron is preferred to tinning, justify your answer. BL-3+

Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

Galvanising coats iron/steel with zinc. Intact zinc is a barrier; if scratched in a suitable electrolyte, zinc remains more active and dissolves sacrificially, protecting exposed iron. Tinning coats with tin, a more noble metal: intact tin is a barrier, but a scratch can create an iron-anode/tin-cathode couple and accelerate local steel attack. Thus galvanising offers sacrificial protection after small coating damage, unlike ordinary tinning.

Short answer · 13What is cathodic protection? Give an example of a sacrificial anode. BL-1+

Cathodic protection makes the structure to be protected the cathode of an electrochemical system, suppressing its anodic dissolution. It may use a more active sacrificial metal or an external DC source. Zinc, magnesium or aluminium can serve as sacrificial anodes; zinc blocks attached to a steel ship hull are a supplied example.

Short answer · 14Brass utensils are usually tinned. Give reasons. BL-2+

Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

The traditional reason for tinning brass utensils is to separate food from copper/zinc-containing brass. Zinc coatings can dissolve in acidic foods and contaminate them, so galvanised vessels are unsuitable for acidic food storage/cooking. Tin linings must be intact and appropriate for food use; no blanket claim of safety for every food, temperature or damaged lining is justified.

Short answer · 15Identify the reasons for not recommending galvanized utensils for cooking. BL-3+

Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

The traditional reason for tinning brass utensils is to separate food from copper/zinc-containing brass. Zinc coatings can dissolve in acidic foods and contaminate them, so galvanised vessels are unsuitable for acidic food storage/cooking. Tin linings must be intact and appropriate for food use; no blanket claim of safety for every food, temperature or damaged lining is justified.

Short answer · 20How is cathodic protection of iron different from its galvanization. BL-3+

Cathodic protection describes the electrochemical method of making iron the cathode, either by an attached sacrificial anode or by impressed current. Galvanization is the application of a zinc coating to iron: the intact coating acts as a barrier, and zinc can additionally provide sacrificial protection where iron is exposed. Thus galvanization is a coating process with a sacrificial action, while cathodic protection is the broader electrochemical protection principle.

Short answer · 21Why does corrosion occur in steel pipe connected to copper plumbing? BL-3+

Steel and copper in contact through plumbing water form a galvanic couple. Iron is the more active metal and becomes the anode, while copper acts as cathode. Iron dissolves as Fe²⁺. Attack is especially severe if a small steel area is connected to a much larger copper area.

Short answer · 22Why are galvanized utensils not used for storage of food stuff? BL-2+

Tin linings must be intact and appropriate for food use; no blanket claim of safety for every food, temperature or damaged lining is justified.

Short answer · 23Zinc-coated iron article is protected from rusting, even when the coating is cracked. Explain. BL-3+

When a crack exposes iron to moisture, zinc and iron remain electrically connected. Zinc is more active and dissolves as the sacrificial anode; iron is the cathode and is protected while sufficient connected zinc remains. This extends protection beyond the coating’s physical barrier.

Short answer · 25List out the factors which affect corrosion? BL-1+

Factors controlling corrosion

Metal factors: position in the relevant galvanic series/electrode tendency, purity, passive film, oxide-film character, stress and relative anode/cathode area. Environmental factors: moisture, oxygen distribution, pH, dissolved salts/conductivity, temperature and corrosive gases. The actual environment and kinetics matter; a standard-potential ranking alone cannot predict every rate.

Essay · 1Explain the mechanism involved in the rusting of iron with the help of wet corrosion theory. BL-1 (OR)+

Electrochemical corrosion and rusting of iron

Corrosion is deterioration of a material by reaction with its environment. Effects include loss of metal/strength, leakage and equipment failure, contamination, poor appearance and replacement/maintenance costs. Dry corrosion is direct chemical attack (often gas/metal reaction at elevated temperature); wet corrosion involves anodic/cathodic reactions connected through a metal and an electrolyte.

For iron in aerated neutral water: anode Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻; cathode O₂ + 2H₂O + 4e⁻ → 4OH⁻. Ferrous ions form Fe(OH)₂: Fe²⁺ + 2OH⁻ → Fe(OH)₂. Further oxidation: 4Fe(OH)₂ + O₂ + 2H₂O → 4Fe(OH)₃. Subsequent dehydration/ageing gives hydrated ferric oxide, Fe₂O₃·xH₂O (rust). The porous rust is not an effective continuous barrier.

In deaerated acidic solution, cathodic hydrogen evolution can occur: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂. For iron, combine this with Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻ to obtain Fe + 2H⁺ → Fe²⁺ + H₂. State the environment when choosing the cathodic reaction; do not add both cathodic reactions indiscriminately.

Essay · 2Discuss in detail the mechanism of electrochemical corrosion theory with the evolution of hydrogen and absorption of oxygen with chemical equations. BL-2+

Essay · 3
a) List out the differences between dry corrosion and wet corrosion. BL-2
b) What is differential metal corrosion? Explain galvanic corrosion with example. BL-2+

(a) Electrochemical corrosion and rusting of iron

Corrosion is deterioration of a material by reaction with its environment. Effects include loss of metal/strength, leakage and equipment failure, contamination, poor appearance and replacement/maintenance costs. Dry corrosion is direct chemical attack (often gas/metal reaction at elevated temperature); wet corrosion involves anodic/cathodic reactions connected through a metal and an electrolyte.

For iron in aerated neutral water: anode Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻; cathode O₂ + 2H₂O + 4e⁻ → 4OH⁻. Ferrous ions form Fe(OH)₂: Fe²⁺ + 2OH⁻ → Fe(OH)₂. Further oxidation: 4Fe(OH)₂ + O₂ + 2H₂O → 4Fe(OH)₃. Subsequent dehydration/ageing gives hydrated ferric oxide, Fe₂O₃·xH₂O (rust). The porous rust is not an effective continuous barrier.

In deaerated acidic solution, cathodic hydrogen evolution can occur: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂. For iron, combine this with Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻ to obtain Fe + 2H⁺ → Fe²⁺ + H₂. State the environment when choosing the cathodic reaction; do not add both cathodic reactions indiscriminately.

(b) Galvanic and differential-aeration corrosion

Galvanic (differential-metal) corrosion occurs when dissimilar metals are electrically connected in an electrolyte. The more active member becomes the anode and dissolves; the more noble member supports cathodic reduction. For a steel–copper connection in typical aerated water, steel is anodic: Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻, while oxygen reduction occurs preferentially on copper. Use compatible metals, electrical isolation and suitable coatings to reduce attack. Nuts and bolts of the same metal avoid creating such a couple.

Differential-aeration corrosion can occur on one metal where oxygen access differs. The oxygen-poor region becomes anodic relative to the oxygen-rich region. Under a water drop, the centre is less aerated and iron dissolves there; the rim supports oxygen reduction. In a partly filled tank, attack is concentrated just below the waterline because that region is less oxygenated than the near-surface cathodic area.

Pitting begins at local passive-film breakdown/occluded regions. A small active pit is coupled to a large surrounding cathodic surface, concentrating anodic current. Restricted transport and chloride-rich acidic conditions can sustain the pit. Chloride solution also increases electrolyte conductivity and can disrupt passive films; not every salt affects every metal equally.

Essay · 7
a) Explain cathodic protection method for controlling corrosion. BL-2
b) Differentiate between galvanising and tinning. BL-2+

(a) Cathodic protection

Cathodic protection suppresses metal dissolution by making the structure the cathode. In the sacrificial-anode method, connect a more active metal such as Mg, Zn or a suitable Al alloy to the steel in the electrolyte. The sacrificial metal oxidises (for example Zn → Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻) and supplies electrons to the steel. Inspect and replace consumed anodes. No external DC supply is needed, but available driving voltage/current is limited.

In impressed-current protection, connect the structure to the negative terminal of a DC source and a suitable auxiliary anode to its positive terminal. Current passes through the electrolyte from the auxiliary anode to the structure; the structure is polarised cathodically. It suits large pipelines, tanks and marine structures, with controllable output. It requires power, monitoring and care against overprotection/interference.

Sacrificial anodes are consumable and simpler for suitably sized systems; impressed-current systems can protect larger/high-current-demand structures but need a maintained power/control installation. Neither method works merely by calling the metal a cathode: an adequate electrical path, electrolyte and protective current are required.

(b) Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

Galvanising coats iron/steel with zinc. Intact zinc is a barrier; if scratched in a suitable electrolyte, zinc remains more active and dissolves sacrificially, protecting exposed iron. Tinning coats with tin, a more noble metal: intact tin is a barrier, but a scratch can create an iron-anode/tin-cathode couple and accelerate local steel attack. Thus galvanising offers sacrificial protection after small coating damage, unlike ordinary tinning.

Galvanising is a surface-coating method that can also give sacrificial cathodic protection. Separate-anode cathodic protection supplies protective current without requiring the whole structure to be zinc-coated. These are related principles, not mutually exclusive phenomena.

The traditional reason for tinning brass utensils is to separate food from copper/zinc-containing brass. Zinc coatings can dissolve in acidic foods and contaminate them, so galvanised vessels are unsuitable for acidic food storage/cooking. Tin linings must be intact and appropriate for food use; no blanket claim of safety for every food, temperature or damaged lining is justified.

Essay · 8Define cathodic protection. Explain sacrificial anode protection and impressed current method. BL-2+

Cathodic protection

Cathodic protection suppresses metal dissolution by making the structure the cathode. In the sacrificial-anode method, connect a more active metal such as Mg, Zn or a suitable Al alloy to the steel in the electrolyte. The sacrificial metal oxidises (for example Zn → Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻) and supplies electrons to the steel. Inspect and replace consumed anodes. No external DC supply is needed, but available driving voltage/current is limited.

In impressed-current protection, connect the structure to the negative terminal of a DC source and a suitable auxiliary anode to its positive terminal. Current passes through the electrolyte from the auxiliary anode to the structure; the structure is polarised cathodically. It suits large pipelines, tanks and marine structures, with controllable output. It requires power, monitoring and care against overprotection/interference.

Sacrificial anodes are consumable and simpler for suitably sized systems; impressed-current systems can protect larger/high-current-demand structures but need a maintained power/control installation. Neither method works merely by calling the metal a cathode: an adequate electrical path, electrolyte and protective current are required.

Short answer · 1Enumerate the effects of corrosion. BL-1+

Electrochemical corrosion and rusting of iron

Corrosion is deterioration of a material by reaction with its environment. Effects include loss of metal/strength, leakage and equipment failure, contamination, poor appearance and replacement/maintenance costs. Dry corrosion is direct chemical attack (often gas/metal reaction at elevated temperature); wet corrosion involves anodic/cathodic reactions connected through a metal and an electrolyte.

Short answer · 2Differentiate between dry corrosion and wet corrosion. BL-3+

Electrochemical corrosion and rusting of iron

Corrosion is deterioration of a material by reaction with its environment. Effects include loss of metal/strength, leakage and equipment failure, contamination, poor appearance and replacement/maintenance costs. Dry corrosion is direct chemical attack (often gas/metal reaction at elevated temperature); wet corrosion involves anodic/cathodic reactions connected through a metal and an electrolyte.

Short answer · 3What is Pilling-Bedworth rule? Give its significance. BL-1+

Factors controlling corrosion

Pilling–Bedworth ratio = volume of oxide formed / volume of metal consumed. Ratios below one often imply incomplete coverage; moderately above one may favour a continuous film; very large ratios can cause stresses/cracking. This is a useful oxide-film guideline, not a universal quantitative corrosion-rate law; adhesion, defects, volatility and service conditions also matter.

Short answer · 4Corrosion of water filled steel tanks occurs below the water level. Give reasons. BL-3+

Galvanic and differential-aeration corrosion

Differential-aeration corrosion can occur on one metal where oxygen access differs. The oxygen-poor region becomes anodic relative to the oxygen-rich region. Under a water drop, the centre is less aerated and iron dissolves there; the rim supports oxygen reduction. In a partly filled tank, attack is concentrated just below the waterline because that region is less oxygenated than the near-surface cathodic area.

Short answer · 5Compare differential aeration corrosion and differential metal corrosion? BL-2+

Differential-metal corrosion arises when dissimilar metals contact in an electrolyte; the more active metal is anodic. Differential-aeration corrosion arises from unequal oxygen access, even on one metal; the oxygen-poor area becomes anodic. Both involve anodic dissolution and a coupled cathodic reduction.

Short answer · 9Passivity results in more corrosion resistance. Why? BL-2+

Factors controlling corrosion

Passivity is formation of a thin, adherent protective film. Aluminium can resist corrosion despite its active position because Al₂O₃ limits further attack, while ordinary iron rust is porous. Higher temperature commonly accelerates reactions/diffusion, but dissolved-oxygen changes and film formation can alter the trend. Acidic pH often accelerates dissolution/hydrogen evolution; amphoteric metals may also be attacked in strong alkali.

Short answer · 10Iron corrodes faster than Al, even though iron is placed below Al in the galvanic series. Why? BL-3+

Factors controlling corrosion

Passivity is formation of a thin, adherent protective film. Aluminium can resist corrosion despite its active position because Al₂O₃ limits further attack, while ordinary iron rust is porous. Higher temperature commonly accelerates reactions/diffusion, but dissolved-oxygen changes and film formation can alter the trend. Acidic pH often accelerates dissolution/hydrogen evolution; amphoteric metals may also be attacked in strong alkali.

Short answer · 13What is cathodic protection? Give an example of a sacrificial anode. BL-1+

Cathodic protection

Cathodic protection suppresses metal dissolution by making the structure the cathode. In the sacrificial-anode method, connect a more active metal such as Mg, Zn or a suitable Al alloy to the steel in the electrolyte. The sacrificial metal oxidises (for example Zn → Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻) and supplies electrons to the steel. Inspect and replace consumed anodes. No external DC supply is needed, but available driving voltage/current is limited.

Short answer · 16Compare sacrificial anode method with impressed current cathodic protection. BL-2+
Sacrificial anodeImpressed current
Active metal such as Mg/Zn supplies electronsExternal DC source supplies electrons
No external power; anode is consumedNeeds power, auxiliary anode and monitoring
Simpler, limited current outputAdjustable output for larger structures
Short answer · 20How is cathodic protection of iron different from its galvanization. BL-3+

Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

Galvanising is a surface-coating method that can also give sacrificial cathodic protection. Separate-anode cathodic protection supplies protective current without requiring the whole structure to be zinc-coated. These are related principles, not mutually exclusive phenomena.

Short answer · 21Why does corrosion occur in steel pipe connected to copper plumbing? BL-3+

Galvanic and differential-aeration corrosion

Galvanic (differential-metal) corrosion occurs when dissimilar metals are electrically connected in an electrolyte. The more active member becomes the anode and dissolves; the more noble member supports cathodic reduction. For a steel–copper connection in typical aerated water, steel is anodic: Fe → Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻, while oxygen reduction occurs preferentially on copper. Use compatible metals, electrical isolation and suitable coatings to reduce attack. Nuts and bolts of the same metal avoid creating such a couple.

Short answer · 23Zinc-coated iron article is protected from rusting, even when the coating is cracked. Explain. BL-3+

Galvanising, tinning and food-contact cautions

Galvanising coats iron/steel with zinc. Intact zinc is a barrier; if scratched in a suitable electrolyte, zinc remains more active and dissolves sacrificially, protecting exposed iron. Tinning coats with tin, a more noble metal: intact tin is a barrier, but a scratch can create an iron-anode/tin-cathode couple and accelerate local steel attack. Thus galvanising offers sacrificial protection after small coating damage, unlike ordinary tinning.

Essay · 4Explain how the following factors affect the rate of corrosion. BL-2
a) passivity
b) position of the metal
c) relative areas of anode and cathode
d) pH+

(a) Passivity

An adherent, continuous passive film restricts transport and further reaction, allowing active metals such as aluminium to resist corrosion in suitable environments. Film breakdown can reverse this protection.

(b) Position of the metal

In a dissimilar-metal couple in a specified electrolyte, the more active member is usually anodic and dissolves. The relevant galvanic series, film condition and polarisation matter; standard potentials alone do not fix every corrosion rate.

(c) Relative areas

A large cathode coupled to a small anode concentrates dissolution current on the small anodic area. High anodic current density produces intense local attack; avoid this area combination.

(d) pH

Acid often increases dissolution and permits cathodic H₂ evolution. Higher pH can favour protective hydroxide/oxide films, but amphoteric metals can also dissolve in strong alkali. The trend is metal- and environment-dependent.

Unit 3 · Energy Sources and Battery Technology

UNIT-3 Energy sources - Battery Technology QB .docx.pdf

Short answer · 1Classify fuels based on their physical state with examples. [BL-2]+

Classification of fuels

A fuel releases useful heat on combustion. By physical state: solid—wood, coal and charcoal; liquid—petroleum, petrol, diesel and kerosene; gaseous—natural gas, LPG, CNG, coal gas and water gas. LPG is stored as a pressurised liquid but used as a gaseous fuel.

Short answer · 2List out the major characteristics of good fuel. [BL-2]+

A good fuel has high calorific value, moderate ignition temperature, low moisture and ash, controllable combustion and low smoke/corrosive impurities. It should be available, economical and convenient to store, transport and handle safely.

Short answer · 3Gaseous fuels are recommended in the place of solid and liquid fuels. Comment? [BL-3]+

Gaseous fuels mix readily with air, burn with controllable flames, normally leave negligible ash, and permit easy starting/stopping. They reduce manual handling and can give cleaner combustion, but require safe leak control and appropriate storage or pipelines.

Short answer · 4What is the purpose of refining crude oil? [BL-2]+

Petroleum refining and fractional distillation

Petroleum is predominantly a mixture of alkanes, cycloalkanes (naphthenes) and aromatic hydrocarbons, with smaller amounts of sulphur-, nitrogen- and oxygen-containing compounds. Refining removes impurities and separates the crude into useful fractions of different boiling ranges.

Short answer · 5List out the main steps involved in petroleum refining. [BL-2]+

Main steps: remove suspended impurities and water, reduce objectionable sulphur compounds, heat the crude oil, and separate its fractions by fractional distillation. Higher-boiling fractions condense lower in the column; lower-boiling fractions are collected higher up.

Short answer · 6In the catalytic cracking process, catalyst requires regeneration. Give reason. [BL-3]+

Coke deposited on a cracking catalyst blocks active sites and lowers activity. Regeneration burns off the coke in air and restores the catalyst for reuse; the recovered heat also supports the cracking process.

Short answer · 7Define cracking petroleum. Mention the types of cracking. [BL-2]+

Cracking converts heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts or both. Types: thermal cracking—liquid/vapour phase; catalytic cracking—fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Short answer · 8Write the general chemical reaction involved in the Fischer–Tropsch process. [BL-2]+

Fischer–Tropsch synthetic fuel

Paraffins: nCO + (2n+1)H₂ → CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ + nH₂O. Olefins: nCO + 2nH₂ → CₙH₂ₙ + nH₂O. Both represent exothermic synthesis. Cool the converter effluent, condense/separate liquids and fractionate them. Heavy products may be cracked; suitable unconverted gas may be recycled.

Short answer · 9Why is the Fischer–Tropsch process considered important in fuel technology? [BL-3]+

Fischer–Tropsch synthetic fuel

Fischer–Tropsch synthesis converts purified synthesis gas (CO + H₂) to liquid hydrocarbons over cobalt- or iron-based catalysts. It provides liquid fuel from non-petroleum feedstocks such as coal, natural gas or biomass; its carbon footprint depends on feedstock and process energy.

Short answer · 10What type of catalyst is used in the Fischer–Tropsch process? [BL-2]+

Fischer–Tropsch synthetic fuel

A typical catalyst contains 100 parts Co, 5 parts thoria, 8 parts magnesia and 200 parts kieselguhr. Cobalt is the active metal; thoria/magnesia are promoters and kieselguhr is a support. This historical formulation is not the only industrial catalyst.

Short answer · 11Name the main sources from which LPG and CNG are obtained. [BL-2]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

LPG is mainly propane/butane obtained from petroleum refining and natural-gas processing. It is stored as a liquid under pressure; leaked vapour is heavier than air and can collect near the ground. Uses: cooking, heating and automotive/industrial fuel.

CNG is compressed natural gas, mainly methane (about 90–95% in the notes), supplied from natural-gas production/processing and associated petroleum gas. It is stored as gas at about 200–250 bar. It usually produces less particulate matter and CO₂ per unit energy than petrol/diesel; actual CO, NOₓ and methane emissions depend on the engine and operating conditions. High-pressure vessels and leak controls are essential.

Short answer · 12State two advantages of using CNG over petrol or diesel. [BL-2]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

CNG is compressed natural gas, mainly methane (about 90–95% in the notes), supplied from natural-gas production/processing and associated petroleum gas. It is stored as gas at about 200–250 bar. It usually produces less particulate matter and CO₂ per unit energy than petrol/diesel; actual CO, NOₓ and methane emissions depend on the engine and operating conditions. High-pressure vessels and leak controls are essential.

Short answer · 13Why is Hythane considered a cleaner fuel? [BL-3]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

Hythane is hydrogen-enriched CNG; the course blend is about 18–20% H₂ and 80–82% CNG by volume. Hydrogen can improve flame propagation and lean combustion and reduce carbon-containing emissions. It requires compatible equipment, and NOₓ reduction is not guaranteed.

Short answer · 14List out the different types of hydrogen based on their production. [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

Short answer · 15Name the three main types of water electrolysis used to produce green hydrogen.+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

The three electrolysers in the notes are alkaline, proton-exchange membrane (PEM) and solid-oxide. Direct current drives water splitting: 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂. Hydrogen forms at the cathode, oxygen at the anode; a separator prevents their mixing.

Short answer · 16Why is hydrogen produced by water electrolysis called “green”? [BL-3]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

Renewable power is essential to the “green” label; electrolysis using fossil-generated electricity is not automatically green. Green hydrogen can help decarbonise fertilisers, refining and difficult-to-electrify industrial processes, subject to energy, cost and storage limitations.

Short answer · 18Give advantages of sodium-ion batteries over lithium-ion batteries.+

Sodium-ion versus lithium-ion batteries

Sodium is abundant and widely distributed, offering supply-security and possible cost advantages. Some designs permit aluminium current collectors on both electrodes and suit cost-sensitive stationary storage. Lithium-ion commonly uses graphite and Li-containing positive materials and has a mature manufacturing base and generally higher energy density in established commercial comparisons.

Larger/heavier Na⁺ and lower cell voltage can reduce gravimetric energy density, so sodium-ion is not automatically better for every portable or vehicle application. Safety, low-temperature behaviour, cycle life and cost depend on the specific chemistry and cell design; abundance alone does not guarantee a lower final battery price.

Short answer · 19Why sodium is considered a suitable alternative to lithium.[BL-3]+

Sodium-ion versus lithium-ion batteries

Sodium is abundant and widely distributed, offering supply-security and possible cost advantages. Some designs permit aluminium current collectors on both electrodes and suit cost-sensitive stationary storage. Safety, low-temperature behaviour, cycle life and cost depend on the specific chemistry and cell design; abundance alone does not guarantee a lower final battery price.

Short answer · 20Write the chemical reactions taking place at electrodes in zinc-air cell. [BL-2]+

Zinc–air battery

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

Short answer · 21Lithium-ion battery is called the king of batteries. Justify? [BL-3]+

Lithium-ion batteries combine high energy density, low self-discharge and useful rechargeable cycle life in a compact, lightweight form. They power phones, laptops, electric vehicles and storage systems. “King/future battery” describes these advantages, not superiority for every application.

Short answer · 22Why is PEMFC preferred in portable and automotive applications? [BL-2]+

Proton-exchange membrane fuel cell

The membrane conducts protons, separates the reactant gases and blocks electronic short-circuiting. Relatively low operating temperature, quick startup, high power density and compact design favour automotive and portable applications. Applications also include backup power. Limitations include catalyst cost, hydrogen purity/storage, catalyst poisoning and water/thermal management.

Short answer · 23How does a fuel cell differ from battery? [BL-2]+
Primary batterySecondary battery
Intended for single dischargeCan be recharged by reversing reactions
Example: dry cell or primary zinc–airExample: lithium-ion or lead–acid
Short answer · 24What is the function of the proton exchange membrane in a PEMFC? [BL-3]+

Proton-exchange membrane fuel cell

Anode: 2H₂ → 4H⁺ + 4e⁻. H⁺ crosses the membrane; electrons cannot pass through it and instead drive the external load. Cathode: O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O. Overall: 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O, releasing electrical energy and heat. Remove product water and heat and maintain membrane hydration.

Essay · 1Explain the process of fractional distillation of crude oil with a neat, labelled diagram. [BL-2]+

Petroleum refining and fractional distillation

Petroleum is predominantly a mixture of alkanes, cycloalkanes (naphthenes) and aromatic hydrocarbons, with smaller amounts of sulphur-, nitrogen- and oxygen-containing compounds. Refining removes impurities and separates the crude into useful fractions of different boiling ranges.

Pretreatment: remove suspended solids by filtration; remove emulsified water by electrical coalescence (also called Cottrell treatment); remove objectionable sulphur compounds. One example uses CuO, producing removable CuS. These are course descriptions, not a claim that every modern refinery uses the same treatment.

Heat the crude to about 400°C and feed it into the lower part of a fractionating column. The column is hot below and cooler above. Rising vapours repeatedly condense and vaporise on trays/bubble caps. High-boiling components condense lower down; low-boiling components reach higher levels. Withdraw each fraction at its appropriate level.

From top to bottom the notes list uncondensed gases, petroleum ether, gasoline (petrol), naphtha, kerosene, diesel and heavy oils. Further treatment/vacuum distillation of heavy material yields lubricating oils, waxes and heavy residues such as asphalt. Straight-run petrol is the gasoline obtained directly by this fractional distillation, before cracking or reforming.

Note: the source uses “naphthalene” for cycloparaffins; the correct class name is naphthenes. Naphthalene itself is aromatic.

Essay · 2What is catalytic cracking? Explain fluidized – bed (moving bed) catalytic cracking method with a neat, labelled diagram. [BL-2]+

Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

Essay · 3Explain the Fischer–Tropsch process for the synthesis of liquid hydrocarbons from synthesis gas, with suitable chemical reactions and a neat, labelled flow diagram. [BL-2]+

Fischer–Tropsch synthetic fuel

Fischer–Tropsch synthesis converts purified synthesis gas (CO + H₂) to liquid hydrocarbons over cobalt- or iron-based catalysts. It provides liquid fuel from non-petroleum feedstocks such as coal, natural gas or biomass; its carbon footprint depends on feedstock and process energy.

In the historical course route, pass steam over red-hot coke to make water gas: C + H₂O → CO + H₂. Adjust the H₂:CO ratio for synthesis and remove sulphur compounds, which poison the catalyst. Compress the purified gas and pass it over catalyst at 200–300°C and 5–25 atm in this example.

A typical catalyst contains 100 parts Co, 5 parts thoria, 8 parts magnesia and 200 parts kieselguhr. Cobalt is the active metal; thoria/magnesia are promoters and kieselguhr is a support. This historical formulation is not the only industrial catalyst.

Paraffins: nCO + (2n+1)H₂ → CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ + nH₂O. Olefins: nCO + 2nH₂ → CₙH₂ₙ + nH₂O. Both represent exothermic synthesis. Cool the converter effluent, condense/separate liquids and fractionate them. Heavy products may be cracked; suitable unconverted gas may be recycled.

Essay · 4Compare LPG, CNG, and Hythane in terms of composition, storage, calorific value, safety, and applications. [BL-2]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

LPG is mainly propane/butane obtained from petroleum refining and natural-gas processing. It is stored as a liquid under pressure; leaked vapour is heavier than air and can collect near the ground. Uses: cooking, heating and automotive/industrial fuel.

CNG is compressed natural gas, mainly methane (about 90–95% in the notes), supplied from natural-gas production/processing and associated petroleum gas. It is stored as gas at about 200–250 bar. It usually produces less particulate matter and CO₂ per unit energy than petrol/diesel; actual CO, NOₓ and methane emissions depend on the engine and operating conditions. High-pressure vessels and leak controls are essential.

Hythane is hydrogen-enriched CNG; the course blend is about 18–20% H₂ and 80–82% CNG by volume. Hydrogen can improve flame propagation and lean combustion and reduce carbon-containing emissions. It requires compatible equipment, and NOₓ reduction is not guaranteed.

Calorific values must use the same gross/net and mass/volume basis. Hydrogen enrichment raises mass-specific energy but lowers energy per normal gas volume compared with methane. LPG has convenient liquid storage; CNG is established for transport; Hythane is useful in suitably adapted low-emission fleets. There is no universally preferred fuel independent of cost, storage, infrastructure and safety requirements.

For a clearly stated illustrative lower-calorific-value comparison by mass: LPG ≈46 MJ kg⁻¹, methane-rich CNG ≈50 MJ kg⁻¹. Taking Hythane as 20 mol% H₂ and 80 mol% CH₄, H₂ mass fraction = (0.20×2)/(0.20×2+0.80×16) ≈0.0303. With LHV(H₂)≈120 and LHV(CH₄)≈50 MJ kg⁻¹, blend LHV≈0.0303×120+0.9697×50≈52.1 MJ kg⁻¹. These are general-chemistry illustrative values, not measured values supplied for a particular commercial fuel; do not compare them with gross or volume-based values.

Essay · 5Explain the characteristic features of sodium-ion batteries and compare them with lithium-ion batteries in terms of cost, availability, and performance. [BL-2]+

Sodium-ion versus lithium-ion batteries

Sodium-ion cells store energy by reversible Na⁺ transfer. A representative cell uses a hard-carbon negative electrode, a sodium-containing transition-metal-oxide positive electrode and a sodium-salt electrolyte such as NaPF₆ in carbonate solvents. On discharge, sodium leaves the charged negative host, travels as Na⁺ through the electrolyte and inserts into the positive host; electrons flow through the load. Charging reverses the transfer.

Sodium is abundant and widely distributed, offering supply-security and possible cost advantages. Some designs permit aluminium current collectors on both electrodes and suit cost-sensitive stationary storage. Lithium-ion commonly uses graphite and Li-containing positive materials and has a mature manufacturing base and generally higher energy density in established commercial comparisons.

Larger/heavier Na⁺ and lower cell voltage can reduce gravimetric energy density, so sodium-ion is not automatically better for every portable or vehicle application. Safety, low-temperature behaviour, cycle life and cost depend on the specific chemistry and cell design; abundance alone does not guarantee a lower final battery price.

Essay · 6Explain the construction working principle, & applications of a Proton Exchange Membrane fuel cells with a neat, labelled diagram. [BL-2]+

Proton-exchange membrane fuel cell

Construction: two porous gas-diffusion electrodes with catalysts (commonly Pt), a proton-conducting polymer membrane between them, current collectors/flow channels and external load. Feed hydrogen to the anode and oxygen/air to the cathode.

Anode: 2H₂ → 4H⁺ + 4e⁻. H⁺ crosses the membrane; electrons cannot pass through it and instead drive the external load. Cathode: O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O. Overall: 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O, releasing electrical energy and heat. Remove product water and heat and maintain membrane hydration.

The membrane conducts protons, separates the reactant gases and blocks electronic short-circuiting. Relatively low operating temperature, quick startup, high power density and compact design favour automotive and portable applications. Applications also include backup power. Limitations include catalyst cost, hydrogen purity/storage, catalyst poisoning and water/thermal management.

Essay · 7Classify the different types of hydrogen based on their production mechanism. [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

The three electrolysers in the notes are alkaline, proton-exchange membrane (PEM) and solid-oxide. Direct current drives water splitting: 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂. Hydrogen forms at the cathode, oxygen at the anode; a separator prevents their mixing.

Alkaline (KOH/NaOH): cathode 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻; anode 2OH⁻ → ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻. OH⁻ moves toward the anode.

PEM: anode H₂O → ½O₂ + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻; cathode 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂. Protons move through the membrane toward the cathode. Solid oxide: cathode H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + O²⁻; anode O²⁻ → ½O₂ + 2e⁻.

Renewable power is essential to the “green” label; electrolysis using fossil-generated electricity is not automatically green. Green hydrogen can help decarbonise fertilisers, refining and difficult-to-electrify industrial processes, subject to energy, cost and storage limitations.

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis
