Essay · 9What is a secondary battery? Explain the working principle of secondary battery with necessary equations. [BL-3]+

Construction: graphite negative electrode, lithium cobalt oxide positive electrode, a non-aqueous lithium-salt electrolyte and a porous separator.

Discharging

  • Lithium ions move through the electrolyte from the negative electrode to the positive electrode.
  • Electrons flow through the external load, producing electrical current.

Negative electrode

\[\mathrm{LiC_6\rightarrow C_6+Li^++e^-}\]

Positive electrode

\[\mathrm{CoO_2+Li^++e^-\rightarrow LiCoO_2}\]

Overall discharge reaction

\[\boxed{\mathrm{LiC_6+CoO_2\rightarrow C_6+LiCoO_2}}\]

The separator prevents direct contact between the electrodes while allowing ionic conduction. During discharge, graphite is the anode and the positive electrode is the cathode; oxidation and reduction occur at these respective electrodes.

Charging: an external supply reverses these reactions and returns lithium ions to the graphite electrode.

Essay · 10Describe the working of zinc-air battery with relevant equations and summarize its features and applications. [BL-2]+

Zinc–air battery

Construction: zinc powder/gel is the negative electrode; a porous carbon/catalyst air electrode is the positive electrode. A separator prevents shorting. Aqueous KOH (about 30% in this example) conducts ions, and gas-permeable layers admit atmospheric oxygen while managing electrolyte.

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

High energy density and low material cost follow partly from obtaining oxygen externally. Sealed shelf life is long, but life after opening the air seal and power capability are limited. Uses include hearing aids and small medical/portable devices. A standard primary hearing-aid zinc–air cell should not be assumed rechargeable; specialised rechargeable metal–air systems are different designs.

Essay · 11Discuss the classification of batteries into primary, secondary, reserve and fuel cells. [BL-2]+
TypeOperationExample
PrimaryIntended for single discharge; not normally rechargeableZinc–carbon dry cell
SecondaryReversible reactions permit repeated charging and dischargingLithium-ion or lead–acid battery
ReserveAn essential component is kept separate until activation, giving long storage lifeWater-activated magnesium–silver chloride battery
Fuel cellFuel and oxidant are continuously supplied from outsideHydrogen–oxygen fuel cell

Primary and secondary batteries store their active reactants inside the cell. A secondary battery is recharged by reversing its reactions with an external supply. A reserve battery is activated only when needed, whereas a fuel cell continues working by receiving fresh fuel and oxidant.

Short answer · 1Classify fuels based on their physical state with examples. [BL-2]+

Classification of fuels

A fuel releases useful heat on combustion. By physical state: solid—wood, coal and charcoal; liquid—petroleum, petrol, diesel and kerosene; gaseous—natural gas, LPG, CNG, coal gas and water gas. LPG is stored as a pressurised liquid but used as a gaseous fuel.

Short answer · 2List out the major characteristics of a good fuel.+

A good fuel has high calorific value, moderate ignition temperature, low moisture and ash, controllable combustion and low smoke/corrosive impurities. It should be available, economical and convenient to store, transport and handle safely.

Short answer · 3Gaseous fuels are recommended in the place of solid and liquid fuels. Comment? [BL-3]+

Gaseous fuels mix readily with air, burn with controllable flames, normally leave negligible ash, and permit easy starting/stopping. They reduce manual handling and can give cleaner combustion, but require safe leak control and appropriate storage or pipelines.

Short answer · 4Name the highest-ranking coal. Give its approximate calorific value.+

Anthracite is the highest-ranking coal. Its approximate calorific value is 8,000–8,500 kcal/kg, depending on grade and the reporting basis.

Short answer · 5Justify how determination of volatile content in a coal sample helps in designing furnaces in metallurgy.+

Volatile matter affects ignition, flame length, smoke and combustion-air requirements. Its determination helps select the furnace size, combustion space and air supply needed for efficient burning of the coal.

Short answer · 6A good sample of coal should contain less nitrogen content. Give reason.+

Nitrogen contributes little useful heat and may form nitrogen oxides during combustion. Low nitrogen content is therefore desirable to reduce heat dilution and NOₓ pollution.

Short answer · 7Rationalize your reason why a good sample of coal should contain less amount of ash and high amount of fixed carbon?+

Ash is non-combustible: it lowers useful heating value, causes slagging and creates disposal problems. Fixed carbon gives sustained heat and is important for metallurgical coke. Hence good coal should contain low ash and a suitable high fixed-carbon content.

Short answer · 8Enumerate the significance in the determination of moisture in coal?+

Moisture consumes heat during evaporation, lowers the effective calorific value and increases transport/handling weight. Measuring it helps assess fuel quality and the useful heat obtainable from the coal.

Short answer · 9In catalytic cracking process, catalyst requires regeneration. Give reason.+

Coke deposited on a cracking catalyst blocks active sites and lowers activity. Regeneration burns off the coke in air and restores the catalyst for reuse; the recovered heat also supports the cracking process.

Short answer · 10Define cracking of petroleum? Mention the types of cracking.+

Cracking converts heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts or both. Types: thermal cracking—liquid/vapour phase; catalytic cracking—fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Short answer · 11What is straight run petrol? How is it obtained?+

Straight-run petrol is the gasoline fraction obtained directly by fractional distillation of crude petroleum, before cracking or reforming. It is collected in the appropriate gasoline boiling range from the fractionating column.

Short answer · 12What is Knocking? How can it be prevented? OR What is knocking? What are the ill effects of knocking? [BL-2]+

Knocking in a petrol engine is sudden autoignition of unburnt end-gas, causing pressure waves and a metallic sound. Use suitable high-octane fuel, correct ignition timing, effective cooling and an appropriate compression ratio to reduce it.

Short answer · 13Define octane and cetane number.+

Octane number measures petrol’s resistance to knocking by comparison with iso-octane (100) and n-heptane (0). Cetane number measures diesel ignition quality; higher cetane means shorter ignition delay. They describe different engine-fuel properties.

Short answer · 14Arrange n-octane, naphthalene, isooctane in the increasing order of their knocking tendency?+

Increasing knocking tendency in a petrol engine: naphthalene < iso-octane < n-octane. Aromatic/branched structures resist knock better than the straight-chain n-octane in this comparison.

Short answer · 15Gasoline containing TEL is used in internal combustion engines. Give reason.+

Tetraethyl lead was historically added to petrol as an antiknock agent. It suppresses premature autoignition and raises octane quality, improving smooth operation. Its toxic lead emissions led to the adoption of unleaded petrol.

Short answer · 16Why is a small quantity of ethyl bromide added to petrol along with anti- knocking agent TEL? [BL-2]+

Ethyl bromide was used as a scavenger with tetraethyl lead. It helps convert lead-containing combustion deposits into more volatile lead bromide, reducing deposits on engine parts. This describes historical leaded petrol, not modern unleaded-fuel practice.

Short answer · 17List out the different types of hydrogen based on their production. [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

Short answer · 18Substantiate your reason for green hydrogen being the need of hour in the day to day to life. [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

Renewable power is essential to the “green” label; electrolysis using fossil-generated electricity is not automatically green. Green hydrogen can help decarbonise fertilisers, refining and difficult-to-electrify industrial processes, subject to energy, cost and storage limitations.

Short answer · 19Differentiate primary and secondary batteries with examples. [BL-2]+
Primary batterySecondary battery
Intended for single dischargeCan be recharged by reversing reactions
Example: dry cell or primary zinc–airExample: lithium-ion or lead–acid
Short answer · 20Write the chemical reactions taking place at electrodes in zinc-air cell. [BL-2]+

Zinc–air battery

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

Short answer · 21Lithium-ion battery is called the king of batteries. Justify? [BL-3]+

Lithium-ion batteries combine high energy density, low self-discharge and useful rechargeable cycle life in a compact, lightweight form. They power phones, laptops, electric vehicles and storage systems. “King/future battery” describes these advantages, not superiority for every application.

Short answer · 22State the principle involved in methanol-oxygen fuel cell?+

A methanol–oxygen fuel cell converts chemical energy directly into electricity. Methanol is oxidised at the anode and oxygen reduced at the cathode. Overall: CH₃OH + 3/2 O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O. Electrons pass through the external load.

Short answer · 23How does a fuel cell differ from battery? [BL-2]+
Primary batterySecondary battery
Intended for single dischargeCan be recharged by reversing reactions
Example: dry cell or primary zinc–airExample: lithium-ion or lead–acid
Short answer · 24Justify the reason for using fuel cells in space vehicles.+

Hydrogen–oxygen fuel cells give direct chemical-to-electrical conversion, useful efficiency and continuous output while reactants are supplied. They have quiet operation, and their product water can be useful in spacecraft. Reactant storage and system reliability remain important.

Essay · 1Explain in detail how is coal analyzed by proximate analysis method and give its significance.+

Coal analysis: proximate and ultimate

Anthracite is the highest-rank coal. Its calorific value varies with grade and reporting basis; the traditional course approximation is about 8,000–8,500 kcal kg⁻¹ (roughly 33–36 MJ kg⁻¹), rather than one universal measured value.

Proximate analysis: (1) Moisture—heat a known sample near 105–110°C to constant mass; moisture % = loss/original sample mass ×100. (2) Volatile matter—heat the dried sample in a covered crucible near 925°C for about 7 minutes under the specified test method; volatile % = additional loss/original mass ×100. Excluding air avoids burning the fixed carbon. (3) Ash—heat a separate known sample/open crucible in air near 700–750°C to constant residue; ash % = residue/original mass ×100. (4) Fixed carbon % = 100 − (moisture % + volatile % + ash %), all on the same reporting basis. Fixed carbon is not the same as total elemental carbon.

Moisture wastes heat in evaporation and increases handling weight. Volatile matter affects ignition, flame length, smoke and combustion-air/furnace design. Ash is non-combustible, reduces useful heat per mass and can cause slagging and disposal problems. High fixed carbon supports a sustained hot bed and is important in metallurgical coke. Low nitrogen reduces dilution and fuel-NOₓ potential. Sulphur can cause SOₓ pollution, corrosion and undesirable sulphur in metallurgical products; oxygen already bound in fuel generally lowers useful heating value.

Ultimate analysis reports elemental C, H, N, S and O (with ash/moisture basis stated). Burn a known sample in oxygen: C is calculated from collected CO₂, H from collected H₂O: %C = (12/44)(mass CO₂/sample mass)×100 and %H = (2/18)(mass H₂O/sample mass)×100. Determine N by an appropriate nitrogen-analysis method (the traditional course procedure is Kjeldahl), S by conversion to sulphate and BaSO₄ weighing: %S = (32/233)(mass BaSO₄/sample mass)×100. Oxygen is traditionally calculated by difference after accounting for all constituents on the stated basis. These results guide combustion-air calculation, calorific-value estimates and pollution control.

Kjeldahl working: digest a known coal mass with concentrated H₂SO₄ and the specified catalyst/salts; nitrogen is converted to ammonium species. Add excess NaOH, distil the liberated NH₃ into a known amount of standard acid and back-titrate the excess acid with a blank correction. For net V mL of acid of normality N consumed by NH₃ and m g coal, %N = 1.4VN/m. The balanced liberation step is (NH₄)₂SO₄ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₄ + 2NH₃ + 2H₂O. The packet’s version omits one water molecule; this is an atom-balance correction. For its sulphur method, burn coal in oxygen in a bomb, extract sulphate in the washings/residue and precipitate with BaCl₂; Ba²⁺ + SO₄²⁻ → BaSO₄. Oxygen by difference must include ash/moisture corrections appropriate to the reporting basis; simply subtracting CHNS from 100 in an ash-containing sample overstates oxygen.

General chemistry clarifies the source’s unbalanced ammonia-liberation step, oxygen-by-difference basis and the approximate, grade-dependent calorific value.

Essay · 3Explain the significance of the following constituents present in a coal sample
a) Moisture content
b) Volatile matter
c) Total carbon
d) Sulphur
e) Oxygen+

(a) Moisture

Moisture consumes heat on evaporation, reduces useful heat per kilogram and increases handling/transport mass; high moisture is usually undesirable.

(b) Volatile matter

Volatile matter controls ignition behaviour, flame length and smoke formation. Its amount helps select furnace volume, air supply and firing conditions; its significance depends on the intended use.

(c) Total carbon

Total carbon includes carbon in fixed carbon and volatile constituents. It is measured in ultimate analysis and is distinct from proximate fixed carbon; a larger useful combustible-carbon fraction generally increases available heat.

(d) Sulphur

Sulphur can contribute some heat but produces SOₓ and corrosive products and can contaminate metallurgical products; low sulphur is desirable.

(e) Oxygen

Bound oxygen generally reduces the net heating value relative to a comparable fuel richer in combustible carbon/hydrogen. Oxygen-by-difference requires a consistent ash/moisture reporting basis.

Essay · 5
a) What is catalytic cracking of petroleum? Explain fluidized — bed catalytic method with a neat labelled diagram. (5M)
b) Write short note on knocking in internal combustion engines. (3M)+

(a) Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

(b) Knocking, octane and cetane numbers

In a spark-ignition petrol engine, knocking is rapid autoignition of the unburnt end-gas ahead of the normal flame, causing pressure waves and a metallic sound. It lowers smoothness/efficiency and can damage parts. Suitable high-octane fuel, correct ignition timing, cooling and appropriate compression ratio help prevent it. Diesel knock arises from excessive ignition delay followed by rapid combustion; do not confuse the two mechanisms.

Octane number measures resistance to knock in a standard spark-ignition test by comparison with iso-octane (100) and n-heptane (0). Cetane number measures ignition quality of diesel in a standard compression-ignition test; a higher value normally means a shorter ignition delay. It is not an efficiency rating of petrol. The historical reference pair is cetane (100) and α-methylnaphthalene (0); modern testing also uses calibrated reference fuels.

For the qualitative question comparing iso-octane, naphthalene and n-octane, aromatic naphthalene has high antiknock tendency and straight-chain n-octane knocks strongly; the conventional course ordering of increasing petrol knocking tendency is naphthalene < iso-octane < n-octane. This is a qualitative historical comparison, not a recommendation to blend solid naphthalene into fuel.

Tetraethyl lead (TEL) was historically added as an antiknock agent; it interferes with combustion-chain reactions. Ethylene dibromide was used as a lead scavenger, converting lead compounds to more volatile lead bromide and reducing deposits. The question’s “ethyl bromide” is historically imprecise: standard ethyl fluid used ethylene dibromide (often with ethylene dichloride). Leaded petrol and its scavengers are toxic legacy practice, not current fuel advice.

Essay · 6
a) Define cracking. Explain fluid-bed cracking with diagram. [BL-2]
b) Explain working of methanol-oxygen fuel cell with chemical equations. [BL-2]+

(a) Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

(b) Methanol–oxygen fuel cell

A direct methanol cell electrochemically oxidises supplied methanol and reduces oxygen. In an acidic/proton-conducting description: anode CH₃OH + H₂O → CO₂ + 6H⁺ + 6e⁻; cathode 3/2 O₂ + 6H⁺ + 6e⁻ → 3H₂O. Overall CH₃OH + 3/2 O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O. Electrons pass through the load while ions move through the electrolyte.

The older alkaline form is equivalent: anode CH₃OH + 6OH⁻ → CO₂ + 5H₂O + 6e⁻; cathode 3/2 O₂ + 3H₂O + 6e⁻ → 6OH⁻. In an alkaline electrolyte, CO₂ can subsequently form carbonate. State which electrolyte convention is used rather than mixing half-reactions. Methanol is a convenient liquid fuel, but toxicity, crossover, catalyst cost and carbon emissions are limitations; it is distinct from the R25 hydrogen PEMFC.

Essay · 7What is knocking? Explain the significance of Octane number and Cetane number in determining the efficiency of petrol?+

Essay · 8
a) Give the composition of petroleum and explain how it is refined in distilleries. (4M)
b) Explain the fluidized catalytic cracking process with a neat labelled diagram. (4M)+

(a) Petroleum refining and fractional distillation

Petroleum is predominantly a mixture of alkanes, cycloalkanes (naphthenes) and aromatic hydrocarbons, with smaller amounts of sulphur-, nitrogen- and oxygen-containing compounds. Refining removes impurities and separates the crude into useful fractions of different boiling ranges.

Pretreatment: remove suspended solids by filtration; remove emulsified water by electrical coalescence (also called Cottrell treatment); remove objectionable sulphur compounds. One example uses CuO, producing removable CuS. These are course descriptions, not a claim that every modern refinery uses the same treatment.

Heat the crude to about 400°C and feed it into the lower part of a fractionating column. The column is hot below and cooler above. Rising vapours repeatedly condense and vaporise on trays/bubble caps. High-boiling components condense lower down; low-boiling components reach higher levels. Withdraw each fraction at its appropriate level.

From top to bottom the notes list uncondensed gases, petroleum ether, gasoline (petrol), naphtha, kerosene, diesel and heavy oils. Further treatment/vacuum distillation of heavy material yields lubricating oils, waxes and heavy residues such as asphalt. Straight-run petrol is the gasoline obtained directly by this fractional distillation, before cracking or reforming.

Note: the source uses “naphthalene” for cycloparaffins; the correct class name is naphthenes. Naphthalene itself is aromatic.

(b) Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

Essay · 9
a) Classify the different types of hydrogen based on their production mechanism? (2M) [BL-2]
b) Discuss the mechanism of electrolysis of water involved in the production of green hydrogen with necessary equations. (6M) [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

The three electrolysers in the notes are alkaline, proton-exchange membrane (PEM) and solid-oxide. Direct current drives water splitting: 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂. Hydrogen forms at the cathode, oxygen at the anode; a separator prevents their mixing.

Alkaline (KOH/NaOH): cathode 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻; anode 2OH⁻ → ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻. OH⁻ moves toward the anode.

PEM: anode H₂O → ½O₂ + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻; cathode 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂. Protons move through the membrane toward the cathode. Solid oxide: cathode H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + O²⁻; anode O²⁻ → ½O₂ + 2e⁻.

Renewable power is essential to the “green” label; electrolysis using fossil-generated electricity is not automatically green. Green hydrogen can help decarbonise fertilisers, refining and difficult-to-electrify industrial processes, subject to energy, cost and storage limitations.

Essay · 10What is a secondary cell? Explain the working of Lithium ion battery and mention its Applications.+

Construction: graphite negative electrode, lithium cobalt oxide positive electrode, a non-aqueous lithium-salt electrolyte and a porous separator.

Discharging

  • Lithium ions move through the electrolyte from the negative electrode to the positive electrode.
  • Electrons flow through the external load, producing electrical current.

Negative electrode

\[\mathrm{LiC_6\rightarrow C_6+Li^++e^-}\]

Positive electrode

\[\mathrm{CoO_2+Li^++e^-\rightarrow LiCoO_2}\]

Overall discharge reaction

\[\boxed{\mathrm{LiC_6+CoO_2\rightarrow C_6+LiCoO_2}}\]

The separator prevents direct contact between the electrodes while allowing ionic conduction. During discharge, graphite is the anode and the positive electrode is the cathode; oxidation and reduction occur at these respective electrodes.

Charging: an external supply reverses these reactions and returns lithium ions to the graphite electrode.

Essay · 11
a) Describe the working of zinc air battery with relevant equations and summarize its features and applications? (4M) [BL-2]
b) What is a solar cell? Outline its principle and list out its applications? (4M) [BL-2]+

(a) Zinc–air battery

Construction: zinc powder/gel is the negative electrode; a porous carbon/catalyst air electrode is the positive electrode. A separator prevents shorting. Aqueous KOH (about 30% in this example) conducts ions, and gas-permeable layers admit atmospheric oxygen while managing electrolyte.

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

High energy density and low material cost follow partly from obtaining oxygen externally. Sealed shelf life is long, but life after opening the air seal and power capability are limited. Uses include hearing aids and small medical/portable devices. A standard primary hearing-aid zinc–air cell should not be assumed rechargeable; specialised rechargeable metal–air systems are different designs.

(b) Solar cell

A solar cell converts light directly to electricity through the photovoltaic effect. In a semiconductor p–n junction, absorbed photons generate electron–hole pairs. The junction’s built-in electric field separates carriers, and contacts collect them to produce current through an external load. No fuel combustion is involved. Uses include rooftop/utility power, satellites, solar lighting, calculators and remote power supplies. Output depends on illumination, temperature and device efficiency; energy storage is needed for supply when sunlight is unavailable.

General chemistry/energy knowledge supplies the explanation here: the supplied packet asks this question but does not provide sufficient explanatory notes for it.

Essay · 12Discuss the classification of batteries into primary, secondary, reserve and fuel cells. [BL-2]+
TypeOperationExample
PrimaryIntended for single discharge; not normally rechargeableZinc–carbon dry cell
SecondaryReversible reactions permit repeated charging and dischargingLithium-ion or lead–acid battery
ReserveAn essential component is kept separate until activation, giving long storage lifeWater-activated magnesium–silver chloride battery
Fuel cellFuel and oxidant are continuously supplied from outsideHydrogen–oxygen fuel cell

Primary and secondary batteries store their active reactants inside the cell. A secondary battery is recharged by reversing its reactions with an external supply. A reserve battery is activated only when needed, whereas a fuel cell continues working by receiving fresh fuel and oxidant.

Unit 3.pdf

Short answer · 1Classify fuels based on their physical state with examples. [BL-2]+

Classification of fuels

A fuel releases useful heat on combustion. By physical state: solid—wood, coal and charcoal; liquid—petroleum, petrol, diesel and kerosene; gaseous—natural gas, LPG, CNG, coal gas and water gas. LPG is stored as a pressurised liquid but used as a gaseous fuel.

Short answer · 3Gaseous fuels are recommended in the place of solid and liquid fuels. Comment. [BL-3]+

Gaseous fuels mix readily with air, burn with controllable flames, normally leave negligible ash, and permit easy starting/stopping. They reduce manual handling and can give cleaner combustion, but require safe leak control and appropriate storage or pipelines.

Short answer · 11Name the main sources from which LPG and CNG are obtained. [BL-2]+

LPG is obtained from petroleum refining and natural-gas processing. CNG is methane-rich natural gas obtained from gas production/processing, including associated petroleum gas, and compressed for storage and use.

Short answer · 12State two advantages of using CNG over petrol or diesel. [BL-2]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

CNG is compressed natural gas, mainly methane (about 90–95% in the notes), supplied from natural-gas production/processing and associated petroleum gas. It is stored as gas at about 200–250 bar. It usually produces less particulate matter and CO₂ per unit energy than petrol/diesel; actual CO, NOₓ and methane emissions depend on the engine and operating conditions. High-pressure vessels and leak controls are essential.

Short answer · 13Why is Hythane considered a cleaner fuel? [BL-3]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

Hythane is hydrogen-enriched CNG; the course blend is about 18–20% H₂ and 80–82% CNG by volume. Hydrogen can improve flame propagation and lean combustion and reduce carbon-containing emissions. It requires compatible equipment, and NOₓ reduction is not guaranteed.

Short answer · 14List out the different types of hydrogen based on their production. [BL-2]+

Hydrogen production colours and green electrolysis

Hydrogen colours identify production routes, not different hydrogen molecules. Grey: fossil methane reforming without CO₂ capture; brown/black: coal gasification; blue: fossil-based production with carbon capture; turquoise: methane pyrolysis producing solid carbon; green: water electrolysis powered by renewable electricity. Capture efficiency and upstream emissions prevent blue hydrogen from being automatically zero-carbon.

Short answer · 17Differentiate primary and secondary batteries with examples. [BL-2]+
Primary batterySecondary battery
Intended for single dischargeCan be recharged by reversing reactions
Example: dry cell or primary zinc–airExample: lithium-ion or lead–acid
Short answer · 20Write the chemical reactions taking place at electrodes in zinc-air cell. [BL-2]+

Zinc–air battery

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

Short answer · 21Lithium-ion battery is called the king of batteries. Justify. [BL-3]+

Lithium-ion batteries combine high energy density, low self-discharge and useful rechargeable cycle life in a compact, lightweight form. They power phones, laptops, electric vehicles and storage systems. “King/future battery” describes these advantages, not superiority for every application.

UNIT-3 Questions.pdf

Short answer · 2List out the major characteristics of a good fuel. [BL-2]+

A good fuel has high calorific value, moderate ignition temperature, low moisture and ash, controllable combustion and low smoke/corrosive impurities. It should be available, economical and convenient to store, transport and handle safely.

Short answer · 4Name the highest-ranking coal. Give its approximate calorific value. [BL-2]+

Anthracite is the highest-ranking coal. Its approximate calorific value is 8,000–8,500 kcal/kg, depending on grade and the reporting basis.

Short answer · 5Justify how determination of volatile content in a coal sample helps in designing furnaces in metallurgy. [BL-3]+

Volatile matter affects ignition, flame length, smoke and combustion-air requirements. Its determination helps select the furnace size, combustion space and air supply needed for efficient burning of the coal.

Short answer · 6A good sample of coal should contain less nitrogen content. Give reason. [BL-2]+

Nitrogen contributes little useful heat and may form nitrogen oxides during combustion. Low nitrogen content is therefore desirable to reduce heat dilution and NOₓ pollution.

Short answer · 7Rationalize your reason why a good sample of coal should contain less amount of ash and high amount of fixed carbon? [BL-2]+

Ash is non-combustible: it lowers useful heating value, causes slagging and creates disposal problems. Fixed carbon gives sustained heat and is important for metallurgical coke. Hence good coal should contain low ash and a suitable high fixed-carbon content.

Short answer · 8Enumerate the significance in the determination of moisture in coal? [BL-3]+

Moisture consumes heat during evaporation, lowers the effective calorific value and increases transport/handling weight. Measuring it helps assess fuel quality and the useful heat obtainable from the coal.

Short answer · 9In catalytic cracking process, catalyst requires regeneration. Give reason. [BL- 3]+

Coke deposited on a cracking catalyst blocks active sites and lowers activity. Regeneration burns off the coke in air and restores the catalyst for reuse; the recovered heat also supports the cracking process.

Short answer · 10Define cracking of petroleum? Mention the types of cracking. [BL-2]+

Cracking converts heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts or both. Types: thermal cracking—liquid/vapour phase; catalytic cracking—fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Short answer · 11What is straight run petrol? How is it obtained? [BL-2]+

Straight-run petrol is the gasoline fraction obtained directly by fractional distillation of crude petroleum, before cracking or reforming. It is collected in the appropriate gasoline boiling range from the fractionating column.

Short answer · 13Define octane and cetane number. [BL-2]+

Octane number measures petrol’s resistance to knocking by comparison with iso-octane (100) and n-heptane (0). Cetane number measures diesel ignition quality; higher cetane means shorter ignition delay. They describe different engine-fuel properties.

Short answer · 14Arrange n-octane, naphthalene, isooctane in the increasing order of their knocking tendency? [BL-2]+

Increasing knocking tendency in a petrol engine: naphthalene < iso-octane < n-octane. Aromatic/branched structures resist knock better than the straight-chain n-octane in this comparison.

Short answer · 15Gasoline containing TEL is used in internal combustion engines. Give reason. [BL-3]+

Tetraethyl lead was historically added to petrol as an antiknock agent. It suppresses premature autoignition and raises octane quality, improving smooth operation. Its toxic lead emissions led to the adoption of unleaded petrol.

Short answer · 19Differentiate primary and secondary batteries with examples. [BL-2]+
Primary batterySecondary battery
Intended for single dischargeCan be recharged by reversing reactions
Example: dry cell or primary zinc–airExample: lithium-ion or lead–acid
Short answer · 22State the principle involved in methanol-oxygen fuel cell? [BL-2]+

A methanol–oxygen fuel cell converts chemical energy directly into electricity. Methanol is oxidised at the anode and oxygen reduced at the cathode. Overall: CH₃OH + 3/2 O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O. Electrons pass through the external load.

Short answer · 24Justify the reason for using fuel cells in space vehicles. [BL-3]+

Hydrogen–oxygen fuel cells give direct chemical-to-electrical conversion, useful efficiency and continuous output while reactants are supplied. They have quiet operation, and their product water can be useful in spacecraft. Reactant storage and system reliability remain important.

Essay · 1Explain in detail how is coal analyzed by proximate analysis method and give its significance. [BL-2]+

Coal analysis: proximate and ultimate

Anthracite is the highest-rank coal. Its calorific value varies with grade and reporting basis; the traditional course approximation is about 8,000–8,500 kcal kg⁻¹ (roughly 33–36 MJ kg⁻¹), rather than one universal measured value.

Proximate analysis: (1) Moisture—heat a known sample near 105–110°C to constant mass; moisture % = loss/original sample mass ×100. (2) Volatile matter—heat the dried sample in a covered crucible near 925°C for about 7 minutes under the specified test method; volatile % = additional loss/original mass ×100. Excluding air avoids burning the fixed carbon. (3) Ash—heat a separate known sample/open crucible in air near 700–750°C to constant residue; ash % = residue/original mass ×100. (4) Fixed carbon % = 100 − (moisture % + volatile % + ash %), all on the same reporting basis. Fixed carbon is not the same as total elemental carbon.

Moisture wastes heat in evaporation and increases handling weight. Volatile matter affects ignition, flame length, smoke and combustion-air/furnace design. Ash is non-combustible, reduces useful heat per mass and can cause slagging and disposal problems. High fixed carbon supports a sustained hot bed and is important in metallurgical coke. Low nitrogen reduces dilution and fuel-NOₓ potential. Sulphur can cause SOₓ pollution, corrosion and undesirable sulphur in metallurgical products; oxygen already bound in fuel generally lowers useful heating value.

Ultimate analysis reports elemental C, H, N, S and O (with ash/moisture basis stated). Burn a known sample in oxygen: C is calculated from collected CO₂, H from collected H₂O: %C = (12/44)(mass CO₂/sample mass)×100 and %H = (2/18)(mass H₂O/sample mass)×100. Determine N by an appropriate nitrogen-analysis method (the traditional course procedure is Kjeldahl), S by conversion to sulphate and BaSO₄ weighing: %S = (32/233)(mass BaSO₄/sample mass)×100. Oxygen is traditionally calculated by difference after accounting for all constituents on the stated basis. These results guide combustion-air calculation, calorific-value estimates and pollution control.

Kjeldahl working: digest a known coal mass with concentrated H₂SO₄ and the specified catalyst/salts; nitrogen is converted to ammonium species. Add excess NaOH, distil the liberated NH₃ into a known amount of standard acid and back-titrate the excess acid with a blank correction. For net V mL of acid of normality N consumed by NH₃ and m g coal, %N = 1.4VN/m. The balanced liberation step is (NH₄)₂SO₄ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₄ + 2NH₃ + 2H₂O. The packet’s version omits one water molecule; this is an atom-balance correction. For its sulphur method, burn coal in oxygen in a bomb, extract sulphate in the washings/residue and precipitate with BaCl₂; Ba²⁺ + SO₄²⁻ → BaSO₄. Oxygen by difference must include ash/moisture corrections appropriate to the reporting basis; simply subtracting CHNS from 100 in an ash-containing sample overstates oxygen.

General chemistry clarifies the source’s unbalanced ammonia-liberation step, oxygen-by-difference basis and the approximate, grade-dependent calorific value.

Essay · 3Explain the significance of the following constituents present in a coal sample
a) Moisture content
b) Volatile matter
c) Total carbon
d) Sulphur
e) Oxygen [BL-2]+

(a) Moisture

Moisture consumes heat on evaporation, reduces useful heat per kilogram and increases handling/transport mass; high moisture is usually undesirable.

(b) Volatile matter

Volatile matter controls ignition behaviour, flame length and smoke formation. Its amount helps select furnace volume, air supply and firing conditions; its significance depends on the intended use.

(c) Total carbon

Total carbon includes carbon in fixed carbon and volatile constituents. It is measured in ultimate analysis and is distinct from proximate fixed carbon; a larger useful combustible-carbon fraction generally increases available heat.

(d) Sulphur

Sulphur can contribute some heat but produces SOₓ and corrosive products and can contaminate metallurgical products; low sulphur is desirable.

(e) Oxygen

Bound oxygen generally reduces the net heating value relative to a comparable fuel richer in combustible carbon/hydrogen. Oxygen-by-difference requires a consistent ash/moisture reporting basis.

Essay · 5
a) What is catalytic cracking of petroleum? Explain fluidized – bed catalytic method with a neat, labelled diagram. (5M) [BL-2]
b) Write short note on knocking in internal combustion engines. (3M) [BL-1]+

(a) Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

(b) Knocking, octane and cetane numbers

In a spark-ignition petrol engine, knocking is rapid autoignition of the unburnt end-gas ahead of the normal flame, causing pressure waves and a metallic sound. It lowers smoothness/efficiency and can damage parts. Suitable high-octane fuel, correct ignition timing, cooling and appropriate compression ratio help prevent it. Diesel knock arises from excessive ignition delay followed by rapid combustion; do not confuse the two mechanisms.

Octane number measures resistance to knock in a standard spark-ignition test by comparison with iso-octane (100) and n-heptane (0). Cetane number measures ignition quality of diesel in a standard compression-ignition test; a higher value normally means a shorter ignition delay. It is not an efficiency rating of petrol. The historical reference pair is cetane (100) and α-methylnaphthalene (0); modern testing also uses calibrated reference fuels.

For the qualitative question comparing iso-octane, naphthalene and n-octane, aromatic naphthalene has high antiknock tendency and straight-chain n-octane knocks strongly; the conventional course ordering of increasing petrol knocking tendency is naphthalene < iso-octane < n-octane. This is a qualitative historical comparison, not a recommendation to blend solid naphthalene into fuel.

Tetraethyl lead (TEL) was historically added as an antiknock agent; it interferes with combustion-chain reactions. Ethylene dibromide was used as a lead scavenger, converting lead compounds to more volatile lead bromide and reducing deposits. The question’s “ethyl bromide” is historically imprecise: standard ethyl fluid used ethylene dibromide (often with ethylene dichloride). Leaded petrol and its scavengers are toxic legacy practice, not current fuel advice.

Essay · 7What is knocking? Explain the significance of Octane number and Cetane number in determining the efficiency of petrol? [BL-2]+

Essay · 8
a) Give the composition of petroleum and explain how it is refined in distilleries. (4M) [BL-2]
b) Explain the fluidized catalytic cracking process with a neat, labelled diagram. (4M) [BL-2]+

(a) Petroleum refining and fractional distillation

Petroleum is predominantly a mixture of alkanes, cycloalkanes (naphthenes) and aromatic hydrocarbons, with smaller amounts of sulphur-, nitrogen- and oxygen-containing compounds. Refining removes impurities and separates the crude into useful fractions of different boiling ranges.

Pretreatment: remove suspended solids by filtration; remove emulsified water by electrical coalescence (also called Cottrell treatment); remove objectionable sulphur compounds. One example uses CuO, producing removable CuS. These are course descriptions, not a claim that every modern refinery uses the same treatment.

Heat the crude to about 400°C and feed it into the lower part of a fractionating column. The column is hot below and cooler above. Rising vapours repeatedly condense and vaporise on trays/bubble caps. High-boiling components condense lower down; low-boiling components reach higher levels. Withdraw each fraction at its appropriate level.

From top to bottom the notes list uncondensed gases, petroleum ether, gasoline (petrol), naphtha, kerosene, diesel and heavy oils. Further treatment/vacuum distillation of heavy material yields lubricating oils, waxes and heavy residues such as asphalt. Straight-run petrol is the gasoline obtained directly by this fractional distillation, before cracking or reforming.

Note: the source uses “naphthalene” for cycloparaffins; the correct class name is naphthenes. Naphthalene itself is aromatic.

(b) Cracking and the fluidized-bed catalytic process

Cracking is the conversion of heavy, high-boiling hydrocarbons into smaller hydrocarbons by heat, catalysts, or both. Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₅H₁₂ + C₅H₁₀. Thermal cracking includes liquid-phase and vapour-phase processes; catalytic cracking includes fixed-bed and fluidized-bed processes.

Preheat heavy oil, vaporise it and mix it with finely powdered silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. In the reactor, the gas suspends the particles as a fluidized bed; the course example operates near 500°C and 3–5 kg cm⁻². Heavy molecules crack to lighter products. A cyclone separates catalyst from the vapours, which go to a fractionating column for product recovery.

Coke deposited on the catalyst blocks active sites, reducing catalytic activity. Send spent catalyst to the regenerator and burn off coke in air (about 600°C in this example). Return hot regenerated catalyst to fresh feed; its heat helps sustain cracking. The process improves gasoline yield/quality and supports continuous operation.

Note: “fluidized bed” and “moving bed” are treated together in the question, but they are distinct reactor arrangements. This answer describes the fluidized powder/circulating-catalyst arrangement in the supplied notes. The deposit burned off is coke, not “coal embedded in catalyst”.

Essay · 10What is a secondary cell? Explain the working of Lithium-ion battery and mention its applications. [BL-2]+

Construction: graphite negative electrode, lithium cobalt oxide positive electrode, a non-aqueous lithium-salt electrolyte and a porous separator.

Discharging

  • Lithium ions move through the electrolyte from the negative electrode to the positive electrode.
  • Electrons flow through the external load, producing electrical current.

Negative electrode

\[\mathrm{LiC_6\rightarrow C_6+Li^++e^-}\]

Positive electrode

\[\mathrm{CoO_2+Li^++e^-\rightarrow LiCoO_2}\]

Overall discharge reaction

\[\boxed{\mathrm{LiC_6+CoO_2\rightarrow C_6+LiCoO_2}}\]

The separator prevents direct contact between the electrodes while allowing ionic conduction. During discharge, graphite is the anode and the positive electrode is the cathode; oxidation and reduction occur at these respective electrodes.

Charging: an external supply reverses these reactions and returns lithium ions to the graphite electrode.

08 Gaseous Fuels & Battery Tchnology.pdf

Essay · 4Which is much preferred among the three-LPG,CNG, & Hythane? [BL-3]+

LPG, CNG and Hythane

Calorific values must use the same gross/net and mass/volume basis. Hydrogen enrichment raises mass-specific energy but lowers energy per normal gas volume compared with methane. LPG has convenient liquid storage; CNG is established for transport; Hythane is useful in suitably adapted low-emission fleets. There is no universally preferred fuel independent of cost, storage, infrastructure and safety requirements.

Unit_3_Notes.pdf

Short answer · 1Classify fuels on the basis of their physical state with examples.+

Classification of fuels

A fuel releases useful heat on combustion. By physical state: solid—wood, coal and charcoal; liquid—petroleum, petrol, diesel and kerosene; gaseous—natural gas, LPG, CNG, coal gas and water gas. LPG is stored as a pressurised liquid but used as a gaseous fuel.

Natural/primary fuels occur in nature, for example coal, crude petroleum and natural gas. Secondary/manufactured fuels are obtained by processing, for example coke, petrol and coal gas.

Short answer · 3Why gaseous fuels are recommended in the place of solid and liquid fuels?+

Gaseous fuels mix readily with air, burn with controllable flames, normally leave negligible ash, and permit easy starting/stopping. They reduce manual handling and can give cleaner combustion, but require safe leak control and appropriate storage or pipelines.

Short answer · 4Explain shortly the role of coal in power plants?+

Coal is burnt in a boiler to produce steam. The steam drives a turbine coupled to an electrical generator; the steam is then condensed and the water returned to the boiler. Coal thus supplies the heat for electricity generation.

Short answer · 7Mention the criteria in the selection of a good coal.+

Good coal should have high useful calorific value, low moisture, ash and sulphur, and suitable volatile matter and fixed carbon for the intended furnace. Selection also considers availability, cost, combustion behaviour and pollution requirements.

Short answer · 11What is Knocking? How can it be prevented?+

Knocking in a petrol engine is sudden autoignition of unburnt end-gas, causing pressure waves and a metallic sound. Use suitable high-octane fuel, correct ignition timing, effective cooling and an appropriate compression ratio to reduce it.

Short answer · 15Why a small quantity of ethyl bromide is added to petrol along with anti-knocking agent TEL?+

Ethyl bromide was used as a scavenger with tetraethyl lead. It helps convert lead-containing combustion deposits into more volatile lead bromide, reducing deposits on engine parts. This describes historical leaded petrol, not modern unleaded-fuel practice.

Short answer · 18State the conditions under which fluid bed catalytic cracking occur?+

The fluidized-bed example operates at about 500°C and 3–5 kg/cm² using finely divided silica–alumina/aluminosilicate catalyst. Vapour suspends the catalyst particles; spent catalyst is regenerated by burning off coke.

Short answer · 20Give reasons for the need of alternate fuels to fossil fuels.+

Fossil fuels are exhaustible, unevenly distributed and cause CO₂ and other pollutant emissions. Alternative energy sources improve supply security, diversify energy availability and can reduce pollution and dependence on fossil fuels.

Short answer · 21List out the different non-conventional sources of energy.+

Non-conventional energy sources include solar, wind, biomass/biogas, geothermal, tidal and wave energy. They help diversify supply and reduce dependence on exhaustible fossil fuels.

Short answer · 22Illustrate how wind can contribute to the energy production.+

Wind rotates turbine blades, converting its kinetic energy into shaft rotation. A generator converts the mechanical energy into electricity. Wind is renewable and requires no fuel, but output varies with wind speed and site conditions.

Short answer · 23Name the energy produced due to the decay of radioactive materials and mention its advantages.+

Radioactive decay releases nuclear energy, mainly as heat. Radioisotope generators convert decay heat to electricity for long-lived remote or space power. Advantages are high energy density and continuous output without sunlight; radiation protection and safe containment are essential.

Short answer · 24Mention the features of a good battery.+

Features of a useful battery

Useful battery features include suitable voltage and capacity, high energy/power density for its task, low self-discharge and good shelf life, safe operation, acceptable cost and temperature range, and reliable output. A secondary battery additionally needs useful cycle life and charge efficiency. Rechargeability is not a feature of every primary cell.

Short answer · 26Why is lithium ion battery called as future battery?+

Lithium-ion batteries combine high energy density, low self-discharge and useful rechargeable cycle life in a compact, lightweight form. They power phones, laptops, electric vehicles and storage systems. “King/future battery” describes these advantages, not superiority for every application.

Short answer · 27Give the applications of Zn-air battery.+

Zinc–air batteries are used in hearing aids, small medical devices and other compact portable equipment. They offer high energy density by obtaining oxygen from the surrounding air.

Short answer · 30Substantiate your reason for solar cells being the need of hour in the day to day to life.+

Solar cell

A solar cell converts light directly to electricity through the photovoltaic effect. In a semiconductor p–n junction, absorbed photons generate electron–hole pairs. The junction’s built-in electric field separates carriers, and contacts collect them to produce current through an external load. No fuel combustion is involved. Uses include rooftop/utility power, satellites, solar lighting, calculators and remote power supplies. Output depends on illumination, temperature and device efficiency; energy storage is needed for supply when sunlight is unavailable.

General chemistry/energy knowledge supplies the explanation here: the supplied packet asks this question but does not provide sufficient explanatory notes for it.

Essay · 7
a) What are non-conventional sources of energy? (2M)
b) Discuss the advantages and disadvantages of Geothermal and biomass energy. (6M)+

Conventional and alternative energy

Coal-fired power plants burn coal to heat boiler water into steam; steam drives a turbine coupled to a generator. Condensation and feedwater return complete the cycle. Useful coal requires suitable calorific value, low undesirable moisture/ash/sulphur and combustion properties matched to the plant.

Fossil fuels are finite, unevenly distributed and emit CO₂ and other pollutants. Diversification can improve supply security and reduce pollution. Non-conventional alternatives discussed in older course notes include solar, wind, biomass, geothermal, tidal and wave energy. Renewable does not mean impact-free.

Wind turbines convert moving-air energy into rotor mechanical energy and then generator output. Wind is fuel-free and low-emission during operation, but variable/site-dependent; grid integration, storage, land/ecology and noise require management. Biomass converts organic matter by combustion, digestion (biogas) or other processing. It can use wastes and be storable, but logistics, air emissions, land competition and sustainable regrowth matter; it is not automatically carbon-neutral.

Geothermal systems use underground heat, sometimes producing steam/hot fluid to drive turbines or supply heat. Benefits include firm output at suitable sites and low operating emissions; limitations include site dependence, drilling cost, corrosive fluids and possible induced seismicity.

The question about energy “due to decay of radioactive materials” needs precision: radioactive decay releases nuclear energy; radioisotope generators can convert decay heat into electricity for long-lived remote/space power. Ordinary nuclear power stations principally use controlled fission, not spontaneous decay alone. Advantages include high energy density and reliable output; radiation protection, waste and cost remain concerns. This distinction is a general-chemistry clarification of the question’s wording.

General chemistry/energy knowledge supplies the explanation here: the supplied packet asks this question but does not provide sufficient explanatory notes for it.

Essay · 8Outline the limitations of fossil fuels and discuss the advantages and disadvantages of wind energy and biomass.+

Conventional and alternative energy

Coal-fired power plants burn coal to heat boiler water into steam; steam drives a turbine coupled to a generator. Condensation and feedwater return complete the cycle. Useful coal requires suitable calorific value, low undesirable moisture/ash/sulphur and combustion properties matched to the plant.

Fossil fuels are finite, unevenly distributed and emit CO₂ and other pollutants. Diversification can improve supply security and reduce pollution. Non-conventional alternatives discussed in older course notes include solar, wind, biomass, geothermal, tidal and wave energy. Renewable does not mean impact-free.

It can use wastes and be storable, but logistics, air emissions, land competition and sustainable regrowth matter; it is not automatically carbon-neutral.

Essay · 10
a) Write short note on Zinc air battery, its features and applications? (4M)
b) What is a solar cell? List out its applications? (4M)+

(a) Zinc–air battery

Construction: zinc powder/gel is the negative electrode; a porous carbon/catalyst air electrode is the positive electrode. A separator prevents shorting. Aqueous KOH (about 30% in this example) conducts ions, and gas-permeable layers admit atmospheric oxygen while managing electrolyte.

During discharge, zinc is oxidised and oxygen is reduced. A convenient balanced form is: anode Zn + 2OH⁻ → ZnO + H₂O + 2e⁻; cathode ½O₂ + H₂O + 2e⁻ → 2OH⁻. Overall: Zn + ½O₂ → ZnO. Electrons pass through the external load from zinc to the air electrode.

High energy density and low material cost follow partly from obtaining oxygen externally. Sealed shelf life is long, but life after opening the air seal and power capability are limited. Uses include hearing aids and small medical/portable devices. A standard primary hearing-aid zinc–air cell should not be assumed rechargeable; specialised rechargeable metal–air systems are different designs.

(b) Solar cell

A solar cell converts light directly to electricity through the photovoltaic effect. In a semiconductor p–n junction, absorbed photons generate electron–hole pairs. The junction’s built-in electric field separates carriers, and contacts collect them to produce current through an external load. No fuel combustion is involved. Uses include rooftop/utility power, satellites, solar lighting, calculators and remote power supplies. Output depends on illumination, temperature and device efficiency; energy storage is needed for supply when sunlight is unavailable.

General chemistry/energy knowledge supplies the explanation here: the supplied packet asks this question but does not provide sufficient explanatory notes for it.

Unit 4 · Polymers

UNIT-4 Polymers QB .docx.pdf

Short answer · 1Define polymerization with suitable examples. [BL-1]+

Polymerisation and its types

Polymerisation joins monomer molecules into macromolecules containing repeating units. In the course addition examples, unsaturated monomers add to a growing chain without loss of small molecules: nCH₂=CH₂ → [–CH₂–CH₂–]ₙ; tetrafluoroethylene similarly forms PTFE.

Short answer · 2Describe the polymerization involved in thermosetting plastics with example. [BL-2]+

Polymerisation and its types

Condensation polymerisation joins multifunctional monomers with elimination of small molecules such as water or HCl. A diamine plus a dicarboxylic acid gives polyamide links; phenol–formaldehyde condensation can form a cross-linked thermosetting network. Stepwise reactions first form oligomers and then higher-molar-mass chains/networks.

Short answer · 3Explain copolymerization with suitable example. [BL-1]+

Polymerisation and its types

Copolymerisation uses two or more monomer species. Buna-S is a copolymer of styrene and 1,3-butadiene. Addition/condensation describes the reaction route; thermoplastic/thermoset describes thermal/network behaviour. A condensation polymer such as nylon can be thermoplastic, so the classifications must not be equated.

Short answer · 4Thermoplastics are weaker than thermosets. Justify. [BL-3]+

Thermoplastics and thermosets

Thermoplastics usually have linear/branched chains held together by intermolecular forces. They soften/melt on heating and can be reshaped within suitable processing limits; examples include PE, PVC, nylon and PTFE. Thermosets form a covalently cross-linked three-dimensional network during curing. Cured Bakelite/epoxy cannot simply be melted and remoulded; excessive heating causes degradation.

Networks restrict chain motion, often providing dimensional stability, rigidity and creep/heat resistance. Thermoplastics can often be reprocessed, whereas cured thermosets normally require other recycling routes. “Thermoplastics are weaker” is not a universal rule: strength depends on chemistry, reinforcement, orientation, temperature and the property measured.

Short answer · 5Teflon is an addition polymer but behaves as a thermoset. Give reasons. [BL-3]+
Source discrepancy

The question says PTFE behaves as a thermoset. PTFE is a thermoplastic; its high melt viscosity explains difficult conventional melt processing, not a thermoset network.

Thermoplastics and thermosets

Correction: PTFE is an addition thermoplastic, not a thermoset. Its extremely high melt viscosity makes ordinary melt-flow processing difficult; it is commonly shaped by pressing/sintering or specialised methods. Difficult processing does not imply a covalently cross-linked thermoset network. Nylon is also a thermoplastic, despite its condensation synthesis.

Short answer · 6Write the monomers involved in the preparation of the plastic used in making tires & bullet proof vests. [BL-3]+

Kevlar: preparation, structure, properties and applications

Tyres: Buna-S, made from 1,3-butadiene (CH₂=CH–CH=CH₂) and styrene (C₆H₅–CH=CH₂). Bullet-proof vests: Kevlar, an aromatic polyamide from p-phenylenediamine and terephthaloyl chloride in the practical condensation route. The simplified scheme substitutes terephthalic acid and eliminates water; do not confuse the two preparation schemes.

Short answer · 7Suggest suitable materials for the following applications. [BL-3] 1) Nonstick cook ware 2) Shoe soles 3) Body armor+

(1) Non-stick cookware

PTFE (Teflon): chemically resistant, low-friction and low-surface-energy coating.

(2) Shoe soles

Buna-S (styrene–butadiene rubber): useful abrasion resistance and elasticity after suitable compounding/vulcanisation.

(3) Body armour

Kevlar (aramid fibre): high tensile strength relative to mass and useful energy absorption in an appropriately engineered fabric/composite.

Short answer · 8Conducting polymers are used in electrochromic displays and optical filters. Justify? [BL-3]+

Conducting polymers: classification, doping and uses

Redox/doping changes can reversibly change optical absorption/colour, enabling electrochromic displays and optical filters. Other applications include antistatic coatings, sensors, flexible electronics, battery electrodes, corrosion-protection coatings and photovoltaic devices.

Short answer · 9List out the features responsible for the extensive use of FRPs in various sectors? [BL-3]+

Fibre-reinforced plastics

FRPs combine a polymer matrix (for example epoxy/polyester) with fibres such as glass, carbon or aramid. Fibres carry much of the directional load; the matrix binds and positions them, transfers stress and protects them from the environment. Reinforcement can be continuous/unidirectional, woven or chopped.

Advantages: high strength/stiffness relative to mass, corrosion resistance, fatigue performance and freedom to form complex shapes. Limitations: anisotropy, delamination/impact damage, repair/recycling difficulty and temperature limits set by the matrix. Uses: aircraft/vehicle components, boat hulls, sports goods, chemical tanks/pipes, wind-turbine components and structural strengthening. No universal claim that every FRP is stronger/cheaper than every metal is justified.

Short answer · 10What are Shape memory polymers? How do they differ from traditional polymers? [BL-3]+

Shape-memory polymers

A shape-memory polymer (SMP) can be programmed into a temporary shape and recover a permanent shape when stimulated. Permanent netpoints/cross-links preserve the reference shape; switching segments fix and release the temporary shape through a transition such as glass transition or melting.

Thermal cycle: heat above the switching transition, deform to a temporary shape, cool under constraint to fix it, then remove the constraint. Reheat to activate segmental mobility and recover the permanent shape. Unlike ordinary plastic deformation, the temporary deformation is deliberately recoverable.

Short answer · 11List out the reasons for the need for biodegradable polymers.+

Biodegradable polymers and PLA

Biodegradable polymers break down through biological activity under suitable conditions. They can reduce persistent waste where appropriate collection/treatment is available, and allow temporary biomedical devices that gradually resorb. Biodegradable does not mean instant, harmless degradation in every environment.

Polymerisation and its types

Essay · 3Give a comparative account of thermoplastics and thermosets. [BL-2]+

Thermoplastics and thermosets

Thermoplastics usually have linear/branched chains held together by intermolecular forces. They soften/melt on heating and can be reshaped within suitable processing limits; examples include PE, PVC, nylon and PTFE. Essay · 4Write the preparation, properties, and uses of Teflon/Buna S/ Kevlar. [BL-2] OR+

PTFE: preparation, properties and applications

Monomer: tetrafluoroethylene, CF₂=CF₂. Free-radical addition polymerisation under controlled pressure using an appropriate initiator gives nCF₂=CF₂ → [–CF₂–CF₂–]ₙ. The strong C–F bonds and shielding fluorine atoms contribute to chemical inertness and a low-energy non-stick surface.

Properties include chemical resistance, low friction, useful thermal resistance and excellent electrical insulation. PTFE is a thermoplastic with high melt viscosity. Uses: non-stick cookware coatings, chemical-resistant liners, gaskets/seals, bearings and wire/cable insulation. The source’s quoted high temperature is not a safe universal continuous-service rating; actual service limits are grade/design dependent.

Why it adheres to a pan: the coating is attached by engineered surface preparation and primer/binder systems, often with mechanical anchoring into a roughened/treated substrate, then cured/sintered. Its low surface adhesion to food does not imply that a specially engineered coating cannot be bonded to a substrate.

Buna-S: preparation, properties and applications

Monomers are 1,3-butadiene (CH₂=CH–CH=CH₂) and styrene (C₆H₅–CH=CH₂). Emulsion copolymerisation, traditionally about 75:25 butadiene:styrene in this example with an initiator around 50°C, produces styrene–butadiene rubber (SBR/Buna-S). Conditions and microstructure vary by grade.

The butadiene-derived segments provide flexible, vulcanisable unsaturation; styrene units improve useful strength/abrasion behaviour. Vulcanisation introduces cross-links to improve elasticity and service performance. It has useful abrasion resistance and load-bearing properties, but can swell in organic solvents and suffer ozone attack. Uses: tyres, shoe soles, hoses, conveyor belts, gaskets and cable insulation.

Kevlar: preparation, structure, properties and applications

Kevlar is an aromatic polyamide (aramid), poly(p-phenylene terephthalamide). Monomers for the practical condensation route are p-phenylenediamine, H₂N–C₆H₄–NH₂, and terephthaloyl chloride, ClCO–C₆H₄–COCl. They form [–NH–C₆H₄–NH–CO–C₆H₄–CO–]ₙ with elimination of HCl (chain ends neglected in the repeating-unit scheme).

The simplified scheme uses terephthalic acid instead of the acid chloride and eliminates water; it identifies the same aromatic amide backbone but should not be mistaken for the industrial reaction conditions. Rigid para-linked aromatic chains, alignment and intermolecular hydrogen bonding give high tensile strength relative to mass.

Properties: high strength/stiffness, low density, heat/flame resistance and low electrical conductivity. UV exposure and some chemical environments can damage it. Uses: ballistic vests/helmets, ropes, cables, tyre reinforcement, aerospace/sporting composites and protective clothing. For the applications question: non-stick cookware—PTFE; shoe soles/tyres—Buna-S; body armour—Kevlar. The tyre material and body-armour material are different polymers, not one plastic.

Essay · 6Define conducting polymers. How are they classified? Discuss their applications. [BL-2]+

Conducting polymers conduct electricity through their conjugated backbone or incorporated conducting materials.

Classification

  • Intrinsically conducting polymers: possess conjugated double bonds. Examples: polyacetylene, polyaniline and polypyrrole. Doping increases conductivity.
  • p-type doping removes electrons; n-type doping adds electrons.
  • Extrinsically conducting polymers: insulating polymers made conducting by adding carbon, metal particles or conducting polymers.

In intrinsically conducting polymers, overlapping p-orbitals along the conjugated chain permit electron delocalisation. Doping creates additional mobile charge carriers. In extrinsic materials, conducting fillers or conducting-polymer blends provide connected paths for current.

Applications: antistatic coatings, sensors, battery electrodes, electrochromic displays and flexible electronic devices.

Essay · 7Give a detailed account of features and applications of FRPs. [BL-2]+

Fibre-reinforced plastics

FRPs combine a polymer matrix (for example epoxy/polyester) with fibres such as glass, carbon or aramid. Fibres carry much of the directional load; the matrix binds and positions them, transfers stress and protects them from the environment. Reinforcement can be continuous/unidirectional, woven or chopped.

Glass FRP is relatively economical with useful strength and corrosion resistance; carbon FRP offers high specific stiffness/strength; aramid FRP offers low mass and useful impact resistance. Fibre orientation, fibre fraction and fibre–matrix bonding control performance.

Advantages: high strength/stiffness relative to mass, corrosion resistance, fatigue performance and freedom to form complex shapes. Limitations: anisotropy, delamination/impact damage, repair/recycling difficulty and temperature limits set by the matrix. Uses: aircraft/vehicle components, boat hulls, sports goods, chemical tanks/pipes, wind-turbine components and structural strengthening. No universal claim that every FRP is stronger/cheaper than every metal is justified.

Essay · 8What are Shape memory polymers? Explain the features and applications of shape memory polymers? [BL-2]+

Shape-memory polymers retain a temporary shape and recover their permanent shape when exposed to a suitable stimulus.

Working: heat above the transition temperature, deform and cool under constraint to fix the temporary shape. Reheating restores the permanent shape.

Class / stimulusExample or application
Thermally activatedSelf-deploying structures and actuators
Electrically activatedConducting-filler composites for actuators and robotic grippers
Magnetically activatedMagnetic-particle composites heated by a magnetic field
Light activatedLight-responsive actuators
Water activatedA programmed bent rod recovering its permanent shape in water

Permanent cross-links or netpoints retain the original shape. Switching segments fix the temporary shape on cooling and regain mobility on stimulation, allowing recovery. Electrical and magnetic activation often work by heating the polymer through conducting fillers or magnetic particles.

Suitable biomedical SMPs are used or investigated for catheters, stents and drug-delivery devices.

Essay · 9What are biodegradable polymers? Elaborate on Polylactic Acid (PLA) as an environmentally friendly polymer (key characteristics & applications).+

Biodegradable polymers break down through biological activity under suitable environmental conditions.

Features

  • Reduce accumulation of persistent polymer waste.
  • Contain linkages that can undergo degradation.
  • Suitable grades can form temporary biomedical products.
  • Degradation depends on moisture, temperature and biological conditions.

Polylactic acid (PLA) is a biodegradable polyester made from lactic acid obtained by fermentation of carbohydrates.

Repeating unit

\[\mathrm{[{-}O{-}CH(CH_3){-}CO{-}]_n}\]

PLA contains ester linkages that can undergo hydrolysis during degradation. It is a thermoplastic with useful stiffness and transparency. Suitable composting conditions are important; it does not disappear rapidly in every environment. Biomedical grades are useful where gradual resorption is required.

Applications: packaging films, disposable cups, 3D-printing filament, surgical sutures, drug-delivery systems and tissue scaffolds.

Unit 4 questions.pdf

Short answer · 1Classify polymers based on degree of polymerization. [BL-2]+

Degree of polymerisation (DP) is the number of repeating units in a polymer chain. The course classification calls DP below 600 oligo polymers and DP above 600 high polymers. This cutoff is a course convention rather than a universal boundary.

Short answer · 2Define polymerization with suitable examples. [BL-1]+

Polymerisation and its types

Polymerisation joins monomer molecules into macromolecules containing repeating units. In the course addition examples, unsaturated monomers add to a growing chain without loss of small molecules: nCH₂=CH₂ → [–CH₂–CH₂–]ₙ; tetrafluoroethylene similarly forms PTFE.

Short answer · 5What is crystallinity? Mention the factors affecting crystallinity. [BL-2]+

Crystallinity is the extent of ordered packing of polymer chains. Regular, symmetrical chains with small side groups pack readily; branching, bulky groups and irregular stereochemistry reduce packing. Intermolecular attraction and cooling conditions also affect crystallinity.

Short answer · 6Define glass transition temperature. How does it vary with molecular weight and steric effects? BL-2+

Glass-transition temperature (Tg) is the temperature range in which the amorphous portion changes from hard and glassy to soft and rubbery. Tg generally increases with molecular weight towards a limiting value. Bulky groups that restrict chain rotation usually raise Tg; flexible side chains can lower it.

Short answer · 7What is glassy state? In which polymers it is observed? [BL-2]+

The glassy state is the hard, rigid and relatively brittle state below Tg, where large-scale segmental motion is restricted. It occurs in amorphous polymers and in the amorphous regions of semi-crystalline polymers.

Short answer · 8Thermoplastics are weaker than thermosetting resins. Justify. [BL-3]+

Cross-links in thermosets restrict chain movement and often give greater rigidity, dimensional stability and heat resistance. Thermoplastics have less cross-linking and soften on heating. Strength is not universally lower in every thermoplastic; it depends on the polymer and the property compared.

Short answer · 9Compare and contrast compression moulding against extrusion moulding. [BL-2]+
Compression mouldingExtrusion moulding
A measured charge is pressed in a heated mouldA screw forces softened polymer continuously through a shaped die
Makes discrete moulded partsMakes continuous constant-cross-section products such as pipes
Often used for thermosets and some thermoplasticsCommonly used for thermoplastics

Short answer · 11Write the monomers involved in the preparation of the plastic used in making transparencies for overhead projector and storage of carbonated beverages. [BL-3]+

Both overhead-projector transparency film and carbonated-beverage bottles use PET, prepared from ethylene glycol and terephthalic acid.

Short answer · 12Which type of polymerization occurs in polycarbonate? Give its monomers. [BL-2]+

Polycarbonate is formed by condensation polymerisation. A common route uses bisphenol A and phosgene (carbonyl chloride), producing carbonate linkages with elimination of HCl.

Short answer · 13Justify the use of PMMA as an alternative to glass. [BL-3]+

PMMA is transparent, lightweight, weather-resistant and easy to shape. It transmits visible light well and resists shattering better than ordinary glass, so it is used in windows, lenses and display panels. It scratches more readily than glass.

Short answer · 14What is the role of hexamethylenetetramine in the preparation of Bakelite? [BL-2]+

Hexamethylenetetramine acts as a curing/cross-linking agent for novolac. On heating it provides reactive groups that form methylene bridges between phenolic chains, converting the resin into a hard, infusible three-dimensional Bakelite network.

Short answer · 15Bakelite is used in the electrical appliances. Give reason. [BL-2]+

Bakelite is a good electrical insulator and is rigid, heat-resistant and dimensionally stable after curing. These properties make it useful for switches, plugs and other electrical fittings.
