Short answer · 19Which FRP cannot be used as structural composite material? Why? [BL-3]+

No FRP type is universally excluded from structural use. Glass-, carbon- and aramid-fibre composites all have structural applications. GFRP has lower stiffness than CFRP and may be unsuitable where very high stiffness at low weight is essential; suitability depends on the design requirements.

Short answer · 20List out the features of FRPs? [BL-2]+

Features

  • High strength relative to weight.
  • Good corrosion resistance and durability.
  • Properties can be tailored by fibre type, orientation and matrix.
  • Can be moulded into complex shapes.
Short answer · 21Define Shape memory polymers? Give examples. [BL-1]+

Shape-memory polymers can be fixed in a temporary shape and recover their permanent shape under a suitable stimulus. Examples include thermally activated polyurethane SMPs and water-responsive SMPs.

Short answer · 22What can shape memory polymers be used for? [BL-2]+

SMPs are used for actuators, self-deploying structures, robotic grippers, responsive textiles and suitable biomedical devices such as catheters and stents. Their recoverable programmed deformation enables these applications.

Essay · 2Distinguish between addition and condensation polymerization. [BL-2]+

Polymerisation and its types

Polymerisation joins monomer molecules into macromolecules containing repeating units. In the course addition examples, unsaturated monomers add to a growing chain without loss of small molecules: nCH₂=CH₂ → [–CH₂–CH₂–]ₙ; tetrafluoroethylene similarly forms PTFE.

Condensation polymerisation joins multifunctional monomers with elimination of small molecules such as water or HCl. A diamine plus a dicarboxylic acid gives polyamide links; phenol–formaldehyde condensation can form a cross-linked thermosetting network. Stepwise reactions first form oligomers and then higher-molar-mass chains/networks.

Copolymerisation uses two or more monomer species. Buna-S is a copolymer of styrene and 1,3-butadiene. Addition/condensation describes the reaction route; thermoplastic/thermoset describes thermal/network behaviour. A condensation polymer such as nylon can be thermoplastic, so the classifications must not be equated.

Essay · 3Describe a method for moulding of thermoplastics with a neat diagram. [BL-3]+

Extrusion moulding is a continuous method for forming thermoplastics into products of uniform cross-section.

The diagram shows the wire-coating arrangement. The same screw-and-die principle produces pipes, rods and other continuous profiles with a suitable die.

Procedure

  • Feed polymer granules into a hopper above a heated barrel.
  • A rotating screw conveys, compresses and melts the material.
  • Force the melt through a die of the required cross-section.
  • Cool and solidify the extrudate, then pull it forward and cut or wind it.

Products: pipes, rods, sheets and wire insulation. Advantages include continuous production and high output; the cross-section is controlled by the die.

Essay · 4Discuss the fabrication process involved in food packaging industry/uniform cross- sectional objects, with a neat diagram. [BL-3]+

Extrusion produces continuous uniform-cross-section items and packaging film. The material is melted in a heated barrel and forced by a screw through a die, then cooled and collected.

The diagram shows the wire-coating arrangement. The same screw-and-die principle produces pipes, rods and other continuous profiles with a suitable die.

A flat die produces sheet/film; an annular die can produce a tube that is expanded and cooled to make blown film. For hollow packaging containers, blow moulding is used.

Blow-moulding steps

  • Make a hot polymer tube or preform.
  • Close the split mould around it.
  • Blow compressed air inside so the polymer expands against the mould wall.
  • Cool, open the mould and remove the hollow article.

Applications: food/drink containers, bottles and jars. Thermoplastics such as PE, PP and PET are selected to suit the process.

Essay · 5Write the preparation, properties, and uses of Teflon. [BL-2]+

Teflon (PTFE) is prepared by addition polymerisation of tetrafluoroethylene under pressure, using a suitable free-radical initiator such as benzoyl peroxide.

Polymerisation

\[\mathrm{n\,CF_2{=}CF_2\rightarrow[{-}CF_2{-}CF_2{-}]_n}\]

Properties

  • Chemically inert and resistant to many corrosive chemicals.
  • Low coefficient of friction and non-stick surface.
  • Good electrical insulation and low water absorption.
  • Good thermal resistance; allowable service temperature depends on grade and conditions.
  • A thermoplastic with very high melt viscosity, commonly shaped by pressing and sintering.

Uses: non-stick cookware coatings, chemical-resistant liners, gaskets, seals, bearings, and wire/cable insulation. Its non-adhesive surface suits cookware, chemical resistance suits liners, and electrical insulation suits cables.

Essay · 6Explain compression moulding process with a neat diagram? [BL-2]+

Compression moulding shapes a measured polymer charge using heat and pressure in a mould. It is widely used for thermosetting resins and can also process some thermoplastics.

Procedure

  • Place the measured charge in the heated lower mould cavity.
  • Close the upper mould and apply pressure so the charge flows into the cavity.
  • Maintain heat and pressure until the thermoset cures; a thermoplastic is cooled sufficiently to set.
  • Open the mould, eject the article and trim flash.

Advantages: relatively simple tooling, good finish and suitability for large or thick parts. Limitations: slower cycles, flash and less suitability for very complex shapes. Applications: switches, plugs, handles and moulded trays.

Essay · 7Discuss about the preparation, properties, and uses of PET. [BL-1]+

PET (polyethylene terephthalate) is a condensation polyester prepared from ethylene glycol and terephthalic acid, with elimination of water.

Repeating unit

\[\mathrm{[{-}O{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}O{-}CO{-}C_6H_4{-}CO{-}]_n}\]

Properties

  • Strong, lightweight thermoplastic with good chemical resistance.
  • Can be transparent and has useful gas-barrier properties.
  • Can be drawn into strong fibres.

Uses: beverage bottles, food packaging, polyester fibres and photographic/transparency films.

Essay · 8Write down the preparation, properties, and uses of the following polymers. [BL-2]
i) Polycarbonate
ii) PMMA+

(i) Polycarbonate: prepared by condensation of bisphenol A with phosgene, forming carbonate linkages. It is transparent, tough, impact-resistant and dimensionally stable. Uses include safety glazing, protective shields, optical parts and electronic housings.

(ii) PMMA: prepared by addition polymerisation of methyl methacrylate.

PMMA formation

\[\mathrm{n\,CH_2{=}C(CH_3)COOCH_3\rightarrow[{-}CH_2{-}C(CH_3)(COOCH_3){-}]_n}\]

PMMA is transparent, lightweight, weather-resistant and easy to mould, but relatively scratch-sensitive. Uses: acrylic windows, display panels, signs, lenses and rigid contact lenses.

Essay · 9Explain the preparation properties and uses of Bakelite/phenol formaldehyde resin. [BL-1]+

Bakelite is a thermosetting phenol–formaldehyde resin prepared by condensation polymerisation.

Preparation

  • Phenol reacts with formaldehyde at ortho and para positions to form hydroxymethyl phenols.
  • Condensation forms methylene bridges between phenolic rings, eliminating water.
  • Acid catalysis with limited formaldehyde gives novolac; curing with hexamethylenetetramine forms the cross-linked network.
  • Under suitable basic conditions, a resol intermediate can cure on heating to the three-dimensional thermoset.

Methylene-bridge formation (simplified)

\[\mathrm{2\,C_6H_5OH+CH_2O\rightarrow HO{-}C_6H_4{-}CH_2{-}C_6H_4{-}OH+H_2O}\]

Properties: hard, rigid, infusible after curing, heat-resistant and electrically insulating. Uses: switches, plugs, appliance handles, laminates and adhesives.

Further reactions at available ortho/para positions build a rigid three-dimensional network. Curing is irreversible: the finished resin cannot be melted and remoulded like a thermoplastic. The restricted chain motion gives dimensional stability, while electrical insulation and heat resistance suit electrical fittings and handles.

Essay · 10Discuss the following properties of polymers crystallinity and glass transition temperatures. [BL-2]+

Crystallinity is the fraction of ordered regions formed by closely packed polymer chains. Most crystalline polymers also contain amorphous regions.

Factors affecting crystallinity

  • Regularity, symmetry and suitable stereochemistry favour packing.
  • Branching, irregular sequences and bulky side groups hinder packing.
  • Intermolecular forces and cooling/annealing conditions affect the ordered fraction.

Greater crystallinity generally increases density, stiffness and resistance to solvent penetration, while often reducing transparency and flexibility.

Glass transition is the change of the amorphous portion from glassy/rigid to rubbery/mobile as temperature rises through Tg. It differs from melting of crystalline regions.

Factors affecting Tg

  • Greater chain rigidity, strong intermolecular attraction and cross-linking usually raise Tg.
  • Plasticisers and increased chain flexibility generally lower Tg.
  • Increasing molecular weight raises Tg towards a limiting value.
  • Bulky groups that hinder rotation often raise Tg, though flexible side chains can have the opposite effect.
Essay · 11Define conducting polymers. How are they classified? Discuss their applications. [BL-2]+

Conducting polymers conduct electricity through their conjugated backbone or incorporated conducting materials.

Classification

  • Intrinsically conducting polymers: possess conjugated double bonds. Examples: polyacetylene, polyaniline and polypyrrole. Doping increases conductivity.
  • p-type doping removes electrons; n-type doping adds electrons.
  • Extrinsically conducting polymers: insulating polymers made conducting by adding carbon, metal particles or conducting polymers.

In intrinsically conducting polymers, overlapping p-orbitals along the conjugated chain permit electron delocalisation. Doping creates additional mobile charge carriers. In extrinsic materials, conducting fillers or conducting-polymer blends provide connected paths for current.

Applications: antistatic coatings, sensors, battery electrodes, electrochromic displays and flexible electronic devices.

Essay · 12Give a detailed account of features and applications of FRPs. [BL-2]+

Fibre-reinforced plastics

FRPs combine a polymer matrix (for example epoxy/polyester) with fibres such as glass, carbon or aramid. Fibres carry much of the directional load; the matrix binds and positions them, transfers stress and protects them from the environment. Reinforcement can be continuous/unidirectional, woven or chopped.

Glass FRP is relatively economical with useful strength and corrosion resistance; carbon FRP offers high specific stiffness/strength; aramid FRP offers low mass and useful impact resistance. Fibre orientation, fibre fraction and fibre–matrix bonding control performance.

Advantages: high strength/stiffness relative to mass, corrosion resistance, fatigue performance and freedom to form complex shapes. Limitations: anisotropy, delamination/impact damage, repair/recycling difficulty and temperature limits set by the matrix. Uses: aircraft/vehicle components, boat hulls, sports goods, chemical tanks/pipes, wind-turbine components and structural strengthening. No universal claim that every FRP is stronger/cheaper than every metal is justified.

Essay · 13What are Shape memory polymers? Explain the features and applications of shape memory polymers? [BL-2]+

Shape-memory polymers retain a temporary shape and recover their permanent shape when exposed to a suitable stimulus.

Working: heat above the transition temperature, deform and cool under constraint to fix the temporary shape. Reheating restores the permanent shape.

Class / stimulusExample or application
Thermally activatedSelf-deploying structures and actuators
Electrically activatedConducting-filler composites for actuators and robotic grippers
Magnetically activatedMagnetic-particle composites heated by a magnetic field
Light activatedLight-responsive actuators
Water activatedA programmed bent rod recovering its permanent shape in water

Permanent cross-links or netpoints retain the original shape. Switching segments fix the temporary shape on cooling and regain mobility on stimulation, allowing recovery. Electrical and magnetic activation often work by heating the polymer through conducting fillers or magnetic particles.

Suitable biomedical SMPs are used or investigated for catheters, stents and drug-delivery devices.

Short answer · 1Define polymerization with suitable examples. [BL-1]+

A diamine plus a dicarboxylic acid gives polyamide links; phenol–formaldehyde condensation can form a cross-linked thermosetting network. Short answer · 3Explain copolymerization with suitable example. [BL-1]+

Polymerisation and its types

Short answer · 4Thermoplastics are weaker than thermosets. Justify. [BL-3]+

Thermoplastics and thermosets

Thermoplastics usually have linear/branched chains held together by intermolecular forces. They soften/melt on heating and can be reshaped within suitable processing limits; examples include PE, PVC, nylon and PTFE. Thermosets form a covalently cross-linked three-dimensional network during curing. Cured Bakelite/epoxy cannot simply be melted and remoulded; excessive heating causes degradation.

Networks restrict chain motion, often providing dimensional stability, rigidity and creep/heat resistance. Thermoplastics can often be reprocessed, whereas cured thermosets normally require other recycling routes. “Thermoplastics are weaker” is not a universal rule: strength depends on chemistry, reinforcement, orientation, temperature and the property measured.

Short answer · 5Teflon is an addition polymer but behaves as a thermoset. Give reasons. [BL-3]+
Source discrepancy

The question says PTFE behaves as a thermoset. PTFE is a thermoplastic; its high melt viscosity explains difficult conventional melt processing, not a thermoset network.

Thermoplastics and thermosets

Correction: PTFE is an addition thermoplastic, not a thermoset. Its extremely high melt viscosity makes ordinary melt-flow processing difficult; it is commonly shaped by pressing/sintering or specialised methods. Difficult processing does not imply a covalently cross-linked thermoset network. Nylon is also a thermoplastic, despite its condensation synthesis.

Short answer · 6Write the monomers involved in the preparation of the plastic used in making tires & bullet proof vests. [BL-3]+

Kevlar: preparation, structure, properties and applications

Tyres: Buna-S, made from 1,3-butadiene (CH₂=CH–CH=CH₂) and styrene (C₆H₅–CH=CH₂). Bullet-proof vests: Kevlar, an aromatic polyamide from p-phenylenediamine and terephthaloyl chloride in the practical condensation route. The simplified scheme substitutes terephthalic acid and eliminates water; do not confuse the two preparation schemes.

Short answer · 7Suggest suitable materials for the following applications. [BL-3] 1) Nonstick cook ware 2) Shoe soles 3) Body armor+

(1) Non-stick cookware

PTFE (Teflon): chemically resistant, low-friction and low-surface-energy coating.

(2) Shoe soles

Buna-S (styrene–butadiene rubber): useful abrasion resistance and elasticity after suitable compounding/vulcanisation.

(3) Body armour

Kevlar (aramid fibre): high tensile strength relative to mass and useful energy absorption in an appropriately engineered fabric/composite.

Short answer · 8Conducting polymers are used in electrochromic displays and optical filters. Justify? [BL-3]+

Conducting polymers: classification, doping and uses

Redox/doping changes can reversibly change optical absorption/colour, enabling electrochromic displays and optical filters. Other applications include antistatic coatings, sensors, flexible electronics, battery electrodes, corrosion-protection coatings and photovoltaic devices.

Short answer · 9List out the features responsible for the extensive use of FRPs in various sectors? [BL-3]+

Fibre-reinforced plastics

FRPs combine a polymer matrix (for example epoxy/polyester) with fibres such as glass, carbon or aramid. Fibres carry much of the directional load; the matrix binds and positions them, transfers stress and protects them from the environment. Reinforcement can be continuous/unidirectional, woven or chopped.

Limitations: anisotropy, delamination/impact damage, repair/recycling difficulty and temperature limits set by the matrix. No universal claim that every FRP is stronger/cheaper than every metal is justified.

Short answer · 10What are Shape memory polymers? How do they differ from traditional polymers? [BL-3]+

Shape-memory polymers

A shape-memory polymer (SMP) can be programmed into a temporary shape and recover a permanent shape when stimulated. Permanent netpoints/cross-links preserve the reference shape; switching segments fix and release the temporary shape through a transition such as glass transition or melting.

Thermal cycle: heat above the switching transition, deform to a temporary shape, cool under constraint to fix it, then remove the constraint. Reheat to activate segmental mobility and recover the permanent shape. Unlike ordinary plastic deformation, the temporary deformation is deliberately recoverable.

Short answer · 11List out the reasons for the need for biodegradable polymers.+

Biodegradable polymers and PLA

Biodegradable polymers break down through biological activity under suitable conditions. They can reduce persistent waste where appropriate collection/treatment is available, and allow temporary biomedical devices that gradually resorb. Biodegradable does not mean instant, harmless degradation in every environment.

Essay · 2Distinguish between addition and condensation polymerization. [BL-2]+

Polymerisation and its types

Essay · 4Write the preparation, properties, and uses of Teflon/Buna S/ Kevlar. [BL-2] OR+

PTFE: preparation, properties and applications

Monomer: tetrafluoroethylene, CF₂=CF₂. Free-radical addition polymerisation under controlled pressure using an appropriate initiator gives nCF₂=CF₂ → [–CF₂–CF₂–]ₙ. The strong C–F bonds and shielding fluorine atoms contribute to chemical inertness and a low-energy non-stick surface.

Properties include chemical resistance, low friction, useful thermal resistance and excellent electrical insulation. PTFE is a thermoplastic with high melt viscosity. Uses: non-stick cookware coatings, chemical-resistant liners, gaskets/seals, bearings and wire/cable insulation. The source’s quoted high temperature is not a safe universal continuous-service rating; actual service limits are grade/design dependent.

Why it adheres to a pan: the coating is attached by engineered surface preparation and primer/binder systems, often with mechanical anchoring into a roughened/treated substrate, then cured/sintered. Its low surface adhesion to food does not imply that a specially engineered coating cannot be bonded to a substrate.

Buna-S: preparation, properties and applications

Monomers are 1,3-butadiene (CH₂=CH–CH=CH₂) and styrene (C₆H₅–CH=CH₂). Emulsion copolymerisation, traditionally about 75:25 butadiene:styrene in this example with an initiator around 50°C, produces styrene–butadiene rubber (SBR/Buna-S). Conditions and microstructure vary by grade.

The butadiene-derived segments provide flexible, vulcanisable unsaturation; styrene units improve useful strength/abrasion behaviour. Vulcanisation introduces cross-links to improve elasticity and service performance. It has useful abrasion resistance and load-bearing properties, but can swell in organic solvents and suffer ozone attack. Uses: tyres, shoe soles, hoses, conveyor belts, gaskets and cable insulation.

Kevlar: preparation, structure, properties and applications

Kevlar is an aromatic polyamide (aramid), poly(p-phenylene terephthalamide). Monomers for the practical condensation route are p-phenylenediamine, H₂N–C₆H₄–NH₂, and terephthaloyl chloride, ClCO–C₆H₄–COCl. They form [–NH–C₆H₄–NH–CO–C₆H₄–CO–]ₙ with elimination of HCl (chain ends neglected in the repeating-unit scheme).

The simplified scheme uses terephthalic acid instead of the acid chloride and eliminates water; it identifies the same aromatic amide backbone but should not be mistaken for the industrial reaction conditions. Rigid para-linked aromatic chains, alignment and intermolecular hydrogen bonding give high tensile strength relative to mass.

Properties: high strength/stiffness, low density, heat/flame resistance and low electrical conductivity. UV exposure and some chemical environments can damage it. Uses: ballistic vests/helmets, ropes, cables, tyre reinforcement, aerospace/sporting composites and protective clothing. For the applications question: non-stick cookware—PTFE; shoe soles/tyres—Buna-S; body armour—Kevlar. The tyre material and body-armour material are different polymers, not one plastic.

Essay · 9What are biodegradable polymers? Elaborate on Polylactic Acid (PLA) as an environmentally friendly polymer (key characteristics & applications).+

Biodegradable polymers break down through biological activity under suitable environmental conditions.

Features

  • Reduce accumulation of persistent polymer waste.
  • Contain linkages that can undergo degradation.
  • Suitable grades can form temporary biomedical products.
  • Degradation depends on moisture, temperature and biological conditions.

Polylactic acid (PLA) is a biodegradable polyester made from lactic acid obtained by fermentation of carbohydrates.

Repeating unit

\[\mathrm{[{-}O{-}CH(CH_3){-}CO{-}]_n}\]

PLA contains ester linkages that can undergo hydrolysis during degradation. It is a thermoplastic with useful stiffness and transparency. Suitable composting conditions are important; it does not disappear rapidly in every environment. Biomedical grades are useful where gradual resorption is required.

Applications: packaging films, disposable cups, 3D-printing filament, surgical sutures, drug-delivery systems and tissue scaffolds.

Essay · 2What National Monument uses Teflon?+

Teflon in a monument

For the Statue of Liberty, PTFE was used as an electrically isolating/sliding barrier in the support-to-copper connection system to reduce adverse metal contact and allow movement. This does not mean that the whole statue or its entire steel structure is coated with PTFE.

5 conducting polymers (1).ppt

Essay · 1Conducting polymers are used in smart windows. Justify+

Conducting polymers: classification, doping and uses

Conducting polymers carry charge through a conjugated polymer backbone or through conducting constituents in a composite. Intrinsic examples include polyacetylene, polyaniline and polypyrrole. Alternating/conjugated bonds permit delocalisation; doping creates additional mobile charge carriers. Oxidative (p-type) doping removes electrons, for example iodine treatment; reductive (n-type) doping adds electrons, for example with sodium naphthalenide.

Extrinsically conducting materials include polymers filled with carbon/metal particles and blends with conducting polymers. A connected conducting path forms above a composition-dependent percolation threshold. Conductivity depends on doping, structure, morphology and temperature; conjugation alone does not guarantee metal-like conduction.

Redox/doping changes can reversibly change optical absorption/colour, enabling electrochromic displays and optical filters. Other applications include antistatic coatings, sensors, flexible electronics, battery electrodes, corrosion-protection coatings and photovoltaic devices.

Short answer · 1What is Nitinol? List out its features. [BL-2]+

Nitinol and the shape-memory effect

Nitinol is a near-equiatomic nickel–titanium alloy named from Nickel–Titanium–Naval Ordnance Laboratory. Its reversible solid-state transformation is between high-temperature austenite and low-temperature martensite; it is a phase/structure change, not melting. Austenite is commonly described by the ordered cubic B2 structure and martensite by a lower-symmetry, commonly monoclinic B19′ structure.

Features: shape recovery, superelasticity, useful corrosion resistance, high damping and high strength relative to useful device size. Applications include orthodontic wires, stents, guidewires, actuators, couplings and flexible spectacle frames. Biocompatibility depends on alloy surface/device design and patient factors, not an unconditional nickel-safety claim.

Short answer · 2Write the principle involved in amperometric glucose monitor. [BL-2]+

Glucose oxidase catalyses glucose oxidation, producing hydrogen peroxide. At a suitably biased electrode, H₂O₂ → O₂ + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻. The resulting current is related to glucose concentration by calibration, giving amperometric glucose measurement.

Short answer · 3What is the wavelength range for UV-Visible radiation? Give the principle involved in UV-Visible spectroscopy. [BL-2]+

Spectroscopy studies the interaction of electromagnetic radiation with matter. Beer–Lambert law states A = εlc: absorbance is proportional to concentration at fixed path length within the valid range. For dye analysis, measure standards and the sample at a suitable wavelength and use the calibration graph to determine concentration.

Short answer · 4What is Raman effect and write down two important applications of Raman spectroscopy. [BL-2]+

Raman effect and tumour investigation

The Raman effect is inelastic scattering of light: the molecule exchanges energy with the photon so the scattered frequency differs from the incident frequency. Rayleigh scattering has no frequency shift; Stokes photons lose energy and anti-Stokes photons gain energy. Raman-active vibrations change molecular polarisability. The Raman shift is commonly expressed in cm⁻¹.

A laser illuminates the sample; collection optics gather scattered light, filters suppress intense Rayleigh light, and a spectrograph/detector records the Raman spectrum. Bands provide molecular fingerprints, supporting material identification, reaction monitoring and biomedical studies.

Short answer · 5What are smart materials? Give examples. [BL-2]+

Smart materials

Smart materials respond to a stimulus such as temperature, stress, electric/magnetic field, light or chemical environment with a useful change in a property. Examples include Nitinol shape-memory alloy, shape-memory polymers, piezoelectric ceramics and photochromic materials. Their response can be designed for sensing, actuation or adaptation; not every smart material has all these responses.

Short answer · 6What is spectroscopy and state Beer-Lambert law. [BL-2]+

Spectroscopy studies the interaction of electromagnetic radiation with matter. Beer–Lambert law states A = εlc: absorbance is proportional to concentration at fixed path length within the valid range. For dye analysis, measure standards and the sample at a suitable wavelength and use the calibration graph to determine concentration.

Short answer · 7Outline the principle of Atomic Force Microscope/Scanning Tunnelling Microscope. [BL-2]+

Scanning tunnelling microscopy

STM maps suitable electrically conducting surfaces using quantum tunnelling. Bring a sharp conducting tip (commonly tungsten or platinum–iridium) within a very small gap of the conducting sample and apply bias. A tunnelling current flows without ordinary mechanical contact. Sharpness, conductivity and mechanical stability make W/Pt–Ir useful; the tip material alone does not guarantee atomic resolution.

Components: tip, bias/current-measurement circuit, x–y–z piezoelectric scanner, coarse approach, feedback controller/computer and vibration isolation. At comparable bias/electronic conditions the current decreases approximately exponentially with gap: I ∝ exp(−2κs), not directly in proportion to distance. Electronic density of states also affects the signal.

Short answer · 8Name the metal commonly used in making the tip of the STM and why is it preferred? [BL-3]+

Scanning tunnelling microscopy

STM maps suitable electrically conducting surfaces using quantum tunnelling. Bring a sharp conducting tip (commonly tungsten or platinum–iridium) within a very small gap of the conducting sample and apply bias. A tunnelling current flows without ordinary mechanical contact. Sharpness, conductivity and mechanical stability make W/Pt–Ir useful; the tip material alone does not guarantee atomic resolution.

Short answer · 9State the principle involved in IR spectroscopy. [BL-2]+

IR principle, night vision and CO monitoring

IR absorption occurs when radiation matches a molecular vibrational transition that changes the molecular dipole moment. The fundamental mid-IR region is commonly about 4000–400 cm⁻¹. CO has an IR-active stretching vibration (near 4.6–4.7 μm), allowing selective optical detection.

Short answer · 10What are self-healing materials? Outline the principle involved in them. [BL-2]+

Self-healing materials

Self-healing materials restore some lost integrity/function after damage through a built-in repair mechanism. Repair can be autonomic (damage triggers it) or externally activated (for example heat/light). It is not necessarily complete, unlimited or intervention-free.

Extrinsic capsule systems embed healing agent in microcapsules. A crack ruptures capsules, agent flows into the crack and reacts/polymerises, sometimes with an embedded catalyst. Vascular systems deliver agent through channels; replenishment can permit repeated repair. A depleted one-use capsule cannot heal indefinitely.

Short answer · 11List out the applications of self-healing materials. [BL-1]+

Self-healing materials

Applications: anticorrosion/scratch-resistant coatings, aircraft/vehicle composites, concrete systems, sealants, adhesives, pipelines and cables. Benefits include extended service life and lower maintenance; recovery depends on crack size, environment, agent supply and mechanical requirements. Recovery is not guaranteed for every damage condition.

Short answer · 12What are the requirements of a good biosensor? [BL-2]+

Biosensors and amperometric glucose monitoring

By transduction: electrochemical (amperometric current, potentiometric potential, conductometric conductivity), optical, thermal and piezoelectric/mass-sensitive. Useful features are selectivity, sensitivity, reproducibility, suitable response time/range, stable immobilisation and calibration, low interference and appropriate cost/size. Implantable designs also require appropriate biocompatibility.

Essay · 1What is Nitinol? Give a detailed note on nitinol as a shape memory alloy and mention its important applications. [BL-2]+

Nitinol and the shape-memory effect

Nitinol is a near-equiatomic nickel–titanium alloy named from Nickel–Titanium–Naval Ordnance Laboratory. Its reversible solid-state transformation is between high-temperature austenite and low-temperature martensite; it is a phase/structure change, not melting. Austenite is commonly described by the ordered cubic B2 structure and martensite by a lower-symmetry, commonly monoclinic B19′ structure.

Shape-memory cycle: establish the permanent shape in austenite; cool to twinned martensite; deform at low temperature by variant reorientation/detwinning; heat through the reverse-transformation range so austenite reforms and the original shape returns. Cooling alone does not necessarily produce the temporary deformed shape again in a one-way alloy.

Superelasticity is different: above the appropriate transformation temperature, applied stress induces martensite; removal of stress allows transformation back to austenite and large recoverable strain without a heating step. Transformation temperatures and recoverable strain depend on composition, processing and load.

Features: shape recovery, superelasticity, useful corrosion resistance, high damping and high strength relative to useful device size. Applications include orthodontic wires, stents, guidewires, actuators, couplings and flexible spectacle frames. Biocompatibility depends on alloy surface/device design and patient factors, not an unconditional nickel-safety claim.

Essay · 2What is UV-Visible spectroscopy? Give a detailed note on estimation of dye samples in water using UV-Visible spectroscopy with an emphasis on methylene blue indicator. [BL-2]+

Spectroscopy and Beer–Lambert law

Spectroscopy studies interactions of electromagnetic radiation with matter. In UV–visible absorption spectroscopy, suitable photons promote electrons to higher electronic energy levels. A chromophore is the atom/group responsible for characteristic absorption, for example a conjugated dye group. λmax is the wavelength at which an absorption band has its maximum absorbance under the stated conditions.

The conventional UV range is about 200–400 nm for routine solution instruments (the wider UV region extends below 200 nm); visible light is about 400–700 nm, sometimes extended to 800 nm by convention. State the convention rather than treating the boundary as exact.

Beer–Lambert law: A = log₁₀(I₀/I) = εlc, where A is dimensionless absorbance, ε is molar absorptivity (L mol⁻¹ cm⁻¹), l is optical path length (cm) and c is concentration (mol L⁻¹). It assumes suitable monochromatic light and a homogeneous, non-scattering solution within its linear concentration range. Chemical changes, stray light and turbidity can cause deviations.

Dye-effluent analysis by UV–visible spectroscopy

A coloured dye such as methylene blue absorbs at a characteristic visible wavelength through electronic transitions. Use a solvent/reagent blank, scan a representative standard and select λmax (methylene blue is commonly measured near 664 nm in dilute water, but determine the actual maximum for the sample conditions).

Prepare several known standards covering the expected range. Measure their absorbances with the same cuvette/path length, blank-correct them and plot A against concentration. Fit A = mc + b over the verified linear range. Filter/clarify a turbid sample when appropriate without removing the analyte; dilute if needed. Measure the sample under identical conditions.

Calculate c_diluted = (A_sample − b)/m, then c_original = dilution factor × c_diluted. Report units, dilution and calibration range. For treatment studies, removal % = [(c_initial − c_final)/c_initial]×100, comparing consistent volume/basis. No numeric concentration can be obtained without measured absorbance and calibration/ε.

This supports monitoring dye effluents, treatment efficiency and discharge trends. Mixed dyes, pH changes and suspended particles may interfere; a single dye absorbance is not a universal measure of total organic pollution or COD. Dye absorbance alone does not determine COD.

Essay · 3What is IR spectroscopy? Explain its importance in night vision and security. [BL-2]+

IR principle, night vision and CO monitoring

IR absorption occurs when radiation matches a molecular vibrational transition that changes the molecular dipole moment. The fundamental mid-IR region is commonly about 4000–400 cm⁻¹. CO has an IR-active stretching vibration (near 4.6–4.7 μm), allowing selective optical detection.

Active IR systems supply illumination and detect its reflection, transmission or absorption. Passive IR systems detect radiation emitted by objects without an illumination beam. Thermal night-vision cameras map emitted IR into an image; passive motion/security detectors sense changes in received thermal radiation. Active near-IR night-vision systems instead use an illuminator and reflected light.

A typical non-dispersive IR (NDIR) CO analyser is active: IR source → gas sample cell → wavelength-selective filter/detector, with a reference measurement and electronics. CO absorbs at its characteristic band, reducing transmitted intensity. Calibration relates absorbance/signal ratio to CO concentration; reference channels compensate source drift and non-specific effects.

In a PUC application, take a representative exhaust sample, manage particulates/condensation as required, zero/span-calibrate the analyser, measure the CO-specific signal, and report calibrated CO concentration (often volume %). Compare with the applicable vehicle/test limit; no limit can be invented without the regulation/category.

Correction: describing an analyser with its own IR source as “passive” is inconsistent. CO sensing by absorption is not the same as a passive thermal motion detector. Night vision is also an imaging/detection application of IR, not necessarily a molecular spectrometer.

Essay · 4Explain the significance of Infrared spectroscopy in Pollution Under Control? [BL-2]+

Essay · 5Explain how Raman spectroscopy is applied in tumor detection and biomedical diagnostics.[BL-2]+

Raman effect and tumour investigation

The Raman effect is inelastic scattering of light: the molecule exchanges energy with the photon so the scattered frequency differs from the incident frequency. Rayleigh scattering has no frequency shift; Stokes photons lose energy and anti-Stokes photons gain energy. Raman-active vibrations change molecular polarisability. The Raman shift is commonly expressed in cm⁻¹.

A laser illuminates the sample; collection optics gather scattered light, filters suppress intense Rayleigh light, and a spectrograph/detector records the Raman spectrum. Bands provide molecular fingerprints, supporting material identification, reaction monitoring and biomedical studies.

Healthy and tumour tissue can differ in proteins, lipids and nucleic acids. Acquire spectra from tissue/biopsy locations, subtract background/fluorescence carefully, normalise and compare with validated reference spectra. Statistical classification (for example PCA followed by a suitable validated classifier) can distinguish patterns and map candidate tumour boundaries.

Benefits include molecular specificity, potentially limited sample preparation and suitability for spatial mapping. Limitations include weak signals, fluorescence, tissue heterogeneity, laser heating and validation requirements. No single universal peak or protein/lipid ratio proves cancer. The example is supporting diagnostic evidence, not a replacement for validated clinical assessment/histopathology.

Essay · 6What is STM? Explain the working principle and applications of STM. [BL-2]+

Principle: STM works on quantum tunnelling of electrons between a sharp conducting tip and a conducting sample.

Working

  • Bring the tip very close to the sample and apply a small bias to produce a tunnelling current.
  • Move the tip across the surface using a piezoelectric scanner.
  • In constant-current mode, feedback adjusts the tip height to maintain constant current.
  • Record the height changes to obtain a surface image.

The main components are a sharp conducting tip, bias supply, piezoelectric scanner, current detector, feedback controller and computer. Tunnelling current changes very strongly with tip–sample separation, making small surface-height differences detectable. Constant-height mode instead records current changes while scanning at a fixed nominal tip height.

Uses: studying surface atoms, defects and nanostructures. The sample must provide a conducting path.

Essay · 7What is AFM? Explain the working principle and applications of AFM. [BL-2]+

Scanning tunnelling microscopy

STM maps suitable electrically conducting surfaces using quantum tunnelling. Bring a sharp conducting tip (commonly tungsten or platinum–iridium) within a very small gap of the conducting sample and apply bias. A tunnelling current flows without ordinary mechanical contact. Sharpness, conductivity and mechanical stability make W/Pt–Ir useful; the tip material alone does not guarantee atomic resolution.

Components: tip, bias/current-measurement circuit, x–y–z piezoelectric scanner, coarse approach, feedback controller/computer and vibration isolation. At comparable bias/electronic conditions the current decreases approximately exponentially with gap: I ∝ exp(−2κs), not directly in proportion to distance. Electronic density of states also affects the signal.

Constant-current mode: feedback adjusts z to hold a selected current while scanning x–y; record z(x,y). Constant-height mode: hold nominal tip height, record current variations; use sufficiently flat samples to avoid collisions. Fixed height is not a fixed local gap on an uneven surface.

Applications: atomic-scale surface features, defects, adsorbates, semiconductor surfaces, electronic-state studies and controlled atom manipulation. Limitations: conductive path required, sensitivity to drift/vibration/tip condition, small scan areas and specialised operation. STM does not require exactly 0 K and is not a literal optical photograph of atoms. Tunnelling current is not directly proportional to tip distance.

Atomic force microscopy

AFM measures tip–sample interactions using a sharp tip on a flexible cantilever. A piezo scanner moves the tip/sample across the surface; a laser reflected from the cantilever onto a position-sensitive detector senses bending/oscillation. Feedback maintains the chosen interaction setpoint and records surface height. In a calibrated small-deflection description, force F = kδ, where k is cantilever spring constant and δ its deflection.

Contact mode keeps continuous contact and can cause lateral-force damage. Non-contact mode monitors changes in an oscillating cantilever above the surface. Tapping mode makes intermittent contact, often reducing lateral damage to soft samples. Calibration and mode choice affect the interpretation.

Uses: surface topography/roughness, polymer/ceramic/biological imaging, adhesion, friction and mechanical-property mapping. AFM can investigate insulating as well as conducting samples and can operate in air, liquids or vacuum with suitable equipment. Limitations include tip-convolution effects, possible damage, drift, small scan area and slow scans.

Comparison: STM measures tunnelling current with a conducting tip/sample circuit, in constant-current or constant-height modes; AFM measures mechanical interactions with a cantilever in contact/non-contact/tapping modes. Both use fine scanning and feedback. Neither has universally better resolution/speed in every setting; compare a specified instrument, sample and mode. Scan limits depend on the instrument.

Essay · 9What are self-healing materials? Discuss the principle and applications of self-healing materials. [BL-2]+

Self-healing materials possess a built-in ability to repair damage and restore their performance.

Working principle

  • Triggering: damage activates the healing mechanism.
  • Transportation: healing material moves into the damaged region.
  • Chemical repair: polymerisation or bond re-formation closes the crack.

In a microcapsule system, a crack breaks embedded capsules. The healing agent flows into the crack and reacts to form a solid repair. In intrinsic systems, reversible bonds reconnect, often with heating.

Capsule-based and vascular systems are extrinsic: healing material is stored in capsules or channels within the matrix. In intrinsic systems, repair arises from reversible bonds or interactions in the polymer itself. Healing can be autonomic, triggered by damage, or activated externally by heat or another stimulus.

Examples: protective coatings, polymer composites, concrete and scratch-repair coatings.

Essay · 10What are biosensors? How are they classified? Discuss about the amperometric biosensor. [BL-2]+

An amperometric glucose biosensor measures the current produced by an enzyme-catalysed reaction.

Construction: glucose oxidase is immobilised near a working electrode. A reference electrode and current-measuring electronics complete the measuring system.

Enzyme reaction

\[\mathrm{Glucose+O_2\xrightarrow{glucose\ oxidase}Gluconolactone+H_2O_2}\]

Gluconolactone reacts with water to form gluconic acid.

Detecting-electrode reaction

\[\mathrm{H_2O_2\rightarrow O_2+2H^++2e^-}\]

The measured current is related to glucose concentration by calibration.

A suitable potential is maintained at the working electrode. Hydrogen peroxide formed by the enzyme reaction is oxidised there, releasing electrons. Over the calibrated working range, a greater glucose concentration produces a greater reaction-related current. The enzyme provides selectivity; the electrode converts the chemical change into an electrical signal.

Applications: blood-glucose meters, continuous glucose monitoring, and glucose analysis in food and fermentation processes.

Fit A = mc + b over the verified linear range. Filter/clarify a turbid sample when appropriate without removing the analyte; dilute if needed. Measure the sample under identical conditions.

Calculate c_diluted = (A_sample − b)/m, then c_original = dilution factor × c_diluted. Report units, dilution and calibration range. For treatment studies, removal % = [(c_initial − c_final)/c_initial]×100, comparing consistent volume/basis. No numeric concentration can be obtained without measured absorbance and calibration/ε.

This supports monitoring dye effluents, treatment efficiency and discharge trends. Mixed dyes, pH changes and suspended particles may interfere; a single dye absorbance is not a universal measure of total organic pollution or COD. Dye absorbance alone does not determine COD.

Essay · 3What is IR spectroscopy? Explain its importance in night vision and security. [BL-2]+

IR principle, night vision and CO monitoring

IR absorption occurs when radiation matches a molecular vibrational transition that changes the molecular dipole moment. The fundamental mid-IR region is commonly about 4000–400 cm⁻¹. CO has an IR-active stretching vibration (near 4.6–4.7 μm), allowing selective optical detection.

Active IR systems supply illumination and detect its reflection, transmission or absorption. Passive IR systems detect radiation emitted by objects without an illumination beam. Thermal night-vision cameras map emitted IR into an image; passive motion/security detectors sense changes in received thermal radiation. Active near-IR night-vision systems instead use an illuminator and reflected light.

A typical non-dispersive IR (NDIR) CO analyser is active: IR source → gas sample cell → wavelength-selective filter/detector, with a reference measurement and electronics. CO absorbs at its characteristic band, reducing transmitted intensity. Calibration relates absorbance/signal ratio to CO concentration; reference channels compensate source drift and non-specific effects.

In a PUC application, take a representative exhaust sample, manage particulates/condensation as required, zero/span-calibrate the analyser, measure the CO-specific signal, and report calibrated CO concentration (often volume %). Compare with the applicable vehicle/test limit; no limit can be invented without the regulation/category.

Correction: describing an analyser with its own IR source as “passive” is inconsistent. CO sensing by absorption is not the same as a passive thermal motion detector. Night vision is also an imaging/detection application of IR, not necessarily a molecular spectrometer.

Essay · 5Explain how Raman spectroscopy is applied in tumor detection and biomedical diagnostics.[BL-2]+

Raman effect and tumour investigation

The Raman effect is inelastic scattering of light: the molecule exchanges energy with the photon so the scattered frequency differs from the incident frequency. Rayleigh scattering has no frequency shift; Stokes photons lose energy and anti-Stokes photons gain energy. Raman-active vibrations change molecular polarisability. The Raman shift is commonly expressed in cm⁻¹.

A laser illuminates the sample; collection optics gather scattered light, filters suppress intense Rayleigh light, and a spectrograph/detector records the Raman spectrum. Bands provide molecular fingerprints, supporting material identification, reaction monitoring and biomedical studies.

Healthy and tumour tissue can differ in proteins, lipids and nucleic acids. Acquire spectra from tissue/biopsy locations, subtract background/fluorescence carefully, normalise and compare with validated reference spectra. Statistical classification (for example PCA followed by a suitable validated classifier) can distinguish patterns and map candidate tumour boundaries.

Benefits include molecular specificity, potentially limited sample preparation and suitability for spatial mapping. Limitations include weak signals, fluorescence, tissue heterogeneity, laser heating and validation requirements. No single universal peak or protein/lipid ratio proves cancer. The example is supporting diagnostic evidence, not a replacement for validated clinical assessment/histopathology.

Essay · 6What is STM? Explain the working principle and applications of STM. [BL-2]+

Principle: STM works on quantum tunnelling of electrons between a sharp conducting tip and a conducting sample.

Working

  • Bring the tip very close to the sample and apply a small bias to produce a tunnelling current.
  • Move the tip across the surface using a piezoelectric scanner.
  • In constant-current mode, feedback adjusts the tip height to maintain constant current.
  • Record the height changes to obtain a surface image.

The main components are a sharp conducting tip, bias supply, piezoelectric scanner, current detector, feedback controller and computer. Tunnelling current changes very strongly with tip–sample separation, making small surface-height differences detectable. Constant-height mode instead records current changes while scanning at a fixed nominal tip height.

Uses: studying surface atoms, defects and nanostructures. The sample must provide a conducting path.

Essay · 7What is AFM? Explain the working principle and applications of AFM. [BL-2]+

Scanning tunnelling microscopy

STM maps suitable electrically conducting surfaces using quantum tunnelling. Bring a sharp conducting tip (commonly tungsten or platinum–iridium) within a very small gap of the conducting sample and apply bias. A tunnelling current flows without ordinary mechanical contact. Sharpness, conductivity and mechanical stability make W/Pt–Ir useful; the tip material alone does not guarantee atomic resolution.

Components: tip, bias/current-measurement circuit, x–y–z piezoelectric scanner, coarse approach, feedback controller/computer and vibration isolation. At comparable bias/electronic conditions the current decreases approximately exponentially with gap: I ∝ exp(−2κs), not directly in proportion to distance. Electronic density of states also affects the signal.

Constant-current mode: feedback adjusts z to hold a selected current while scanning x–y; record z(x,y). Constant-height mode: hold nominal tip height, record current variations; use sufficiently flat samples to avoid collisions. Fixed height is not a fixed local gap on an uneven surface.

Applications: atomic-scale surface features, defects, adsorbates, semiconductor surfaces, electronic-state studies and controlled atom manipulation. Limitations: conductive path required, sensitivity to drift/vibration/tip condition, small scan areas and specialised operation. STM does not require exactly 0 K and is not a literal optical photograph of atoms. Tunnelling current is not directly proportional to tip distance.

Atomic force microscopy

AFM measures tip–sample interactions using a sharp tip on a flexible cantilever. A piezo scanner moves the tip/sample across the surface; a laser reflected from the cantilever onto a position-sensitive detector senses bending/oscillation. Feedback maintains the chosen interaction setpoint and records surface height. In a calibrated small-deflection description, force F = kδ, where k is cantilever spring constant and δ its deflection.

Contact mode keeps continuous contact and can cause lateral-force damage. Non-contact mode monitors changes in an oscillating cantilever above the surface. Tapping mode makes intermittent contact, often reducing lateral damage to soft samples. Calibration and mode choice affect the interpretation.

Uses: surface topography/roughness, polymer/ceramic/biological imaging, adhesion, friction and mechanical-property mapping. AFM can investigate insulating as well as conducting samples and can operate in air, liquids or vacuum with suitable equipment. Limitations include tip-convolution effects, possible damage, drift, small scan area and slow scans.

Comparison: STM measures tunnelling current with a conducting tip/sample circuit, in constant-current or constant-height modes; AFM measures mechanical interactions with a cantilever in contact/non-contact/tapping modes. Both use fine scanning and feedback. Neither has universally better resolution/speed in every setting; compare a specified instrument, sample and mode. Scan limits depend on the instrument.

Essay · 9What are self-healing materials? Discuss the principle and applications of self- healing materials. [BL-2]+

Self-healing materials possess a built-in ability to repair damage and restore their performance.

Working principle

  • Triggering: damage activates the healing mechanism.
  • Transportation: healing material moves into the damaged region.
  • Chemical repair: polymerisation or bond re-formation closes the crack.

In a microcapsule system, a crack breaks embedded capsules. The healing agent flows into the crack and reacts to form a solid repair. In intrinsic systems, reversible bonds reconnect, often with heating.

Capsule-based and vascular systems are extrinsic: healing material is stored in capsules or channels within the matrix. In intrinsic systems, repair arises from reversible bonds or interactions in the polymer itself. Healing can be autonomic, triggered by damage, or activated externally by heat or another stimulus.

Examples: protective coatings, polymer composites, concrete and scratch-repair coatings.

Essay · 10What are biosensors? How are they classified? Discuss about the amperometric biosensor. [BL-2]+

An amperometric glucose biosensor measures the current produced by an enzyme-catalysed reaction.

Construction: glucose oxidase is immobilised near a working electrode. A reference electrode and current-measuring electronics complete the measuring system.

Enzyme reaction

\[\mathrm{Glucose+O_2\xrightarrow{glucose\ oxidase}Gluconolactone+H_2O_2}\]

Gluconolactone reacts with water to form gluconic acid.

Detecting-electrode reaction

\[\mathrm{H_2O_2\rightarrow O_2+2H^++2e^-}\]

The measured current is related to glucose concentration by calibration.

A suitable potential is maintained at the working electrode. Hydrogen peroxide formed by the enzyme reaction is oxidised there, releasing electrons. Over the calibrated working range, a greater glucose concentration produces a greater reaction-related current. The enzyme provides selectivity; the electrode converts the chemical change into an electrical signal.

Applications: blood-glucose meters, continuous glucose monitoring, and glucose analysis in food and fermentation processes.

unit_5_Notes.pdf

Short answer · 1What are nanomaterials? Mention the methods of synthesizing them. [BL-1]+

Nanomaterials and synthesis

Nanomaterials have at least one relevant structural dimension approximately 1–100 nm. Their high surface-area/volume ratio and size-dependent structure can change optical, electrical, mechanical and catalytic properties compared with bulk material.

Top-down synthesis reduces bulk material: mechanical/ball milling, lithography or suitable etching/ablation. It can suit patterned manufacture but may introduce defects, contamination and size-distribution limits. Bottom-up synthesis builds from atoms/molecules: precipitation, sol–gel, chemical vapour deposition or self-assembly. It permits compositional/size control under suitable conditions but requires control of nucleation, growth and aggregation.

Short answer · 2List out the differences of top-down and bottom-up approach of nanomaterials? [BL-2]+
Top-downBottom-up
Breaks bulk material into nanosized structuresBuilds from atoms, molecules or clusters
Ball milling, etching, lithographySol–gel, precipitation, CVD
May introduce defects/contaminationRequires control of nucleation and growth

Short answer · 6What are self-healing materials? Outline the principle involved in them.[BL-2]+

Self-healing materials

Self-healing materials restore some lost integrity/function after damage through a built-in repair mechanism. Repair can be autonomic (damage triggers it) or externally activated (for example heat/light). It is not necessarily complete, unlimited or intervention-free.

Extrinsic capsule systems embed healing agent in microcapsules. A crack ruptures capsules, agent flows into the crack and reacts/polymerises, sometimes with an embedded catalyst. Vascular systems deliver agent through channels; replenishment can permit repeated repair. A depleted one-use capsule cannot heal indefinitely.

Short answer · 7List out the applications of self-healing materials.[BL-1]+

Self-healing materials

Applications: anticorrosion/scratch-resistant coatings, aircraft/vehicle composites, concrete systems, sealants, adhesives, pipelines and cables. Benefits include extended service life and lower maintenance; recovery depends on crack size, environment, agent supply and mechanical requirements. Recovery is not guaranteed for every damage condition.

Short answer · 8What are the requirements of a good biosensor. [BL-2]+

Biosensors and amperometric glucose monitoring

By transduction: electrochemical (amperometric current, potentiometric potential, conductometric conductivity), optical, thermal and piezoelectric/mass-sensitive. Useful features are selectivity, sensitivity, reproducibility, suitable response time/range, stable immobilisation and calibration, low interference and appropriate cost/size. Implantable designs also require appropriate biocompatibility.

Short answer · 9Mention the advantages of biochips. [BL-1]+

Biochips

Advantages include small reagent/sample volumes, parallel/high-throughput analysis, potential automation, compact size and rapid assays. Limitations include fabrication/calibration costs, cross-reactivity, sample preparation and the need to validate the particular assay. Applications: gene-expression/genetic testing, pathogen detection, drug screening, biomarker research and point-of-care testing.

Short answer · 11Outline the significance of the main components in biosensors. [BL-2]+

Biosensors and amperometric glucose monitoring

A biosensor combines a biological recognition element with a transducer and signal processing. Recognition elements include enzymes, antibodies, nucleic acids and cells. The recognition element selects/interacts with analyte; the transducer converts this change to a measurable signal; electronics amplify/process and display the calibrated result.

Short answer · 12Define a lubricant? List the functions of a lubricant. [BL-1]+

Lubricants: functions and classes

A lubricant is introduced between moving surfaces to reduce friction and wear. It can also remove heat, seal, protect against corrosion, carry debris and damp noise. Liquids include mineral oils, fatty oils and synthetic oils (for example silicones/esters); semisolids include greases; solids include graphite, molybdenum disulphide (MoS₂) and PTFE.

Short answer · 13Name two solid lubricants. Give the composition of the lubricant used in space vehicles? [BL- 2]+

Two solid lubricants are graphite and molybdenum disulphide (MoS₂). Suitable low-volatility MoS₂ coatings are used for some space/vacuum mechanisms. There is no single universal space-lubricant composition; selection depends on temperature, vacuum and the contacting materials.

Short answer · 14What is grease and mention its types. [BL-1]+

Lubricants: functions and classes

Grease is lubricating oil thickened by a soap or another thickener. Traditional soap types include calcium, sodium and lithium greases; their water/temperature properties differ. Consistency describes resistance to deformation/penetration and affects retention and flow. Dropping point is the specified test temperature at which a drop separates; it is not automatically a maximum safe operating temperature.

Short answer · 15What do you understand by consistency and drop point of grease? Explain their significance. [BL-2]+

Lubricants: functions and classes

Grease is lubricating oil thickened by a soap or another thickener. Traditional soap types include calcium, sodium and lithium greases; their water/temperature properties differ. Consistency describes resistance to deformation/penetration and affects retention and flow. Dropping point is the specified test temperature at which a drop separates; it is not automatically a maximum safe operating temperature.

Short answer · 16Synthetic lubricants have added advantage over natural lubricants. Explain. [BL-3]+

Lubricants: functions and classes

Synthetic lubricants can offer more controlled composition, oxidation/thermal stability and low-temperature behaviour than conventional mineral oils, but superiority depends on formulation and compatibility. Blended oils mix base oils and/or additives: antioxidants (for example hindered phenols), corrosion inhibitors, antiwear/EP additives, detergents/dispersants, viscosity-index improvers, pour-point depressants and antifoams. Antioxidants suppress oxidation-chain reactions and sludge/acid formation.

Short answer · 17Antioxidants are added to hydrocarbon oils. Justify with an example. [BL-3]+

Short answer · 19What is lubrication? What type of lubrication is involved in delicate machines like watches, clocks, sewing machines and transformers? [BL-3]+

Lubrication introduces a material between moving surfaces to reduce friction and wear. Fluid-film lubrication is the course classification for suitably lubricated delicate instruments such as watches, clocks and sewing machines. The actual regime depends on speed, load and clearance. Transformer oil mainly provides insulation and cooling.

Short answer · 20Mention the lubricant & mechanism employed in machines operating under heavy load and low speeds? [BL-3]+

Lubrication mechanisms and operating conditions

Boundary/thin-film lubrication: at low speed, high load or startup, a full hydrodynamic film may not form. Polar molecules/antiwear additives adsorb or react on surfaces to provide a protective thin layer. Fatty oils/blends or formulated oils can provide boundary lubricity. Heavy loads and slow speeds commonly require this mechanism; delicate low-load instruments may use low-viscosity oils, with the actual regime determined by speed/load/clearance.

Short answer · 21What type of oils are suitable for thick-film and thin film lubrication?[BL-2]+

Thick-film lubrication uses an oil with suitable viscosity to maintain a continuous separating film, such as a properly selected mineral or synthetic oil. Thin-film lubrication needs high oiliness and adhesion, provided by fatty oils or suitable boundary/antiwear additives in formulated oils.

Short answer · 22Mention the mechanism of lubrication and operating conditions in hypoid gears used in rear axle drive of cars and tractors. [BL-3]+

Lubrication mechanisms and operating conditions

Extreme-pressure (EP) lubrication: severe loads/sliding heat can break down ordinary boundary films. Sulphur-, phosphorus- or other suitable additives react at hot contact points to form protective low-shear films that reduce welding/seizure. Hypoid gears and severe metal-forming/wire-drawing contacts may require EP formulations.

Short answer · 23What type of lubricants are used in transformers and refrigeration system? Why? [BL-3]+

Lubricant properties and oil selection

Transformer oils require high dielectric strength, oxidation stability, suitable low-temperature flow and low moisture/impurities. Refrigeration compressor oils must be compatible with the refrigerant and compressor, with suitable viscosity, low-temperature properties and chemical stability; the suitable mineral/synthetic oil depends on the refrigerant. One oil cannot be specified for every refrigeration system.

Short answer · 24Oils having high viscosity need not necessarily have high viscosity index. Comment. [BL-3]+

Lubricant properties and oil selection

Viscosity measures resistance to flow at a specified temperature. Viscosity index describes how strongly viscosity changes with temperature: high VI means relatively less change. A very viscous oil can still have a low VI, so viscosity and VI are not interchangeable.

Short answer · 25What is extreme pressure lubrication? Which metals are resistant to it and why? [BL-2]+

Lubrication mechanisms and operating conditions

Extreme-pressure (EP) lubrication: severe loads/sliding heat can break down ordinary boundary films. Sulphur-, phosphorus- or other suitable additives react at hot contact points to form protective low-shear films that reduce welding/seizure. Hypoid gears and severe metal-forming/wire-drawing contacts may require EP formulations.

Note: “transformer lubrication” is imprecise—transformer oil primarily insulates electrically and removes heat, not lubricates a moving contact. Gold/platinum and other very inert surfaces may not form the intended reactive EP layer; some sulphur additives instead corrode copper-containing metals. Thus “metals resistant to EP lubrication” has no universal list independent of additive chemistry and temperature.

Short answer · 26Compare Redwood viscometer-1 and 2. [BL-2]+

Lubricant properties and oil selection

Redwood viscometers measure the time for a fixed volume (traditionally 50 mL) to flow through a calibrated orifice at a controlled temperature. No. 1 has a smaller orifice for lower/medium-viscosity oils; No. 2 has a larger orifice for more viscous oils so the outflow time remains practical. Report temperature and instrument number with the time; Redwood seconds are not themselves SI dynamic-viscosity units.

Short answer · 27Outline the significance of cloud and pour point. [BL-1]+

Lubricant properties and oil selection

Cloud point is the specified cooling-test temperature at which wax/cloudiness first appears; pour point is the lowest specified-test temperature at which flow is observed. Low values matter for cold-weather operation and pumpability. Flash point is the lowest specified-test temperature at which vapour momentarily ignites; fire point is the temperature at which combustion is sustained under the test conditions. Adequately high flash/fire points reduce fire risk at operating temperature, but do not alone prove lubrication quality or safety.

Short answer · 28A good lubricant should possess high flash and fire points. Give reason. [BL-2]+

Lubricant properties and oil selection

Cloud point is the specified cooling-test temperature at which wax/cloudiness first appears; pour point is the lowest specified-test temperature at which flow is observed. Low values matter for cold-weather operation and pumpability. Flash point is the lowest specified-test temperature at which vapour momentarily ignites; fire point is the temperature at which combustion is sustained under the test conditions. Adequately high flash/fire points reduce fire risk at operating temperature, but do not alone prove lubrication quality or safety.

Essay · 1Define nanomaterials? Discuss the methods of synthesis of nanomaterials [BL-1]+

Nanomaterials are synthesised by two main approaches.

Top-downBottom-up
PrincipleBreak bulk material into nanosized structuresBuild structures from atoms, molecules or clusters
MethodsBall milling, grinding, etching and lithographySol–gel, chemical vapour deposition and precipitation
AdvantagesSuitable for large-scale productionBetter control of size/composition; narrow size distribution possible
LimitationsBroad size distribution, surface defects and contaminationCareful control of reaction conditions and purification may be needed

Ball milling: repeated impacts between the material and hard milling balls reduce particle size.

Sol–gel: hydrolysis and condensation of a precursor form a sol and then a gel. Drying and suitable heat treatment produce the required material.

Top-down methods reduce size by mechanical action or pattern removal, while bottom-up methods control nucleation and growth from smaller species. In ball milling, contamination and size distribution need control. In sol–gel processing, precursor concentration, reaction conditions and heat treatment affect particle formation and aggregation.

Essay · 5Explain in detail the following mechanisms of lubrication [BL-2]
a) Fluid-film/Hydrodynamic lubrication/Thick film lubrication
b) Boundary lubrication/thin film lubrication
c) Extreme pressure lubrication+

(a) Fluid-film / hydrodynamic lubrication

Hydrodynamic/thick-film lubrication: a continuous viscous oil film separates surfaces; motion and geometry develop load-carrying pressure. Low friction results mainly from shearing fluid, not metal contact. It suits adequate speed and appropriate load/viscosity; a suitable mineral or synthetic oil can be used.

(b) Boundary / thin-film lubrication

Boundary/thin-film lubrication: at low speed, high load or startup, a full hydrodynamic film may not form. Polar molecules/antiwear additives adsorb or react on surfaces to provide a protective thin layer. Fatty oils/blends or formulated oils can provide boundary lubricity. Heavy loads and slow speeds commonly require this mechanism; delicate low-load instruments may use low-viscosity oils, with the actual regime determined by speed/load/clearance.

(c) Extreme-pressure lubrication

Extreme-pressure (EP) lubrication: severe loads/sliding heat can break down ordinary boundary films. Sulphur-, phosphorus- or other suitable additives react at hot contact points to form protective low-shear films that reduce welding/seizure. Hypoid gears and severe metal-forming/wire-drawing contacts may require EP formulations.

Essay · 6Define lubricant. Discuss the various types of lubricants with suitable examples. [BL-1]+

Lubricants: functions and classes

A lubricant is introduced between moving surfaces to reduce friction and wear. It can also remove heat, seal, protect against corrosion, carry debris and damp noise. Liquids include mineral oils, fatty oils and synthetic oils (for example silicones/esters); semisolids include greases; solids include graphite, molybdenum disulphide (MoS₂) and PTFE.

Grease is lubricating oil thickened by a soap or another thickener. Traditional soap types include calcium, sodium and lithium greases; their water/temperature properties differ. Consistency describes resistance to deformation/penetration and affects retention and flow. Dropping point is the specified test temperature at which a drop separates; it is not automatically a maximum safe operating temperature.

Synthetic lubricants can offer more controlled composition, oxidation/thermal stability and low-temperature behaviour than conventional mineral oils, but superiority depends on formulation and compatibility. Blended oils mix base oils and/or additives: antioxidants (for example hindered phenols), corrosion inhibitors, antiwear/EP additives, detergents/dispersants, viscosity-index improvers, pour-point depressants and antifoams. Antioxidants suppress oxidation-chain reactions and sludge/acid formation.

Space/vacuum applications often use suitable low-volatility solid lubricants such as MoS₂ (or specialised fluids selected for the environment). Graphite depends strongly on environment and is not a universal vacuum substitute. There is no single fixed “space lubricant composition” without a specified device; MoS₂ is one example rather than a universal formulation.

Essay · 7Describe the mechanism observed in delicate instruments like watches & sewing machines and wire drawing equipment’s. [BL-3]+

Lubrication mechanisms and operating conditions

Hydrodynamic/thick-film lubrication: a continuous viscous oil film separates surfaces; motion and geometry develop load-carrying pressure. Low friction results mainly from shearing fluid, not metal contact. It suits adequate speed and appropriate load/viscosity; a suitable mineral or synthetic oil can be used. Examples include watches, clocks and sewing machines, though a real instrument’s lubrication regime depends on its load, speed and geometry.

Boundary/thin-film lubrication: at low speed, high load or startup, a full hydrodynamic film may not form. Polar molecules/antiwear additives adsorb or react on surfaces to provide a protective thin layer. Fatty oils/blends or formulated oils can provide boundary lubricity. Heavy loads and slow speeds commonly require this mechanism; delicate low-load instruments may use low-viscosity oils, with the actual regime determined by speed/load/clearance.

Extreme-pressure (EP) lubrication: severe loads/sliding heat can break down ordinary boundary films. Sulphur-, phosphorus- or other suitable additives react at hot contact points to form protective low-shear films that reduce welding/seizure. Hypoid gears and severe metal-forming/wire-drawing contacts may require EP formulations.

Note: “transformer lubrication” is imprecise—transformer oil primarily insulates electrically and removes heat, not lubricates a moving contact. Gold/platinum and other very inert surfaces may not form the intended reactive EP layer; some sulphur additives instead corrode copper-containing metals. Thus “metals resistant to EP lubrication” has no universal list independent of additive chemistry and temperature.

Essay · 8Explain the mechanism of thin film lubrication with diagram. [BL-2]+

Essay · 9Explain the following properties of lubricants and give their significance? [BL-2]
a) Cloud point
b) Pour point
c) Flash point
d) Fire point.+

(a) Cloud point

The specified cooling-test temperature where wax/cloudiness first appears; it helps judge cold-weather suitability.

(b) Pour point

The lowest specified-test temperature at which oil still flows. Select a suitably low pour point for the coldest expected service.

(c) Flash point

The lowest specified-test temperature where vapour flashes momentarily on applying an ignition source; it informs fire-risk assessment.

(d) Fire point

The lowest specified-test temperature for sustained burning under the stated test (traditionally at least five seconds). A suitably high value is desirable, but it is not the maximum operating temperature by itself.

Essay · 10What are biochips? Mention their advantages and applications. [BL-1]+

Biochips

A biochip is a miniaturised platform that performs biological/biochemical measurements, often with many tests in parallel. Examples include DNA microarrays, protein arrays and microfluidic lab-on-chip devices. A biochip is not necessarily an implanted identity device.

Advantages include small reagent/sample volumes, parallel/high-throughput analysis, potential automation, compact size and rapid assays. Limitations include fabrication/calibration costs, cross-reactivity, sample preparation and the need to validate the particular assay. Applications: gene-expression/genetic testing, pathogen detection, drug screening, biomarker research and point-of-care testing.

Essay · 11
a) What are biosensors? How are they classified? [BL-2]
b) List out the advantages of biosensors? [BL-2]+

A biosensor combines a biological recognition element with a transducer to detect an analyte.

Construction

  • Bioreceptor: enzyme, antibody, cell or nucleic acid that recognises the analyte.
  • Transducer: converts the biological response into a measurable signal.
  • Signal processor: amplifies and processes the signal.
  • Display: shows the calibrated result.

Working: the analyte interacts with the bioreceptor, producing a change in current, potential, heat, light or mass. The transducer detects this change; the processed signal gives analyte concentration.

The biological element is immobilised on a support close to the transducer so that it remains active and its response can be detected efficiently. A known-concentration standard is used for calibration. Selectivity, sensitivity, rapid response and reproducibility are important characteristics.

Example: glucose oxidase in a glucose biosensor.

The biological element is immobilised on a support close to the transducer so that it remains active and its response can be detected efficiently. A known-concentration standard is used for calibration. Selectivity, sensitivity, rapid response and reproducibility are important characteristics.

Example: glucose oxidase in a glucose biosensor.
